FOREWORD

**The Moai’s Gaze**

A**S LUCK WOULD HAVE IT**, I grew up across the street from Howard La Fay, a seasoned *National Geographic* editor. Some of my earliest memories are of his home, which brimmed with mementos of his remarkable journeys. My personal favorite: a hand-carved replica of one of Easter Island’s famed and mysterious statues, called moai. Howard had spent weeks on the remote island, farthest outpost of the Polynesian voyagers, researching an article for National Geographic’s flagship magazine.

The moai’s inscrutable gaze haunted my childhood. I figured everyone went to Easter Island. One day, no doubt, I would, too. Then I grew up and learned few venture so far. And so the volcanic outpost became, for me, the very end of the Earth. It didn’t matter that I might never visit: I could, and did, travel there repeatedly in my dreams, and in the vivid stories and images of the Geographic.

This book is a celebration of the places that beckon, transfix, and delight people everywhere. Each day, in 10,000 imaginations, the fire of Mount Vesuvius engulfs Pompeii and Herculaneum once more. The towers of Angkor Wat reflect the glow of dawn, as do the magnificent ruins of the Athenian Acropolis, the Roman Colosseum, Mesa Verde’s cliff dwellings, and Machu Picchu. The ancient sundial at Jaipur, the soaring peaks of Patagonia, the rock arches of Stonehenge, the pyramids of Egypt and Mesoamerica, the incomparable monolith that is Uluru—all trace the hours across the landscape with their shadows. Meanwhile on the Serengeti, the Great Barrier Reef, and the Yellowstone Plateau, the eternal dance of predator and prey plays on.

I never suspected that I would have the privilege, like Howard, of a career with National Geographic, that I would visit so many of these miraculous places, much less share them with travelers on our expeditions. Nor did I anticipate the email from headquarters six years ago that read: “Pack your bags: You’re going to Easter Island.” And so I came full circle and returned the moai’s gaze.

The poet T. S. Eliot understood where these timeless journeys take us. “We shall not cease from exploration,” he wrote. “And the end of all our exploring / Will be to arrive where we started / And know the place for the first time.” In these expeditions to the wonders of our world—supreme achievements of nature and the human spirit—you’ll find endless inspiration, riches to fill your imagination, and, just maybe, insights into that destination you call home.

Don’t take my word for it. Just turn the page.

— FORD COCHRAN,

Director of Programming, National Geographic Expeditions

TURKEY

**Cappadocia**

Cities and sanctuaries honeycombed into rock

S**INCE THE FOURTH CENTURY** A.D., humans have inhabited a landscape of bizarre pinnacles, hoodoos, and natural pillars in the Cappadocia region of central Turkey. Ancient architects excavated the interior of natural rock formations to fashion domed sanctuaries and entire underground cities.

Nature laid the foundation for these remarkable troglodyte dwellings millions of years ago when volcanoes rained ash on the high plateau of Cappadocia. The fine grains solidified into a soft rock called tuff; wind and rain gradually abraded the softer stone, leaving the harder parts intact and forming a tortuous landscape of freestanding columns and badlands.

Thousands of years ago, the human terrain was as volatile as the prehistoric volcanoes. Early Christians seeking refuge from Roman persecution burrowed into the rock to create the first monasteries. Later, it was Arab raiding parties they sought to elude, and they tunneled even deeper, creating underground towns that concealed them from invaders.

Perhaps the most evocative remaining structures are the Christian cave chapels in the open-air museum in the Göreme Valley. These include the tiny Elmalı Kilise (“apple church”) and Yılanlı Kilise (“snake church”). Built between the 10th and 13th centuries, these ornate underground sanctuaries were the anchors of an active troglodyte society. Karanlık Kilise—“dark church”—is considered one of the best remaining examples of 12th-century Byzantine art. Preserved in shadow (the chapel’s tiny narthex window admits little natural light) and beautifully restored, the church’s vaulted ceilings are frescoed with golden-haloed saints and scenes from the life of Christ.

Fifty miles (80 km) southwest of Göreme, the deep Ihlara Valley shelters about 100 additional rock-carved churches and monasteries, whose interior details have faded or been lost to time. Only a handful have been dated definitively, but a few—like the barrel-vaulted Kokar Kilise and the Pürenliseki Kilise—have geometric patterning, blocks of text, and chipping frescoes that evoke pre-Byzantine Syrian Christian and Egyptian Coptic churches.

Cool in summer and warm and dry in winter, the stone dwellings in Cappadocia were as functional as they were covert. This is especially evident 15 miles (24 km) directly south of Göreme at the underground city of Kaymaklı. Here, an entire community sequestered itself in an eight-story subterranean complex behind a one-ton (907 kg) circular rock door. Four levels are still navigable today and include dining rooms, bedrooms, and wine cellars. But the city’s deepest, most protected level hides what was most sacred to the inhabitants—a simple cruciform church.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_009_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_009_r1.jpg)

Early Christians carved shelters and chapels out of the soft tuff stone of the Cappadocia region. [Credit 9](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img9a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[nationalparksofturkey.com/​goreme-national-park-and-the-rock-sites-of-cappadocia](http://nationalparksofturkey.com/goreme-national-park-and-the-rock-sites-of-cappadocia)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Summer—Cappadocia’s high season—is hot and the perfect time to book a cool cave hotel in Göreme. Try Cappadocia Cave Suites (*[cappadociacavesuites.com](http://cappadociacavesuites.com)*) with 36 rooms fashioned from original Hittite, Roman, and Byzantine cave dwellings. Land-based tours of the region are offered in town; rent a bike or car; or book a hot-air balloon that rises above the fairy chimneys at dawn.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Buses and planes depart daily from Istanbul to the western Cappadocia town of Nevşehir, as well as eastern Kayseri. Many visitors opt to stay in Göreme or the surrounding towns of Ürgüp and Avanos. National Geographic also offers trips to Turkey (*[natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)*).**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_010_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_010_r1.jpg)

The *Geographic* team’s car was pushed around narrow curves on ancient roads in Cappadocia to keep it from swiping rock walls. [Credit 10](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img10a)

Modern Wheels on Ancient Roads

O**n the eve of World War II, *National Geographic* sent writer John D. Whiting and photographer Eric Matson to Cappadocia to capture color photographs of the region. The pair made the 3,110-mile (5,005 km) round-trip journey from Jerusalem by car with a mechanic and chauffeur named Friday.**

**“No company would insure the car, not even Lloyd’s of London,” Whiting wrote, and no wonder. The car had to be rocked around tight corners as it navigated roads built millennia ago. At one point, a passing caravan of 40 men and women had to help lift the rear axle off a rock. Another time, the car became wedged in oxcart ruts. And the tires were punctured frequently by the heavy nails used to shoe donkeys that traveled the roads.**

**The *Geographic* team made its destination, though, and Whiting wrote with awe of rocks honeycombed with monasteries and hermit cells. “Rope ladders and toe holes in the cone faces were often the only stairways,” he marveled.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_011_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_011_r1.jpg)

Lanterns light up the famous Treasury in Petra. [Credit 11](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img11a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_012_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_012_r1.jpg)

A well-preserved floor mosaic at Petra (aka Byzantine) Church. [Credit 12](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img12a)

JORDAN

**Petra**

Rock-cut architecture in the Arabian desert

F**OUNDED SOME 2,000 YEARS** ago by the Nabataeans, the red-rock city of Petra in southern Jordan reached a dazzling peak during the Roman Empire. It was an important center of the spice trade under the Nabataeans, but became a hub of Roman political influence, military and commercial might, and cultural reach in the Arabian Peninsula in the second century A.D. Changing trade routes and earthquakes led to Petra’s eventual decline, and by the seventh century, the city was largely abandoned.

Chiseled into a warren of desert canyons between the Red Sea and the Dead Sea, Petra started life as a caravan city on the busy overland routes between Arabia, Egypt, Asia Minor, and Mesopotamia. Its eclectic architecture reflects this crossroads quality, a blend of Hellenistic and Middle Eastern influences.

The Nabataeans transformed their harsh environment into a boon rather than a bane. Tucked into rugged desert mountains and reachable only via narrow gorges like the Siq (a sandstone shaft canyon), the site was easily defendable. Their greatest enemy was the arid climate, but the inhabitants created an ingenious irrigation system that diverted water into Petra through channels and tunnels carved into the stone.

Little remains of their domestic or commercial architecture, but the tombs of Nabataean rulers and nobles have stood the test of time; massive stone-cut structures with elaborate facades and deep, temple-like interiors.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *Weather has stained the rock with exquisite colours…It is like a fairy tale city, all pink and wonderful.*
~ GERTRUDE BELL, BRITISH WRITER, ARCHAEOLOGIST, AND SPY

Although the incomparable Treasury (Al Khazneh) is easily the city’s most photographed structure, there are scores of other Nabataean tombs carved into the canyon walls. Some accounts say that rather than leave them to the dead, the Romans converted many of the rock-hewn tombs into aristocratic mansions.

Outgrowing its red-rock canyons, Petra spilled out onto the adjacent wadi during the first century A.D., just before the Roman conquest. Roman influence was already rife by then, reflected in structures like the amphitheater and Great Temple. The immense Hadrian Gate and elegant Cardo Maximus attest to the long and prosperous Roman era.

Archaeologists are still combing Petra for undiscovered artifacts and buildings. One of their most recent finds was a monumental rectangular structure—discovered via drones and satellite imagery—hidden beneath the sand and stones of the wadi about a half mile (800 m) south of the Great Temple.

The terrain itself is a swirl of red, pink, and orange stone reminiscent of the slot canyons of southern Utah. It can be explored by horseback, carriage, camel rides, or day-long treks to the rock-cut monuments of the Monastery or High Place of Sacrifice.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_013_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_013_r1.jpg)

One of Petra’s many rock-hewn tombs, carved into sandstone that’s whorled in color [Credit 13](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img13a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[visitpetra.jo](http://visitpetra.jo)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Perched at the top of the Siq, the town of Wadi Musa offers dozens of overnight options. Adjacent to the archaeological park entrance, the Mövenpick *([movenpick.com](http://movenpick.com))*, Crowne Plaza *([ihg.com](http://ihg.com))*, and Petra Moon *([petramoonhotel.com](http://petramoonhotel.com))* are among the few hotels within easy walking distance of the ruins.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Petra can be visited as a very long day trip from Aqaba or an overnight jaunt from Amman. But at least two days are advisable to explore the city properly. Travel agencies in both cities offer a variety of Petra options. National Geographic also offers trips to Jordan *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_014_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_014_r1.jpg)

*National Geographic*’s author followed this narrow gorge for more than a mile before the Treasury appeared ahead. [Credit 14](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img14a)

Snaking Through the Siq

*N****ational Geographic* writer John D. Whiting wrote an evocative account of Petra in 1935\. The city was at last becoming accessible to tourists by railroad, highway, and even airplane (National Geographic Society president Gilbert Grosvenor was on the first flight that took travelers to Petra, landing at Ma’an in Jordan).**

**All visitors, however, still had to make their way through the bewitchingly narrow and winding Siq, led by Bedouins on horseback. “In no place may we see far ahead, crooks and corners preventing,” Whiting wrote. “A streak of blue sky like a twisted ribbon is all that is visible from the heavens. At every turn we hear in fancy the tinkling of bells and dream of a long caravan winding its way through, strong with the scent of perfumes, frankincense, and myrrh.”**

**At last, Al Khazneh (the Treasury) burst upon the party “unreal as the figment of a dream,” Whiting wrote. The color of the breathtaking monument, he said, ranged from brilliant red in the sun to deep mahogany in shadow.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_015_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_015_r1.jpg)

Blue is the predominant color in the intricate mosaics of Sheikh Lotfollah Mosque in Isfahan. [Credit 15](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img15a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_016_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_016_r1.jpg)

The vast Imam Mosque seen from Isfahan’s central square. [Credit 16](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img16a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

IRAN

**Isfahan**

Exquisite Islamic monuments in the former Persian capital

I**SFAHAN, A FORMER CAPITAL** of the Persian Empire, rises in a spectacle of gleaming, arabesque-adorned domes. The city flourished during the 16th and 17th centuries during the Iranian dynasty Safavid, and was among the largest and most prominent cities in Central Asia. It sits 278 miles (447 km) from the modern Iranian capital of Tehran.

Isfahan’s central square, constructed in 1602 during the reign of Shah ‘Abbas I, is an opulent oasis surrounded by some of Earth’s most beautiful and important Islamic architecture. Anchored by religious buildings, it is often likened to a Middle Eastern version of San Marco in Venice. Persian poets once said of the square, *“Isfahan nesf-e jahan*—Isfahan is half the world.” And in fact, it remains one of the largest squares in the world, its centuries-old history spanning both the rise and the demise of Persia. Still visible are the polo goalposts of Reza Shah Pahlavi, the shah deposed in the Iranian Revolution of 1979, the same year the square was declared a UNESCO World Heritage site. The square’s modern name, Meidan Emam, replacing Meidan-e Shah, reflects the revolutionary change from rule by royalty to rule by religion.

The major monuments in the square are connected by elegant, two-story arcades. Imam Mosque, formerly Shah Mosque, has Isfahan’s most imposing dome, intricately tiled on the inside and decorated with Arabic calligraphy and motifs on the outside. Sheikh Lotfollah Mosque dazzles visitors with splendid tilework of cobalt blue, turquoise, and lemon yellow, its abstract patterns transforming in hue through the day. The opulent, six-story Ali Qapu Palace is known for its winding mosaic stairs, its magnificent views of the square, and its music room where poets and musicians once entertained the shahs. Nearby, the Grand Bazaar offers a way of shopping that’s been unchanged for centuries. The bazaar’s floors are covered with handmade carpets for sale, and vendors offer passersby cups of tea and a chance to bargain.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_017_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_017_r1.jpg)

The vaulted Grand Bazaar has been home to shoppers, hagglers, and sellers since the 17th century. [Credit 17](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img17a)

Isfahan’s exquisite treasures appear unquantifiable, though along the tree-lined avenues and gardens beyond the square, a French chronicler once counted 273 public baths, 162 mosques, and 1,802 caravansaries. These latter were rest stops with inner courtyards where travelers and traders fed their camels and stayed the night, spinning tales of their journeys over faraway lands. Some caravansaries, like the Abbasi, are now hotels, resplendent with gilding and tiles. This Iranian city also serves as a gateway to the country’s best kept secret—winter skiing in the resorts of the Zagros Mountains that command the region.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *Isfahan is without question one of the great cultural treasures of the world, the jewel of one of history’s most elegant and cultured societies.*
~ PICO IYER, *A PLACE I’VE NEVER BEEN*

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *irantraveler.net, [isfahan.ir](http://isfahan.ir), [irandoostan.com](http://irandoostan.com)***

**• HOW TO STAY: The four-star Abbasi Hotel *([abbasihotel.ir](http://abbasihotel.ir))*, built inside a former caravansary by Shah Sultan Husayn, is close to the main sites of the city, as is the modern Sheykh Bahaei Hotel *([en.sheykhbahaeihotel.com](http://en.sheykhbahaeihotel.com)).***

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Several companies offer Iran tours that include Isfahan along with Tehran and other areas. Iran Doostan Tours includes Isfahan excursions as part of its cultural tours *([irandoostan.com](http://irandoostan.com));* **so does** Iran Traveler *(irantraveler.net).* Travelers should check their country’s foreign ministry or, for U.S. citizens, the Department of State, for warnings pertaining to travel in Iran.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_018_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_018_r1.jpg)

Rug cleaners beat carpets in front of the Allahverdi Khan Bridge in Isfahan before washing them in the Zayandeh River. [Credit 18](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img18a)

A Tour of the Middle East

I**n 1957, U.S. Supreme Court Justice William O. Douglas drove 4,000 miles (6,435 km) across the northern edges of Pakistan, Afghanistan, Iran, Iraq, and Turkey, and wrote of it for *National Geographic.* His wife, Mercedes, was chief mechanic, armed with a car repair manual and spare parts for their 1956 station wagon. Rough roads punctured the gas tank, weak Russian gas destroyed the carburetor, and the muffler fell off. But the road trip offered an unforgettable glimpse into the remote reaches of these vast countries.**

**In Tehran, the Douglases visited with the shah at his Shemiran summer palace, and in Isfahan, they marveled at the garden city’s beauty and tranquility. The bazaar was their favorite spot, though after watching young girls at the looms of the rug factory, Douglas wrote, “Their fingers moved as fast as spindles, and their faces were pressed to the loom as they tied the tiny knots with lightening speed…a girl who starts at 5 years of age is often blind when she reaches 21.”**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_019_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_019_r1.jpg)

A geometric archway at the Jaipur Observatory [Credit 19](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img19a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

INDIA

**Jaipur**

A pink city shimmering on the edge of the Rajasthan desert

J**AIPUR, DUBBED THE “PINK CITY”** because of its rose-colored homes and palaces, is the metropolis of the Indian state of Rajasthan. It flourished during the 18th and 19th centuries under a series of maharajas who avidly championed the arts, architecture, and science.

Landmark structures like the Hawa Mahal (“palace of winds”), City Palace, and Nahargarh Fort are a marvelous fusion of traditional Hindu Rajput, Islamic Mughal, and British colonial design. Expressions of Rajasthan’s diverse heritage, they offer an eye-catching, over-the-top contrast to the sleek Taj Mahal and other purely Mughal buildings.

India’s first planned city, Jaipur was founded in 1727 when Maharaja Jai Singh II moved his royal court from the hilltop Amber Fort to a flatter space (with better water resources) that could expedite his plan to establish a larger and much more splendid capital. A lifelong devotee of architecture, Jai Singh laid out the city with Brahman scholar Vidyadhar Bhattacharya, who consulted ancient texts by Euclid and Ptolemy as well as Hindu writings and astronomical charts. The nine giant blocks and wide avenues of Jai Singh’s capital continue to form the heart of modern Jaipur.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_020_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_020_r1.jpg)

The women’s quarters of the City Palace [Credit 20](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img20a)

In addition to palaces, Jai Singh made sure his capital was outfitted with a royal observatory called the Jantar Mantar. Really more an astronomical garden, the complex features more than a dozen outdoor structures used for observing and measuring the heavens, including the world’s largest sundial. Even more wondrous is the five-story Hawa Mahal, an impressive hive of 953 elaborately decorated *jharokhas* (enclosed stone balconies) used by the women of the royal court to surreptitiously observe goings-on in the streets below.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_021_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_021_r1.jpg)

A honeycomb of lattice-screened windows at the Hawa Mahal allowed royal women to view the street without being seen. [Credit 21](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img21a)

Jaipur’s sobriquet “Pink City” dates from the 1870s, when Maharaja Ram Singh mandated that all structures in the city center be painted with a salmon-pink hue called *gerua* to honor the visiting Prince of Wales, Queen Victoria’s son and the future King Edward VII. It was during the same visit that the foundation stone was laid for the Albert Hall Museum, which today houses many of the artistic treasures of Rajasthan.

Rather than an overgrown museum piece, Jaipur is a dynamic living city filled with the exotic sounds, smells, and substance of modern India. South of the royal palace complex, the Johari Bazaar flaunts the best of local craftsmanship—jewelry and saris, carpets and camel-leather shoes and bags, miniature paintings, embroidered pillows, blue pottery, and silverwork. The bazaar also tenders tea stalls, sweetshops, juice stands, snake charmers, and even the occasional elephant.

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[travel.india.com/​jaipur](http://travel.india.com/jaipur), [jaipur.rajasthan.gov.in](http://jaipur.rajasthan.gov.in), [pinkcity.com](http://pinkcity.com), [jaipur.org.uk](http://jaipur.org.uk)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Jaipur offers plenty of hotel options, including former maharaja residences like the Rambagh Palace *([taj.tajhotels.com/​en-in/​taj-rambagh-palace-jaipur](http://taj.tajhotels.com/en-in/taj-rambagh-palace-jaipur))* and Royal Heritage Haveli, and the atmospheric Pearl Palace Heritage *([pearlpalaceheritage.com](http://pearlpalaceheritage.com)).* The Tourist Composite Ticket allows entrance to the city’s seven major historical sights.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Jaipur is a 90- to 105-minute plane flight from Mumbai and a nearly five-hour train ride from New Delhi via the *Shatabdi Express*. The city center is compact and easy to walk; outlying landmarks like Amber Fort are best reached by taxi or hotel car. National Geographic also offers trips to India *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_022_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_022_r1.jpg)

Women celebrate monsoon season: “At the first swing, O my mother, the clouds appear,” they sang. [Credit 22](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img22a)

Homecoming to Rajasthan

I**n 1976, *National Geographic* writer and photographer Raghubir Singh returned to his home state of Rajasthan, India, to meet and photograph the musicians, craftsmen, and nomads who lived there. “In legend our ancestors sprang from the sun, the moon, or fire and emulated the heroes of such ancient Indian epics as the *Ramayana,”* he wrote of his people, the Rajputs.**

**In the Thar Desert, Singh spoke with an outlaw who played his flute at night on the dunes and boasted of digging out of his jail cell with his bare hands. In Jaipur, he watched children playing in the streets beneath frescoes depicting the Hindu deity Krishna. In great fortress cities, he attended exuberant weddings in the palaces of former maharajas, and in rural villages, he watched as women on swings celebrated the beginning of the monsoon rains that ensured a good harvest: “Up go the swings as down come the rains,” Singh wrote.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_023_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_023_r1.jpg)

[Credit 23](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img23a)

CLASSIC SHOT

T**he famous Taj Mahal—built over 17 years in the 17th century—is a four-hour drive from Jaipur. Photographer Michael Melford showed up at sunrise, ahead of the tourists, to photograph the iconic structure. “There was this group of women who also came early and they were wearing beautiful colorful saris. I slowed down the shutter speed to get some motion and followed them as they walked toward the mausoleum.” He suggests that any visitor to the Taj Mahal arrive early to see it in morning light, without crowds, “but also stay there as it fills up, and enjoy all the colors of the Indian people.”**

~ MICHAEL MELFORD

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_024_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_024_r1.jpg)

Near the west entrance of Angkor Wat (above) sits a restored, eight-armed statue of the Hindu god Vishnu ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c01_r1.xhtml_img25)). [Credit 24](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img24a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_025_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_025_r1.jpg)

[Credit 25](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img25a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

CAMBODIA

**Angkor Wat**

Jungle-enshrouded temples dating to the ninth century

T**HE SOUNDS OF** modern Siem Reap are clamor: the hustle of the market and blaring of *tuk-tuk* (three-wheeled rickshaw) horns; the chatter of tourists and locals. But in the jungle just north of this gateway city, thousands of dancing apsaras, carved into crumbling temple walls, are an emblem of an ancient world of religion, artistic inspiration, and architectural ambition. The temple complex at Angkor is not only one of the world’s largest religious monuments, it is an elaborate expression of Hindu cosmology.

Built during the Khmer Empire, from the 9th to the 15th centuries, the massive 400-square-mile (1,036 km²) Angkor temple complex rises out of the dense jungle in central Cambodia. It consists of dozens of temples in various states of ruin, among the last visible remnants of the capital cities of the Khmer Empire, whose domain once extended from modern-day Myanmar to Vietnam.

Angkor Wat is the undisputed star of these temples. It was constructed during the reign of King Suryavarman II in the 12th century as a home for Vishnu, one of the main deities of Hinduism. Its five stone pinnacles—a central one surrounded by four smaller ones—symbolize the peaks of the mythical Mount Meru, the center of the physical and spiritual universe of Hinduism. Galleries of bas-reliefs depict characters and scenes from Hindu mythology and history, and rows of exquisite ornamental stone balusters fill the windows. Large columns help support the immense weight of the temple’s two tapered upper levels. A massive complex-encircling moat, about 650 feet (198 m) thick and guarded by sandstone lions and serpent-like *naga* deities, symbolizes the mythical ocean that separates Earth from the cosmos. But Angkor’s involved system of waterways once served a more prosaic purpose, too—it irrigated crops and provided the city’s population with freshwater.

Visitors stream into Angkor Wat by the thousands, ambling across its causeway and onto the temple grounds. They snap photos of the spires in reflecting pools at sunrise, and retreat to cool alcoves, thick with incense, later in the day.

With hired transport, visitors can hop from Angkor Wat to other must-sees in the complex. Highlights include Angkor Thom, a 3.5-square-mile (9 km²) city at the center of which is Bayon Temple, decorated by dozens of huge beaming stone faces; Preah Khan, a collapsing Buddhist university and city; and Ta Prohm, a 12th-century monastery being slowly strangled by the jungle roots of fig and silk-cotton trees.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_026_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_026_r1.jpg)

Buddhist monks stroll along the Siem Reap River, their distinctive saffron robes catching the sun. [Credit 26](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img26a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[tourismcambodia.com/​attractions/​angkor.htm](http://tourismcambodia.com/attractions/angkor.htm)***

**• HOW TO STAY: All visitors on a multiday pass (recommended) to the Angkor Archaeological Park retreat to Siem Reap between temple visits. There are budget options within the city limits, and ritzier lodgings, like the Khmer-style timber stilted bungalows at Sala Lodges *([salalodges.com](http://salalodges.com))*, are south of town.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Regular flights connect larger Asian hubs and the Cambodian capital, Phnom Penh, to Siem Reap. From there, organized tours or private guides can be hired for the 3.5-mile (5.5 km) trip to Angkor. All stop at the Angkor Conservation Area ticket booth for required permits and photo ID passes. National Geographic also offers trips to Cambodia *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_027_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_027_r1.jpg)

At Angkor, roots of silk-cotton trees creep like tentacles over the ruins of the temple Preah Kahn. [Credit 27](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img27a)

Ghosts of Angkor

I**n 1960, *National Geographic* published a view of Angkor Wat never seen before. Working with French archaeologists, artist Maurice Fievet re-created daily life during the Khmer reign with vivid, detailed illustrations. This was a feat, since, as *National Geographic* writer W. Robert Moore noted, the writing and books of the Khmers had long since been destroyed by fire, rot, and war.**

**Fievet had to rely instead on reliefs of everyday life carved on temple walls (“as real as action photographs,” wrote Moore) and Chinese eyewitness chronicles to create his depictions of court and village life, battles, and celebrations.**

**Meantime, Moore wandered the jungle-choked remnants of Ta Prohm. He was enchanted but deeply affected by it: “Moss overgrows fallen stones…A spider casts a web over a woman’s bare bosom. Doorway guardians stand leprous with lichen…Ta Prohm is like a prisoner manacled and trussed with heavy ropes.” Leaving the haunting ruin, he looked up with relief at the open blue sky.**

Hidden Journeys

LAOS

**Wat Phu, Champasak**

Remote ruins older than Angkor

**MORE THAN 200 MILES (322 KM)** northeast of Angkor in southern Laos, a remote Khmer outpost sits in an entirely different kind of landscape. The sleepy, lesser known remnants of Wat Phu rest in patchy fields grazed by water buffalo. These temple ruins contain foundations and structures that predate Angkor Wat by several centuries.

Yet, Angkor’s Khmer stylings are recognizable at Wat Phu, which was originally Hindu, and later converted to a Buddhist temple. A lingam-lined walking path bisects two rectangular pools, and massive sandstone bricks are strewn in a debris field around two weather-battered, roofless 11th- to 13th-century sandstone palaces. Tiers of steep, uneven stairs culminate at a sacred sanctuary atop a 330-foot (100 m) promontory. There, dancing apsaras line the entrance to a Buddhist temple.

The entrance to the temple complex is off a dusty road beyond the tiny town of Champasak, with French colonial buildings; it was home to regional kings until the 1940s. Near the ticket office, a brightly lit single-room museum protects and interprets recovered sculptures and architectural remnants from the area.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_028_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_028_r1.jpg)

A statue of Buddha at the sanctuary in Wat Phu in Laos [Credit 28](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img28a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[vatphou-champassak.com](http://vatphou-champassak.com/)***

**• HOW TO STAY: A handful of guesthouses are within walking or biking distance of Wat Phu, including the River Resort *([theriverresortlaos.com](http://theriverresortlaos.com))*, an eco-lodge with infinity pools overlooking longtail boats on the Mekong.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: The hub of this remote area is Pakxé, connected to greater Laos and beyond by limited Mekong boat access, bus service, and flights. Boats and buses crossing the Lao-Nippon Bridge connect east bank Pakxé with west bank Champasak 25 miles (40 km) away. Many visitors choose to explore the temple complex seven miles (11 km) south of Champasak by moped or bicycle.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_029_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_029_r1.jpg)

[Credit 29](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img29a)

CLASSIC SHOT

P**hotographer Amy Toensing captured these monks at Angkor Wat blessing tourists while visiting the Buddhist site on an around-the-world National Geographic Expedition. She recalls the monks’ intense spirituality, and she challenged herself to capture that mood. The monks demonstrated the “same devotion and ardor that must have been behind the construction of this huge, impressive monument—at a time when the people building it had relatively few resources,” she says. The details in the golden pedestal bowl, skein of red yarn, and flickering candles, with the praying Buddha figures, add to the meditative effect.**

~ AMY TOENSING

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_030_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_030_r1.jpg)

The Great Wall of China (above and [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c01_r1.xhtml_img31)) ribbons its way thousands of miles across the country, through mountains and deserts. [Credit 30](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img30a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_031_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_031_r1.jpg)

[Credit 31](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img31a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

CHINA

**Great Wall of China**

An ancient fortification thousands of miles long

T**HE GREAT WALL** snakes across northern China for hundreds and hundreds of miles, the world’s largest human-made military structure and one of its oldest. Qin Shi Huang Di, the first emperor to unify China under one dynasty (he also built the army of terra-cotta warriors), began constructing the Great Wall about 220 B.C. Anxious to protect China from marauding barbarians, Qin Shi Huang Di built onto existing earthworks and linked these old remnants into a unified wall as best he could. But the bulk of the wall still standing today, reinforced with stone and brick and with turrets and watchtowers, was constructed centuries later, during the Ming dynasty (1368–1644).

According to a recent Chinese study, this marvel of engineering rambles for more than 12,400 miles (19,960 km) from the Korean border west to the Gobi. (More conservative figures place the wall’s extent at about 5,500 miles/8,851 km.) The wall was built over centuries by soldiers, slaves, prisoners, criminals, and common people, and many died in their toils. Built on average 32 feet (10 m) high, the wall’s typical width is 16 feet (5 m)—enough space for 10 soldiers or five horses to stand abreast.

The Great Wall is not one uninterrupted rampart, but a network of many sections cobbled together. The building materials varied by era and location—in some places the wall was constructed of adobe bricks; in others, stone; in still others, wooden planks and earth. Sometimes features like hills and river gorges took the place of a built segment of the fortification.

Massive as it is, in the end the Great Wall failed its primary task: It didn’t keep out enemies. The Mongols rode right through gaps, conquering most of northern China during the early 13th century. Later, the Manchus overtook the Ming dynasty when the traitor General Wu Sangui decided they constituted China’s best chance at order, and let them through the wall.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_032_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_032_r1.jpg)

Villagers gather in a gate tower of the Great Wall to pray for peace and good harvests. [Credit 32](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img32a)

More than 10 million people visit the Great Wall of China every year. The beautifully preserved but crowded Badaling section is closest to Beijing. A little farther away, visitors can escape the hordes at the Mutianyu section of wall by climbing it and taking in views of far-off misty green hills. Farther from Beijing, the wall becomes more rudimentary and less restored—sometimes it disappears altogether. Here, in the far reaches of the former empire, visitors can best comprehend how ancient and extensive this ruin is.

The Great Wall continues to be one of China’s most enduring national symbols, but one myth can be put to rest once and for all: It cannot be seen from space.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *There in the mist, enormous, majestic, silent and terrible, stood the Great Wall of China.*
~ W. SOMERSET MAUGHAM, *ON A CHINESE SCREEN*

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[greatwall-of-china.com](http://greatwall-of-china.com), [cnto.org](http://cnto.org)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Escape the crowds by staying overnight at a hotel along a section of the wall—some even offer camping. Options include the eco-friendly Brickyard Retreat *([brickyardatmutianyu.com](http://brickyardatmutianyu.com))* at Mutianyu Great Wall, housed in a beautifully restored glazed tile factory.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Most visitors explore the wall from Beijing, via guided tour, taxi, or rental car, with the most popular section being Badaling, 40-some miles (64 km) from the city. Some other wall sections that can be visited include Mutianyu, Jiankou, Xiangshuihu, Juyongguan, Simatai, Jinshanling, and Huanghuacheng. National Geographic also offers trips to China *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_033_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_033_r1.jpg)

A Mongolian caravan in Nankow Pass (left); a village leans against the wall (top right); a colossal statue in Longmen Grottoes in Henan Province (bottom right). [Credit 33](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img33a)

The Great Wall in White

I**n 1923, *National Geographic* writer Adam Warwick described his wanderings along the Great Wall of China, from the eastern portion’s solidly imposing edifice to the crumbling ridge of clay and stones far to the west.**

**He spotted tiny villages leaning right against the wall, camel caravans and shaggy Mongol ponies heading through a pass, and shrines dedicated to the guardian spirits of the wall. Along desolate stretches, it was difficult to tell where the wall began and the mountains ended—“They seem, indeed, to have grown into one another,” he wrote.**

**Warwick’s favorite sight, however, was the wall in winter, “when the bare, jagged peaks over which the Wall passes are smoothed under a coverlet of snow,” he wrote. “Then the rocks take new and strange forms under their white mantle, then the distant views of hills and valleys…are brought unbelievably near.” That seems to be a timeless attraction: *National Geographic*’s contemporary photographer was drawn to the wall in winter, too (see [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c01_r1.xhtml_page42)).**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_034_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_034_r1.jpg)

[Credit 34](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img34a)

CLASSIC SHOT

P**hotographer George Steinmetz crash-landed a paraglider trying to capture an aerial winter image of the Great Wall for *National Geographic*. After that, he realized his best bet was to get a shot from the ground. He was shooting in another province months later when he got word it was snowing heavily on the Jinshanling section of the wall. He packed up, rushed to the airport, and drove all night to arrive by sunrise. “I shot all morning, afternoon, and again the next morning, but by far the best photos were shot that first sunrise, on an hour’s sleep,” Steinmetz says. He took this shot from high on the wall, slipping up icy steps to get a good perspective.**

~ GEORGE STEINMETZ

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_035_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_035_r1.jpg)

The temple at Wat Phra Kaew [Credit 35](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img35a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_036_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_036_r1.jpg)

Small boats of banana leaves and candles at the Loy Krathong festival on the Mekong in Chiang Rai [Credit 36](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img36a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

THAILAND

**Golden Triangle**

An eastern kingdom and former opium stronghold in northern Thailand

T**IGHT UP AGAINST** Laos and Myanmar, Thailand’s far-flung northwestern corner is a patchwork of tea plantations, hills of teak trees, and villages on stilts. Beginning in the 13th century, this remote mountainous region was the heart of the Lanna kingdom—“land of a million rice fields”—which was unified under King Mangrai. It had its own gracious way of life and a unique take on architecture (temples with steep, multitiered rooflines and lacquered pillars), cuisine (not much coconut milk or sugar, but lots of wild herbs and plants), and dance. By the end of the 19th century, the kingdom had been annexed to Siam (which was renamed Thailand in 1949), and Lanna culture had become endangered. Luckily, Princess Dara Rasmi, daughter of the last Lanna king, continued to follow Lanna traditions, and made earnest efforts to revive that culture in northern Thailand in the early 20th century.

Then there’s the region’s history with opium. For thousands of years, opium poppies were used for traditional medicine, but the Golden Triangle (so called by the CIA) became a center of major commercial production in the mid-20th century. By the 1950s, it had a reputation as the world’s most prolific opium supplier, but eradication efforts in the 1990s curtailed large-scale production. Today, old opium routes offer an enchanting place to roam—by car or on foot.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *He who conquers many thousand men in battle is not the noblest victor, but he who conquers himself is, indeed, the noblest victor.*
~ BUDDHIST PROVERB, HALL OF OPIUM IN SOP RUAK

Quiet Chiang Rai, north of Chiang Mai, was established as the Lanna capital in 1262 and is the region’s gateway today. Here, 14th-century Wat Phra Kaew still stands, a revered Buddhist temple with Lanna-style, multitiered roofs. Legend says that lightning struck the *chedi* (stupa) in 1434, revealing the fabled Emerald Buddha (now displayed in Bangkok in another—more famous—Wat Phra Kaew). Nearby, sleepy Chiang Saen on the Mekong River is another Lanna capital, dating from the 14th century and sprinkled with ancient temples. About six miles (10 km) upstream, Sop Ruak has two excellent opium museums that give visitors a window on the drug’s impact on the region.

Ethnic minorities live in villages throughout the Golden Triangle; these include the Hmong and the Karen (famous for their long necks). Caught up in the opium stronghold both as laborers as well as addicts, they have benefited from programs to transition the region back to farming. The Mae Fah Luang Art and Cultural Park near Chiang Rai is a fascinating place to delve into their traditions. Visitors should head out on a trek to explore the hill-tribe villages, spend the night in a bamboo stilt house, or rent a longtail boat to get out on the Mekong.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_037_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_037_r1.jpg)

Boats along the Mekong River transport visitors between Thailand, Laos, and Myanmar, with stops at temples and villages. [Credit 37](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img37a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[us.tourismthailand.org](http://us.tourismthailand.org), [maefahluang.org](http://maefahluang.org)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Lanjia Lodge *([asian-oasis.com](http://asian-oasis.com))* in Chiang Khong is an eco-friendly lodge committed to improving the life of hill-tribe villagers while sharing their culture and traditions with visitors. Packages include trekking and Golden Triangle exploration. For a (luxurious) jungle experience, the Four Seasons Tented Camp Golden Triangle *([fourseasons.com/​goldentriangle](http://fourseasons.com/goldentriangle))* offers 15 tents in a bamboo jungle.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: The city of Chiang Rai in northern Thailand is the main gateway for those visiting the Golden Triangle; the Chiang Rai airport has 75-minute flights that connect to Bangkok International Airport. National Geographic also offers trips to Thailand *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_038_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_038_r1.jpg)

The Indian buffalo—surly by nature—could stand down a tiger but was still gentle enough to carry a child. [Credit 38](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img38a)

Buffalo, Tigers, and Villagers

I**n 1928, *National Geographic* writer Merian C. Cooper went to Thailand (then Siam) and found a predominant attitude in the Nan district of the north: terror of the jungle-dwelling tiger. He wrote, “The tigers of the Nan district have a frightful reputation as man-killers, and it is a well-deserved one.” Records showed tigers had killed and eaten several hundred people in the Nan district in preceding years. The natives were afraid to even utter the beast’s name aloud.**

**Villagers managed to trap one notorious man-eater called Mr. Crooked. The ferocious cat was loath to be caged; it tore holes in its log-and-bamboo enclosure four times, with ceaseless energy, when the villagers attempted to repair it.**

**“The Laos are no match for these beasts,” Cooper admitted. “Only the elephant, and on occasion the buffalo, can make a stand against them.” Curiously, though the wild buffalo could match wits with a snarling tiger, it could also be domesticated to the point that children could ride it about (see [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c01_r1.xhtml_img38)).**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_039_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_039_r1.jpg)

[Credit 39](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img39a)

CLASSIC SHOT

I**n this photo by Tino Soriano, Kayan children in Chiang Rai wear traditional brass neck coils. Considered a sign of beauty, the rings—which make the neck appear long—may originally have been worn to ward off tiger attacks. These days, some are concerned that the ring-wearers are exploited for tourist dollars. Soriano spent several days in the region with the tribe. “I wanted the opportunity to share their lives more deeply than a circumstantial visitor,” he explains. He visited a local school to get these shots of children. “I remained for hours with the kids, so they felt comfortable in front of my camera,” he says. “It’s easy with such friendly people.”**

~ TINO SORIANO

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_040_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_040_r1.jpg)

The village of Batad is nestled into paddies. [Credit 40](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img40a)

Rice, harvested by hand in Banaue, is grown on terraces in the Philippine Cordilleras. [Credit 41](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img41a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

PHILIPPINES

**Rice Terraces of the Cordilleras**

Rice paddies carved into precipitous mountains

F**OR MORE THAN 2,000** years, Ifugao farmers—who just a century ago were engaged in the fearsome practice of headhunting—have tended rice terraces carved into the steep slopes of the remote, rugged Philippine Cordilleras. On the island of Luzon, these mountainside rice fields represent an ingenious and ancient collaboration of man and nature. The paddies were never intended for commerce or profit; to this day they simply feed the locals, who work hard to cultivate them so they can eat.

Following the natural contours of the region’s hills—some slope at an angle of 70 degrees—the terraces were carved by hand, and then fixed with stone or mud walls. So precipitous and ordered are the paddies that from certain angles, they resemble the architecturally precise tiers of a Maya temple. An intricate irrigation system channels water from mountaintop rain forests to the stepped pond fields. It is a system so effective that after two millennia, it remains substantively the same, though earthquakes have required the water system to be periodically rerouted.

Communal knowledge of everything from lunar cycles, to pest control, to soil conservation has been passed orally from one generation of Ifugao people to the next. And another thing every generation learns—rice cultivation is backbreaking work. A popular Filipino folk song says: “Planting rice is never fun, / Bent from morn till set of sun. / Cannot stand and cannot sit, / Cannot rest a little bit.”

But the significance of the rice paddies goes even beyond their beauty and function. They represent an ancient cultural identity that is still essential to modern Ifugao people. Twelve colorful agrarian rituals throughout the year celebrate various stages of rice production, and they culminate with postharvest rituals that incorporate dancing, traditional games, and native dress.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_042_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_042_r1.jpg)

The rice terraces of Bangaan have been cultivated for more than 2,000 years. [Credit 42](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img42a)

The rice terraces cover an expansive area in the Philippine Cordillera mountain range, but five clusters are included in a UNESCO World Heritage site: Batad, Bangaan, Mayoyao, Hungduan, and Nagacadan. Of these, Batad is the most famous, with a tiny village situated at the base of a dramatic bowl of terraces. Both the steep terraces and village are remote, and accessible only by foot. Rivaling Batad’s charm, the sprawling Mayoyao terraces are sprinkled with traditional farmers’ houses, or *bale.* Surrounding villages, including Pula and Kambulo, have their own striking paddies. Visitors are free to wander the steep, often harrowing paths that meander through the terraces. This is by far the best way to see this ancient landscape—and sturdy walking shoes and at least a moderate level of fitness are advised.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *Nothing in Nature is more lovely than a valley of green maturescent rice paddy, encircled by verdant hills.*
~ JOHN FOREMAN, *THE PHILIPPINES ISLANDS*

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[visitmyphilippines.com](http://visitmyphilippines.com), [banaue.info](http://banaue.info)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Stay in an authentic Ifugao nipa hut at Ramon’s Homestay and Restaurant in Batad (reserve through Agoda, *[agoda.com](http://agoda.com)*). Banaue View Inn *([banaue.info/​banaueview.html](http://banaue.info/banaueview.html))* atop Magubon hill overlooks the famed rice terraces.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: There are no direct flights. To reach Batad, take a bus or private car nine hours from Manila to Banaue (or fly from Manila to Cauayan or Tuguegarao and arrange a car). From Banaue, hire a tricycle or jeepney to the area called the Saddle; then walk 45 minutes to Batad.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_043_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_043_r1.jpg)

The man at lower right repairs a paddy while above him, Ifugaos carry logs along a motor road Americans built. [Credit 43](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img43a)

Taking Back the Philippines

“T**oday, as this is written, the largest American army ever assembled anywhere in the Pacific has landed on Luzon, north of Manila,” wrote Frederick Simpich in a 1945 issue of *National Geographic.* Invaded the day after the bombing of Pearl Harbor, the Philippines had been occupied by Japan since 1942\. Three years later, American soldiers were fighting island by island to take them back. A National Geographic map showed the islands in full color detail so readers could follow the American advance.**

**It was an era in which age-old landscapes were necessarily overlaid with new infrastructure. Read the original caption on this photo, “Americans carved the motor road, but Ifugaos built the rice terraces centuries ago.”**

**Filipino guerrillas and civilians fought, too: They saved downed American pilots, spied and informed on the Japanese, and sabotaged the enemy’s movements. “Heroic beyond any call of duty were the exploits of these Filipino irregulars,” Simpich wrote.**

Hidden Journeys

PHILIPPINES

**Palawan**

A tropical island paradise

**UNDERWATER EXPLORER JACQUES COUSTEAU** called Palawan’s chain of 1,780 islands the most beautiful place he’d ever explored. This tropical realm of limestone cliffs, gin-clear waters, sugary-sand beaches, and coral atolls strings its way about 400 miles (644 km) south to Borneo in the western Philippines. Its wildlife ranges from giant clams and the legless Malatgan River caecilian (a snakelike amphibian), to blue-naped parrots and the Chihuahua-size Philippine mouse deer.

On the northern tip of Palawan, El Nido’s Bacuit Bay has more than 800 species of fish. It’s also home to the rare dugong (commonly called a sea cow), a slow-moving creature that’s more closely related to the elephant than to many of its fellow sea mammals. Tubbataha Reefs Natural Park, on the archipelago’s main island (also called Palawan), is a major nesting spot for two species of endangered sea turtles.

The Puerto Princesa Subterranean River, also on the main island of Palawan, is Asia’s longest underground river, and the world’s second longest. It flows for five miles (8 km) through a limestone cave, emptying into a crystal clear lagoon in the South China Sea. Paddleboats take visitors partway inside the cavern to admire the enormous chambers and whimsical rock formations.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_044_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_044_r1.jpg)

Visitors kayak on the Big Lagoon on Miniloc Island in the Palawan archipelago. [Credit 44](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img44a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[visitpalawan.info](http://visitpalawan.info), [palawanisland.org](http://palawanisland.org), [visitmyphilippines.com](http://visitmyphilippines.com), [gopalawan.travel](http://gopalawan.travel)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Sheridan Beach Resort and Spa *([sheridanbeachresort.com](http://sheridanbeachresort.com))* is an eco-resort on the main island of Palawan. Lagen Island Resort *([elnidoresorts.com](http://elnidoresorts.com))* in El Nido is another good eco option. Amanpulo *([aman.com](http://aman.com))*, a private island northeast of Palawan, is accessible only by plane.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Puerto Princesa is Palawan’s main gateway. Cebu Pacific Air and Philippine Airlines have regular service from Manila (80 minutes) and Cebu (75 minutes). An 18- to 22-hour ferry ride *([gopalawan.travel/​palwan-transportation](http://gopalawan.travel/palwan-transportation))* connects Manila and Puerto Princesa.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_045_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_045_r1.jpg)

[Credit 45](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img45a)

CLASSIC SHOT

L**ocals work on one of the rice terraces of Banaue, on the island of Luzon in the Philippines. “The beautiful landscape obscures the fact that this is backbreaking work,” says photographer Lynn Johnson. “Just being there was a lesson in how most of the people in the world work hard to get by.” The photo shoot, which involved scaling the steep terraces, wasn’t easy for Johnson either. At that point she was a beginner photographer. “I had not traveled widely, so every location was like stepping into a dream—a sweaty, hardworking, nervous-making dream,” she says. “It was like being thrown into the pool to learn how to swim without a lesson.”**

~ LYNN JOHNSON

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_046_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_046_r1.jpg)

Sunrise washes over the bell-shaped stupas of Borobudur (above), revealing some of its exquisitely carved reliefs ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c01_r1.xhtml_img47)). [Credit 46](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img46a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_047_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_047_r1.jpg)

[Credit 47](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img47a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

INDONESIA

**Borobudur**

Buddhism rises to a spectacular summit

T**HE GREATEST INSTANCE** of Buddhist architecture in all of Indonesia, the massive temple complex of Borobudur is the world’s biggest Buddhist temple. It’s also debatably the largest stone structure in the Southern Hemisphere.

Constructed during the eighth and ninth centuries A.D., Borobudur was a crowning achievement of the Shailendra dynasty, a Buddhist monarchy that reigned over much of Java island. The fertile Kedu Plain of central Java, not far from modern-day Yogyakarta, was the center of their kingdom and the place where the Shailendra erected their most enduring monuments.

Rising 95 feet (29 m) above the plain is Borobudur, fashioned from some two million volcanic stones gathered from the surrounding plain and fitted together in perfect harmony. From a distance, it resembles a step pyramid, but on closer examination, the temple is actually a much more complicated structure.

The architecture (and the path to the summit) reflects Buddhist cosmology—most agree that it mimics a giant mandala: three levels of human existence rising to a state of nirvana. The base is a metaphor for *kamadhatu*, the sphere of humanly desires; the five square terraces of the midsection represent *rupadhatu*, the sphere of form where humans have abandoned their desires but not yet their bodies; the three circular platforms and large stupa (or dome-shaped structure) at the summit symbolize *arupadhatu*, the sphere of formlessness or cosmic consciousness that marks the paramount achievement of existence.

It gets even more complex. The terraces house 72 smaller stupas and more than 500 Buddha statues. Arrayed along the walls and balustrades are 1,460 stone reliefs representing scenes from the life of Gautama Buddha as well as teachings from the dharma and sutras (rules). Many of the latter are masterpieces of Buddhist art.

Borobudur was rediscovered by Sir Stamford Raffles during the British occupation of Java during the Napoleonic Wars. It was meticulously restored in the 1970s and became a place of active Buddhist worship again.

Buddhist monks visit almost daily, but the apex of the sacred calendar is Vesak Day, when hundreds of saffron-clad monks flock to the holy site. Celebrated during a full moon in May or June, the holy day commemorates the birth, enlightenment, and passage into nirvana of Gautama Buddha.

Vesak rites also take place at nearby Mendut and Pawon Temples. Built about the same time as Borobudur, these smaller shrines are cosmically linked to the larger temple, the trio arranged in a perfect line across the plain.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_048_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_048_r1.jpg)

Monks release a lantern into the air on Vesak Day at Borobudur temple. [Credit 48](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img48a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[borobudurpark.com](http://borobudurpark.com)***

**• HOW TO STAY: The temple complex is located about 25 miles (40 km) from Yogyakarta, where there are many hotels, restaurants, and other tourist services. Manohara Resort is only a few minutes’ walk from Borobudur Temple *([manoharaborobudur.com](http://manoharaborobudur.com)).* Vesak Day (May or June) is a wonderful time to visit, but also the most crowded. Borobudur at sunrise and sunset is also special.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Yogyakarta is a one-hour flight or nine-hour train ride from Jakarta, Indonesia’s capital city and primary international gateway. Hop a local taxi to Borobudur or sign up for one of the many guided day trips offered by travel shops in Yogyakarta.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_049_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_049_r1.jpg)

With the help of a guide, *National Geographic* author Helen Schreider mounts the rim of Java’s 7,641-foot (2,329 m) Mount Bromo. [Credit 49](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img49a)

Eruptions in Indonesia

I**n 1961, *National Geographic* husband-wife team Helen and Frank Schreider went island-hopping in Indonesia in an amphibious jeep. Their first evening on the island of Java, at the encouragement of his host Mrs. Mohasson, Frank enthusiastically poured the local *sambal* (pepper sauce) over his Indonesian meal. “The result was as explosive as the eruption of Krakatau, Indonesia’s famous volcano,” he wrote. “Blinded by tears, I groped for the tea in a frantic effort to put out the fire. ‘It adds a little something, doesn’t it?’ asked Mrs. Mohasson modestly.”**

**It wasn’t his only encounter with a volcano; in this image, a guide helps Helen up to the rim of Java’s Mount Bromo. She and Frank ascended a strenuous 12-mile (19.5 km) trail at night to reach the 8,000-foot (2,438 m) crest. Winded at the top, Helen pointed with dismay to a route descending the opposite slope. “ ‘Why yes, tuan,’ the guide said casually, ‘that’s a road. You could have driven all the way here from Probolinggo.’ ”**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_050_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_050_r1.jpg)

Kyoto’s marvels include the iconic Kiyomizu-dera temple ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c01_r1.xhtml_img51)) and the Arashiyama Bamboo Grove’s rustling stalks (above). [Credit 50](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img50a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_051_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_051_r1.jpg)

[Credit 51](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img51a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

JAPAN

**Kyoto**

The grace and formal traditions of Japan

J**UST ABOUT EVERYTHING** the world knows of Japan—geisha, temples, and raked pebble Zen gardens—can be found in Kyoto, a city of exquisite Eastern architecture, landscape, and cultural tradition.

Kyoto was Japan’s capital for more than 1,000 years and is now a UNESCO World Heritage site with hundreds of temples and shrines.

One of the most famous is Kinkaku-ji, or the Golden Temple, on the northwest periphery of Kyoto. Built in the 14th century as a villa for the Japanese shogun Ashikaga Yoshimitsu and partially gilded, it was later converted to a Zen temple. It burned in 1950, but was soon restored, and later sheathed with even more gold leafing. Set over a still pond that reflects its brilliance, the temple now houses sacred relics and can be a blinding spectacle on a sunny day.

Kiyomizu-dera, the Temple of Pure Water, was built near a waterfall in 778\. It is sublime during spring’s cherry blossom season and the maple foliage in fall, but it is crowded year-round. It is reached by strolling through the historic Higashiyama neighborhood, which has been selling souvenirs and refreshments to visitors for more than a millennium.

The temple complex includes a multilevel pagoda, and a grand terrace with views over the city and a gorge. At the waterfall itself, metal cups attached to poles allow visitors to sip from the cascade. Each stream offers a different blessing.

Across town lies another astonishing site, the Fushimi Inari Shrine to the god of rice. Here, a mountainside path passes under thousands of bright orange arches. The trails stretch for more than two miles (3 km), past hundreds of stone and bronze foxes, the animal that is said to be the messenger of Inari.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_052_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_052_r1.jpg)

Geisha (here applying makeup) are traditional hostesses trained in the Japanese arts. [Credit 52](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img52a)

Other visitors seek solace at Sanjusangendo, a temple with 1,001 statues of Kannon, the goddess of mercy. The temple, stretching 394 feet (120 m), is Japan’s longest wooden building.

On the western outskirts of Kyoto, thousands of towering bamboo stalks sway in the wind in Arashiyama Bamboo Grove. Some visitors ride bikes though the forest, while others take rickshaws or dress up as geisha for their visit. However they pass through, they find a place unique for its sounds and patterns of light and shadow.

Kyoto is also one of the few places in the world to see geisha, traditional hostesses trained in classic Japanese arts such as dancing and singing. Gion is the best known of several city districts where young women called *maiko* are apprenticed in the secretive lifestyle. And while spending an evening with a geisha can be difficult to arrange and expensive, many visitors head out at dusk to spot the elaborately dressed young women clacking by on wooden sandals as they head to appointments.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *I used to see the Golden Temple soaring up into the morning sky amidst the rays of the sun as it rose from the folds of these eastern hills.*
~ YUKIO MISHIMA, *THE TEMPLE OF THE GOLDEN PAVILION*

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[kyoto.travel/​en](http://kyoto.travel/en)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Experience a traditional *ryokan* inn at the 12-room Ohanabo *([ohanabo.com](http://ohanabo.com))*, where you’ll sleep on the floor on futons and enjoy an elaborate multicourse *kaiseki* meal. The Hyatt Regency Kyoto *([kyoto.regency.hyatt.com](http://kyoto.regency.hyatt.com))* offers Western luxury and Japanese grace just a few steps from the Sanjusangendo temple. For a budget choice, try Eco and Tec Kyoto *([ecoandtec.jp/​en](http://ecoandtec.jp/en))*, with free breakfasts and massage chairs.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Kyoto is within range of several international airports and is also easily reached on the Shinkansen bullet train, which leaves regularly from Tokyo. The trip takes about three hours and twenty minutes. National Geographic also offers trips to Japan *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_053_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_053_r1.jpg)

This 10-inch (25 cm) heirloom doll, worth $80 in 1976, represents a lion dancer of Kabuki theater. [Credit 53](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img53a)

Heirloom Dolls

“P **ast and present in Kyoto are like loving sisters who are always in one another’s thoughts, and often in one another’s houses,” wrote *National Geographic* writer Charles McCarry in 1976\. He explored the city’s glorious sense of harmony and tradition, kept alive by its artists, priests, and poets. McCarry reveled in the perfect silence at Daisen-in temple at dawn; wrote haiku with master poet Kaido Maruyama; and was spellbound by a torchlit Noh performance in the courtyard of Heian Shrine.**

**He also spent hours in the workshop of father-son artists who crafted intricately detailed armor for dolls, creations that could take a year to make and could sell for up to $30,000\. The dolls, ordered by families to add to their heirlooms, were highly technical undertakings. The artists consulted authoritative texts and took field trips to armor museums to study exhibits and ascertain old methods of craftsmanship. These beautiful dolls, McCarry observed, “become treasures, not toys.”**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_054_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_054_r1.jpg)

[Credit 54](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img54a)

CLASSIC SHOT

T**he Japanese have observed the springtime ritual of *hanami* (flower viewing), specifically cherry blossoms, for more than 1,200 years. Traditionally, this was a time to reflect on the ephemeral nature of life. The ritual is even more poignant at night, which the Japanese call *yozakura.* “Many famous gardens and parks in Kyoto are artificially lit with floodlights during yozakura,” say photographers Diane Cook and Len Jenshel. “Working at night with artificial light is always a challenge—but one we love. Magical things happen with evening light.” Here, they have captured the ritual at the Hirano Shrine, where it has taken place every year since 985.**

~ DIANE COOK AND LEN JENSHEL

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_055_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_055_r1.jpg)

The Vltava River runs under Charles Bridge and through the heart of Prague, capital of Czechia (the Czech Republic). [Credit 55](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img55a)

The huge slabs of Stonehenge (above) and mounded Silbury Hill ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c02_r1.xhtml_img57)) are evidence of ancient human societies on the British Isles. [Credit 56](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img56a)

UNITED KINGDOM

**Stonehenge, Avebury, Orkney**

Ancient megaliths on the English plains

N**EAR PASTURES OF GRAZING** sheep and quiet country towns, Neolithic sites in Great Britain have withstood thousands of years of blustery days. An organized prehistoric society once built earthen mounds, rock-lined tombs, and mysterious stone monuments, feats of engineering so ancient they largely predate written language.

Stonehenge’s sarsen stones were hoisted into place nearly 5,000 years ago. Early laborers brought them to England’s Salisbury Plain from the Preseli Hills in Wales nearly 180 miles (290 km) away; some of the monstrous bluestone slabs weighed up to 50 tons (45 metric tons). One early theory about the monument was that the cosmically oriented stones helped the builders mark the passage of time. Or they may have served as a sacred temple for healing or ritual hunting of aurochs, extinct relatives of modern cattle. But research on Stonehenge is ongoing and new hypotheses are continually put forth.

Though Stonehenge is the British Isles’ best known Neolithic site, it isn’t the oldest or biggest. Twenty-five miles (40 km) north, the world’s largest prehistoric stone circle, now visible as a giant earthwork berm and trench, surrounds the quaint village of Avebury. One hundred eighty stones once formed the henge, and though many have been toppled or buried, some sandstone pillars still stand in partial circles at the site. Avebury’s surrounding area includes several smaller sites like Silbury Hill, a large prehistoric mound, and the Sanctuary, a ring of timber posts (later replaced with stones) that stood atop human remains for centuries.

Nearly 700 miles (1,127 km) to the north, on the grassy, windswept archipelago of Orkney off the coast of Scotland, humans have eked out an existence for 8,000 years. In 1850, shifting sands during a storm on the shore of the Bay of Skaill uncovered the settlement of Skara Brae, with stacked-stone dwellings arranged with fireplaces, stone beds, and dressers. These were inhabited around the time Stonehenge was built.

About eight miles (13 km) southeast, Maeshowe Chambered Cairn is a 4,500-year-old tomb aligned to send a shaft of sunlight beaming into its inner chamber on the winter solstice. Nearby, the Ring of Brodgar and the Standing Stones of Stenness are situated between two freshwater lochs. Between these two stone circles, archaeologists discovered in 2002 another huge Neolithic stone temple complex, Ness of Brodgar. It has dozens of buildings, walkways, colored stonework, and artifacts like Neolithic art and fine stone tools.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_058_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_058_r1.jpg)

Houses at the settlement of Skara Brae on Scotland’s Orkney Islands had stone beds and dressers. [Credit 58](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img58a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[stonehengeandaveburywhs.org](http://stonehengeandaveburywhs.org), [visitorkney.com](http://visitorkney.com)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Several cozy bed-and-breakfasts operate in Salisbury, nine miles (14.5 km) south of Stonehenge. Enjoy English fare at the historic (and reputedly haunted) Red Lion Pub, encircled by Avebury’s ancient henge. In Orkney, try the family-run Ayre Hotel *([ayrehotel.co.uk](http://ayrehotel.co.uk))* near Kirkwall’s center.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Same-day package tours lump Stonehenge and Avebury together and depart from London and Glastonbury. Kirkwall Airport in Orkney has flights that connect to London, England; and Glasgow and Edinburgh, both in Scotland. Once there, Tour Orkney *([tourorkney.co.uk](http://tourorkney.co.uk))* has two tours that visit all of the main Neolithic sites, including the recent Ness of Brodgar excavations.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_059_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_059_r1.jpg)

Druids conduct solstice rituals at Stonehenge with a rose, water, embers, bread, and a linen cloth. [Credit 59](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img59a)

Druids at Stonehenge

M**IT professor Harold E. Edgerton first viewed Stonehenge through the rear-gunner’s escape hatch of a bomber during World War II while training airmen in night reconnaissance photography. In 1959, he returned at the midsummer solstice to study and photograph the stones further. For five years prior, National Geographic photographers at Stonehenge had been thwarted by clouds and fog; Edgerton finally got clear skies.**

**He marveled at the monument’s extraordinary workmanship: “How did men who never knew steel shape this tough, dense sandstone?” Camped out overnight, he took pictures of the eerie stones with the light of emergency flares.**

**In the chilly hours before sunrise, hundreds of pilgrims gathered among the stones, including a group of Druids, who have performed rites at Stonehenge for centuries. As dawn broke on the solstice, devotees wearing flowing white robes marched in a processional around the monument and chanted incantations at the Altar Stone.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_060_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_060_r1.jpg)

Daraxa’s Garden ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c02_r1.xhtml_img61)) and the massive fortress and palaces of the Alhambra (above) were built by the Moors in Granada. [Credit 60](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img60a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_061_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_061_r1.jpg)
