SPAIN

**Granada**

Moorish palaces perched over modern Granada

I**N THE 13TH CENTURY**, Muhammad I Ibn Nasr, al-Ahmar, the first ruler of the Nasrid dynasty (the last Muslim dynasty in Spain), spotted a red-rock plateau in the foothills fronting the Sierra Nevada. He had a monumental idea, and over the next 300 years the outcropping was transformed into an imposing palace-city: the Alhambra. It was endowed with wondrous works of engineering and covered with elaborate Moorish decoration. The visionary king had water diverted from the Darro River to fill fountains and pools, and to sustain elegant gardens.

The rosy-hued fortress was constructed of clay-and-gravel bricks strong enough to last for centuries. Its plain, heavy walls belie the exquisite cavernous, domed Islamic palaces within. Latticed, arched windows admit natural light that illuminates the geometric carved and tile patterns of interior rooms. These ornate spaces represent the height of the Nasrid reign. They are so fantastical that after the Reconquista, the Christian rulers that assumed reign of the Iberian Peninsula imitated the Moorish design in their own buildings.

The Alhambra fell into neglect in the 18th century, and it wasn’t until American writer Washington Irving visited in the late 1820s, writing effusively of its grandeur, that restoration efforts were seriously undertaken.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *All the fairy fretwork of these domes, apparently as unsubstantial as the crystal fabrics of a morning’s frost, yet exist after the lapse of centuries.*
~ WASHINGTON IRVING, *TALES OF THE ALHAMBRA*

Today, visitors to the Alhambra can trace its architectural evolution atop Al Sabika hill, on which it sits. The Alcazaba on the promontory’s westernmost point has military watchtowers, terraces, and the Plaza de Armas, all of which date to the mid-13th century. The inner sanctums of the Nasrid rulers include the Mexuar, which once served as a reception and meeting room; the Comares Palace, the official residence of the king; and the Palace of the Lions, the private chambers of the royal family.

In the magnificent gardens, porticoes, and pools of the Generalife, the Moorish kings found respite and rest. The Palace of Charles V—built after the Catholic conquest of Granada in 1492—was the Alhambra’s last great construction. The early 1500s-era palace blends Roman influence with Moorish symmetry and is considered one of the most important architectural achievements of the Spanish Renaissance.

Tickets to the Alhambra are available in limited quantities and for certain time slots, and sometimes sell out weeks in advance during the busy summer season. Local tour operators can often get discounts, and they can give visitors an alternative to the standard self-guided visit.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_062_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_062_r1.jpg)

Columns frame the Court of the Lions, one of the main patios at the heart of the Alhambra. [Credit 62](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img62a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[alhambra-patronato.es](http://alhambra-patronato.es), [alhambradegranada.org](http://alhambradegranada.org)***

**• HOW TO STAY: There are plenty of hotels in Granada, but only one property offers overnight stays atop Al Sabika hill. Parador de Granada *([parador.es/​en/​paradores/​parador-de-granada](http://parador.es/en/paradores/parador-de-granada))*, a 15th-century convent built on a former Nasrid palace, houses 40 modern guest rooms.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Domestic flights land at Granada Airport, but most international arrivals come via Málaga, 85 miles (137 km) to the southwest and connected to Granada by bus and train. Private cars cannot visit the Alhambra, so most visitors reach the citadel from Plaza Nueva via public bus or a hike up a steep footpath. National Geographic also offers trips to Spain *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_063_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_063_r1.jpg)

Moorish architects adorned the king’s palace in Fez, Morocco, with colorful arabesques and tilework. [Credit 63](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img63a)

Following the Moors

I**n 1988, *National Geographic* writer Thomas J. Abercrombie followed in the footsteps of the Moors, first as they swept through Morocco to conquer Spain in the eighth century A.D., and then as they retreated from the Christians during the Reconquista. In Córdoba, Abercrombie watched stonemasons repair the walls of the glorious Mezquita; in Granada, he listened to Arabic poetry sung in the city’s labyrinthine Albaicín quarter; and in the Alpujarras Mountains, which were the last Moorish stronghold, he visited mile-high villages that still bear their Arabic names.**

**Abercrombie was dazzled by the Moorish art and architecture, describing how filigreed arabesques, curving arches, and complex geometric patterns delight the eye. Nowhere was this ornate decoration on better display than the Alhambra. “Here the walls themselves speak—if you know Arabic,” Abercrombie wrote. “We traced out poems in the supple calligraphy of the friezes, archways, and fountains.”**

Hidden Journeys

SPAIN

**Córdoba**

Home to a massive cathedral-mosque

**FOOTFALLS ECHO AMONG HUNDREDS** of Byzantine columns, as visitors wander under the red-and-white-striped arches they support. This is the mazelike interior of La Mezquita, a sprawling mosque turned Catholic cathedral. It is the gem of Córdoba, once a great city of medieval Europe and the Islamic capital of Spain from the eighth to the eleventh centuries, and now a smaller, quieter city than Granada. Like the Alhambra 109 miles (175 km) to the southeast, La Mezquita blends Moorish and Christian architecture. The original mosque was built on the site of a Christian church in A.D. 784, and over subsequent centuries, it was expanded until it doubled in size. It was converted into a Catholic church in 1235.

By decree from the same Carlos V whose palace dominates the center of the Alhambra, La Mezquita’s interior was replaced in the 16th century with a cathedral that still offers daily mass. In springtime, the fragrance from blossoms in the adjacent Patio de los Naranjos (the mosque’s former court of ablutions) wafts into the mosque-cathedral. And from atop the Torre Campanario, the 177-foot-tall (54 m) minaret turned bell tower, visitors have great views of the city’s historic downtown—settlement here dates to Roman rule in the second century B.C.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_064_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_064_r1.jpg)

Stripes on the double arches in the interior of La Mezquita in Córdoba [Credit 64](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img64a)

**• HOW TO STAY: The Hospes Palacio del Bailío *([hospes.com/​en/​ourhotels](http://hospes.com/en/ourhotels))* in Córdoba’s historic downtown has the city’s only modern Roman baths, its own fruit orchard, and deluxe suites with hardwood floors and 17th-century frescoes.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Córdoba is accessible by high-speed rail, bus, and standard train from Seville, Málaga, and Madrid. Once there, the historic city and most of its star attractions are easily navigable on foot.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_065_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_065_r1.jpg)

[Credit 65](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img65a)

CLASSIC SHOT

M**oorish royalty used the terraced gardens, fountains, and pools of the Generalife—the summer palace—as a place of rest and a refuge from Granada’s searing summertime heat. “I wasn’t at the Alhambra to shoot,” says photographer Stephen Alvarez. “I was attending a special dinner, walking around the garden beforehand when the light on the summer palace got to be unbelievable.” Alvarez, who keeps a personal visual diary each day, observed of this image, “it just goes to show it pays to have a camera ready!” It had been overcast for much of the day, and the sun came out for just a few minutes before sunset.**

~ STEPHEN ALVAREZ

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

FRANCE

**Normandy**

A coastal province of rugged beauty and profound history

N**ORMANDY BECAME THE SITE** of a momentous World War II invasion on June 6, 1944, when Allied troops crossed the English Channel and stormed its beaches. D-Day began the liberation of France and the Allied drive to Berlin that forced Germany to surrender. Visiting the windy beaches and memorials today—including Pointe du Hoc, the Mémorial de Caen, and the Normandy American Cemetery—is a moving and deeply humbling experience. Allied troops fought fiercely in the most dramatic operation of the European theater of war, and thousands perished during the Battle of Normandy, along with thousands of German soldiers and French civilians.

But long before D-Day, this apple-growing province of France had already figured prominently in world history. Medieval villages dot the countryside, and each has its own tale of Vikings, English conquerors, Impressionists, and saints.

One of the most famous sites is Mont-St.-Michel. The jumble of Romanesque and Gothic buildings teeters on a rocky isle in the middle of a bay. Powerful tides rush in to surround the mount, and then recede to expose vast, shimmering mudflats. In 708, legend has it, archangel Michael instructed the Bishop of Avranches in a vision to construct an oratory on the isle. The bishop did, and pilgrims have since wandered up its single street to the monastery.

To the east is Bayeux, the first city freed by the Allies during the Battle of Normandy. The charming town also recognizes another decisive (and much earlier) battle with the Bayeux Tapestry—the Battle of Hastings of 1066\. This 230-foot-long (70 m) meticulously embroidered linen portrays in detail William the Conqueror’s invasion of England from the shores of Normandy.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_068_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_068_r1.jpg)

Chalk cliffs, beaches, and arches along the coastline at Étretat, in the Normandy region of France. [Credit 68](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img68a)

Then there’s Rouen, the ancient capital of the Vikings who settled in the region. During the Hundred Years’ War, the fate of France’s patron saint, Jeanne d’Arc (Joan of Arc), unfolded at the town’s market square. A modern church now marks the spot where she was burned at the stake in 1431, crying: “Rouen! Rouen! Must I die here?”

Art lovers will recognize Rouen’s Cathédrale Notre-Dame for its connection to another famous Frenchman—Impressionist painter Claude Monet, who captured the Gothic cathedral on canvas in many different lights. Visitors can find more insight into this art movement—which at the time was criticized for departing from the more formal established style—at several other places that inspired the Impressionists. These include the Côte d’Albâtre (Alabaster Coast), the fishing village of Honfleur, and Monet’s house and gardens at Giverny.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *It is beautiful here…and I want to paint it all—my head is bursting.*
~ CLAUDE MONET

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[www.normandy-tourism.org](http://www.normandy-tourism.org)***

**• HOW TO STAY: If you can, stay overnight on Mont-St.-Michel to enjoy the quiet after the day-trippers have left. The hotels aren’t fancy; Auberge Saint-Pierre *([auberge-saint-pierre.fr](http://auberge-saint-pierre.fr))* is a cozy choice. To experience the scenic inspiration that kicked off Impressionism, stay in Honfleur at the Manoir des Impressionnistes *([en.manoirdesimp­ressionnistes.com](http://en.manoirdesimpressionnistes.com))*, an enchanting 18th-century manor house overlooking the sea.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Normandy is an easy train trip from Paris *([raileurope.com](http://raileurope.com)).* Base yourself at Rouen, Caen, or Bayeux, all less than three hours from Paris. Or rent a car and explore the tiny villages in this province. National Geographic also offers trips to France *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_069_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_069_r1.jpg)

The spools of wire on this false beach powered a telephone line that was laid under the English Channel after D-Day. [Credit 69](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img69a)

A Secret Allied Strategy on D-Day

A **year after the D-Day invasion, *National Geographic* detailed a top-secret Allied strategy that contributed to its success: portable harbors, codeword Mulberry.**

**With the French ports sealed by the Nazis, the Allies needed to find a way to land heavy artillery on the beachhead during an invasion. On D-Day, 60 blockships (damaged and obsolete ships) crossed the English Channel, lined up bow to stern off the Normandy coast, and scuttled themselves to make breakwaters. Sunken concrete blocks, 200 feet (61 m) long, created additional seawalls. They formed two Mulberries, an American one at Omaha Beach and a British one at Gold Beach.**

**Ship decks on pontoons created floating piers that reached the shore. Protected from ocean waves by the artificial breakwaters, the piers enabled the Allies to offload thousands of tons of vehicles and supplies every day. Remains of the British Mulberry can still be seen offshore near the Musée du Débarquement in Arromanches.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_070_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_070_r1.jpg)

[Credit 70](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img70a)

CLASSIC SHOT

Q**uicksand-filled mudflats were the last obstacle for pilgrims making their way to the monastery on Mont-St.-Michel. Today’s visitors must hire a guide to negotiate the flats, and look out for the incoming tide. “I was here just before the full moon in spring,” says photographer Babak Tafreshi. “The sea goes out about 10 miles (16 km) from the coast and comes back in massive flood rivers.” He took this image during the lowest tide; a few hours later the area was under 40 feet (12 m) of water. This boy, he notes, was not on the flats alone—he was part of a family group, not visible in the frame.**

~ BABAK TAFRESHI

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_071_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_071_r1.jpg)

Sunrise over the cathedral along the banks of the Seine River [Credit 71](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img71a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_072_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_072_r1.jpg)

Gargoyles command Paris from the Cathédrale Notre-Dame. [Credit 72](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img72a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

FRANCE

**Île de la Cité, Paris**

Birthplace of the City of Light

Î**LE DE LA CITÉ**, a small, ship-shaped isle in the middle of the Seine River, was the first part of Paris to be settled more than 2,000 years ago. It has been the historic and cultural heart of this great European city ever since. From above, it looks rather like the Paris coat of arms, which is adorned with the image of a great ship and the Latin words *fluctuat nec mergitur* inscribed beneath: “Whatever the storm, the ship sails on.” And as a ship navigates choppy seas, so has Île de la Cité seen Paris through centuries of revolution, romance, plague, beheadings, coronations, invasions, and liberation.

The Parisii tribe was the first to permanently settle on this small natural island. When the Romans arrived in 52 B.C., Julius Caesar himself understood its strategic possibilities, situated as it was in the middle of the Seine, and by A.D. 508, Paris was the seat of the kings of the Franks.

Between 1163 and 1272, the Cathédrale Notre-Dame, one of the greatest Gothic cathedrals of the Middle Ages, was built over the ruins of two earlier churches and a Roman temple dedicated to Jupiter. The cathedral is an architectural masterpiece of flying buttresses, gargoyles, rose windows, Gothic towers, exquisite stained-glass windows, and an ornate facade.

With such a grand cathedral at its heart, Île de la Cité historically took on the role as the city’s religious center, but it also served as the secular seat. Today, judges sit in court in the Palais de Justice, on the very spot where the Roman governor ruled. Adjacent to that is one of the world’s most spectacular rayonnant Gothic gems, Sainte-Chapelle. Louis IX (eventually canonized a saint) built this diminutive palace chapel between 1242 and 1248 to house the relic Crown of Thorns, thought to be that worn by Christ. In the second-floor sanctuary are 50-foot-high (15 m) stained-glass windows that sparkle like jewels on sunny days.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *He who contemplates the depths of Paris is seized with vertigo. Nothing is more fantastic. Nothing is more tragic. Nothing is more sublime.*
~ VICTOR HUGO, FRENCH AUTHOR

Baron Georges-Eugène Haussmann destroyed much of Île de la Cité’s ancient character in the mid-19th century, when he was hired to modernize the city with broad avenues and uniform buildings. The stalls and timbered houses that edged right up to the grand cathedral were razed to create a large parvis—which, it must be noted, is a splendid space from which to stand and admire Notre-Dame.

But some places luckily escaped Haussmann’s wrecking balls. Place Dauphine, on the isle’s western end, is a quiet, secluded square surrounded by town houses; it retains much of its 17th-century historic charm.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_073_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_073_r1.jpg)

A couple at the verdant Square du Vert-Galant rounds the tip of Île de la Cité in the middle of the Seine. [Credit 73](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img73a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[us.france.fr](http://us.france.fr), [parisinfo.com](http://parisinfo.com), [notredamedeparis.fr](http://www.notredamedeparis.fr/), [sainte-chapelle.fr](http://sainte-chapelle.fr)***

**• HOW TO STAY: The best way to experience Paris is to stay in a historic hotel. Five-star La Réserve Paris *([lareserve-paris.com](http://lareserve-paris.com))*, housed in a 19th-century mansion in the eighth arrondissement, was once Pierre Cardin’s residence. The Presidential Suite has views of Notre-Dame’s spires. Or use VRBO *([vrbo.com](http://vrbo.com))* to rent a 17th-century apartment on Place Dauphine.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: The closest Paris Métro stations to Île de la Cité are Saint-Michel (Line 4/fuschia) and Cité (Line 4/fuschia). National Geographic also offers trips to France *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_074_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_074_r1.jpg)

A sculpture student at the École des Beaux-Arts (bottom left); toy sailboats to rent at Luxembourg Garden (top left); admiring Paris from Château de Vincennes (right) [Credit 74](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img74a)

Paris in Spring

I**n 1936, *National Geographic* writer Maynard Owens Williams wrote a paean to springtime in Paris. He captured the city’s joyous embrace of warmth and sunshine as fresh leaves shaded the boulevards, street cafés set out tables and chairs, women strolled the avenues in the latest fashions, and children shrieked in delight at petting zoos and Punch and Judy puppet shows.**

**“Young couples walk in this ageless city arm in arm and openly steal kisses, some of which must be for Paris itself,” Williams wrote.**

**He watched a powerful play staged in front of Notre-Dame’s soaring facade, and spotted boys sailing toy boats in the Palais Royal fountain. In the Tuileries Gardens, “human beings suddenly outnumber the statues,” he wrote. “Students read, artists paint, and even self-satisfied bureaucrats from nearby offices become human.”**

**In the midst of this bliss, however, was a disquieting hint of the war to come: Métro stops were being outfitted to serve as shelters from poison gas attacks.**

Hidden Journeys

ENGLAND

**York Minster**

A medieval Gothic masterpiece

**GOTHIC ARCHITECTURE** came to England by way of France in the mid-12th century, and every major stage of Gothic architectural development in England is reflected in the gorgeous sacred structure of York Minster, seat of the archbishop of York. Built on the site of several previous churches, the oldest dating from A.D. 627, the Cathedral and Metropolitical Church of St. Peter in York (as York Minster is officially known) began as a Norman church around 1100\. An architectural makeover was begun in 1220, and by 1472, York Minster was northern Europe’s largest medieval cathedral.

Religious tensions in subsequent centuries threatened the cathedral. Its treasures were plundered during the English Reformation, and vestments and altars were removed during Elizabeth I’s reign. Major restorations in the 20th century brought back much of York Minster’s original splendor—highlights include the 53-foot-high (16 m) 13th-century Five Sisters windows in the north transept, John Thornton’s Great East Window from 1408, and England’s widest Gothic nave.

One of the best ways to approach York Minster is from the Shambles, a medieval cobbled street with 14th- and 15th-century timber houses overhanging the narrow, winding lane.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_075_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_075_r1.jpg)

The elegant 13th-century Gothic cathedral York Minster in York, England [Credit 75](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img75a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[visityork.org](http://visityork.org), [yorkminster.org](http://yorkminster.org)***

**• HOW TO STAY: York Minster is next to Grays Court *([grayscourtyork.com](http://grayscourtyork.com))*, a beautifully decorated hotel with period furniture. One of Britain’s great railroad hotels, the Royal York Hotel *([phcompany.com/​principal/​york-hotel](http://phcompany.com/principal/york-hotel))* has been restored to its former Victorian glory. Middlethorpe Hall and Spa *([www.middlethorpe.com](http://www.middlethorpe.com))* is a sophisticated 300-year-old country retreat about 1.5 miles (2.5 km) from town.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: York is just over 200 miles (322 km) from London; it’s directly linked to London by rail, a two-hour trip. From the train station in York, it’s just about a half-mile (800 m) walk to the minster.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_076_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_076_r1.jpg)

[Credit 76](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img76a)

CLASSIC SHOT

A**s the Seine River flows past, lovers kiss on the tip of Île de la Cité in Paris. William Albert Allard was the photographer for a *National Geographic* magazine essay that reflected on the Seine as the city’s historic and cultural artery. “Paris, of course, is a very romantic city and a common sight is that of couples kissing or embracing unabashedly, in the midst of crowds,” Allard says. “I often went along the quays of Île de la Cité where many Parisians and tourists gather to enjoy the soft warm light at the end of day. Wandering through Paris is like walking through a series of one-act plays.”**

~ WILLIAM ALBERT ALLARD

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_077_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_077_r1.jpg)

Climbers attempt to ascend “the roof of Europe,” Mont Blanc. [Credit 77](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img77a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

ITALY/FRANCE/SWITZERLAND

**Mont Blanc and Great St. Bernard Pass**

The snowcapped crown jewel of the Alps

T**OWERING MONT BLANC**—“white mountain”—is western Europe’s highest peak, soaring 15,781 feet (4,810 m) above sea level. Dome shaped, glacier gouged, and capped by snow year-round, this Alpine behemoth has entranced mountaineers and thwarted travelers for centuries. The mountain falls on the boundaries of three countries—France, Italy, and Switzerland.

Long considered insurmountable, Mont Blanc was finally scaled in 1786 by Frenchmen Michel-Gabriel Paccard and Jacques Balmat, a historic event that is often considered the birth of modern mountaineering. But while adventurers were trying to figure out how to summit the mountain, for centuries others simply needed to get *past* it. The legendary route they took was an 8,100-foot (2,469 m) pass at the highest point of passage, between Martigny, Switzerland, and Aosta, Italy: the Great St. Bernard Pass.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_078_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_078_r1.jpg)

The village Bourg-St.-Pierre on Great St. Bernard Pass [Credit 78](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img78a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_079_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_079_r1.jpg)

The resort town of Chamonix, France, near the Alpine crossroads of France, Switzerland, and Italy [Credit 79](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img79a)

It has a history all its own. The 49-mile (79 km) route, used as early as the Bronze Age, was a treacherous undertaking—the pass is snow-free only a few months a year. Those who traveled it faced threats from avalanches, severe weather, and marauding bandits. An Augustine monk, Bernard of Montjoux, established a hospice and monastery at the top of the pass in Switzerland in 1050 (the route is named for him). Sometime between 1660 and 1670, the monks discovered that their large, reddish-brown-and-white watchdogs had an uncanny sense of smell and an excellent sense of direction, and they began using the hardy, loyal “St. Bernard dogs” to help find and rescue distressed travelers.

Much has changed since then. A tunnel opened beneath the pass in 1964, giving motorists the option of driving the route when the sinuous—but spectacular—pass road closes in winter. And emergency personnel today rely on helicopters, not dogs, for rescues. But the hospice remains at the top of the pass, and the monks kept St. Bernard dogs in residence there until 2004\. The friendly giants still come back to visit the monastery in summer.

Today, more than 20,000 climbers a year attempt to summit Mont Blanc, and dedicated skiers flock there for some of the world’s best extreme skiing. But you don’t have to be a fanatical adventurer to enjoy Mont Blanc. There are activities for all, including hiking trails that range from gentle to the multiday Tour du Mont Blanc *around* the mountain. Cozy villages sprinkle the mountain’s slopes, including Italy’s Courmayeur and France’s St.-Gervais-les-Bains. The epicenter of activity, however, is Chamonix, France, a year-round resort that has been serving the mountaineering crowd since the first luxury hotel was built in 1816\. This cobblestone village even hosted the first Winter Olympics in 1924.

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[visit-mont-blanc.com](http://visit-mont-blanc.com), [chamonix.com](http://chamonix.com)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Chamonix is the most popular base from which to explore Mont Blanc, with a range of accommodations including Le Hameau Albert 1er *([hameaualbert.fr/​en](http://hameaualbert.fr/en))*, the premier luxe four-season retreat. Or stay in the monks’ hostel at the apex of Great St. Bernard Pass *([gsbernard.com](http://gsbernard.com))*, with dorm-style rooms and communal dining; in winter, the only way in is by snowshoes or skis.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: There are four major airports serving Mont Blanc: On the French/Swiss side are Geneva, Lyon, Grenoble, and Chambéry. For the Italian side, fly into Milan or Turin.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_080_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_080_r1.jpg)

Barry—a prize 150-pound (68 kg) St. Bernard dog—stands to greet Father Formaz, who is on skis. [Credit 80](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img80a)

Of Monks and St. Bernard Dogs

*N****ational Geographic* writer George Pickow attempted the grueling eight-hour climb over the Great St. Bernard Pass to the hospice of St. Bernard in 1956\. The elevation and freezing cold nearly did him in. “I gasped for breath. I thought I could not possibly go on,” Pickow wrote. “Then, mercifully, an enormous St. Bernard dog bounded up to me. He wore no cask of brandy, but after him came a monk carrying a canteen of hot tea mixed with wine.”**

**Aided to the hospice, Pickow met the rest of the friendly, dependable dogs housed there to help stranded travelers. For two centuries, their keen noses had pinpointed people buried in snow, their deep barks signaling salvation to those in distress on the pass. The dogs never carried the fabled kegs of brandy, but they dug out victims and warmed them with their great bulk. “Several times they have pulled one of us out of the path of an avalanche before we could hear it coming,” said Father Quaglia, the hospice prior.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_081_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_081_r1.jpg)

Cells are exposed beneath the partially rebuilt floor of the Colosseum. [Credit 81](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img81a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

ITALY

**Rome and the Colosseum**

Vestiges of the great Roman Empire

T**HE COLOSSEUM** is one of the world’s most instantly identifiable edifices; a symbol of the Roman Empire at the peak of its power and—some would say—its decadence. The ancient stone amphitheater, commissioned by the Roman emperor Vespasian and built in the first century A.D., is certainly colossal, but that’s not how it got its name. The public auditorium was constructed on the site of Nero’s lavish private palace (the Golden House) in part to efface that emperor’s legacy of excess. But ironically, the Colosseum is thought to have picked up its name from Nero anyway—specifically, from its then proximity to the Colossus of Nero.

The setting of the arena is magnificent. It abuts the Forum, where visitors walk the same streets once trod by Caesars and senators. It’s hard to overestimate the influence these ancient sites had on Western culture.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_082_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_082_r1.jpg)

Only ruins are left of the Imperial Palace in the Roman Forum. [Credit 82](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img82a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *It is the most beautiful of ruins; there breathes all the majesty of ancient Rome.*
~ STENDHAL, 19TH-CENTURY FRENCH WRITER

While the Colosseum, officially called the Flavian Amphitheater, is impressive at first sight, one needs to visit to fully appreciate it. The stadium, which seated 50,000, probably feels vaguely familiar. Modern arenas still borrow from the design. The tiers along the perimeter once held rows of seats, and the now exposed cells beneath the partially rebuilt floor held wild animals and gladiators. Both were brought in front of screaming crowds to fight, often to the death.

A Colosseum ticket also provides entrance to the adjacent Forum, the civic, political, and social center of the Roman Empire. The Forum at its prime teemed with lawyers, slaves, and elected leaders. Many would have been heading to the Curia Julia, or Senate house. The brick building was later converted to a church, which is why it still stands today. Nearby is where Julius Caesar was cremated after his assassination.

In the middle of the Forum stands the house of the Vestal Virgins and the Temple of Vesta. Here lived revered priestesses, who were buried alive if they broke their vow of chastity. Their most important duty was tending a sacred fire, which was believed to protect the city.

The 75-foot-tall (23 m) Arch of Septimius Severus honors an emperor who ruled during the second and third centuries A.D. Even in ruins, the arch is impressive: It must have had a profound effect on conquered foes who were hauled back from the battlefield to be paraded down these streets after another Roman legion victory.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_083_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_083_r1.jpg)

Eight upright columns remain from the fourth-century A.D. Temple of Saturn in the Roman Forum. [Credit 83](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img83a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[coopculture.it](http://coopculture.it)***

**• HOW TO STAY: For five-star amenities and a Colosseum view, try the Palazzo Manfredi *([palazzomanfredi.com](http://palazzomanfredi.com))*, a former palace. Rooms are smallish but views are great from the Mercure Roma Centro Colosseo *([mercure.com](http://mercure.com))*, a few minutes’ walk from the monument. Also convenient is Hotel Colosseum *([hotelcolosseum.com](http://hotelcolosseum.com))*, on one of Rome’s seven hills.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Take the Metro B Line to the Colosseo stop, across the street from the landmark. To skip lines, visitors can buy a Roma Pass online *([romapass.it](http://romapass.it))*, or from an authorized vendor. National Geographic also offers trips to Italy *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_084_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_084_r1.jpg)

Tourists in Rome in the 1920s look through the Arch of Titus to the Colosseum beyond. [Credit 84](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img84a)

Romance and Wrath in Ancient Rome

“W**ith the name of Rome there comes to me always a vision of wide-open spaces radiant with blinding sunshine and of great bands and pools of velvety purple shadow,” wrote *National Geographic* writer Florence Craig Albrecht in 1922\. In the midst of the modern city with its trolley cars and electric lights, she traced the remains of the past, visiting the Roman Forum, Pantheon, Trevi Fountain, and Arch of Constantine. “The Colosseum flushes a soft rose, then dims, and suddenly all is purple dusk,” she wrote at sunset.**

**It wasn’t all romance. Albrecht also imagined the monuments of the Forum as they must have appeared in their heyday. The Arch of Titus (pictured) was erected to commemorate the capture of Jerusalem by the Roman general Titus in A.D. 70\. Notes the caption, “The acts that made Titus famous in Rome won him the hatred of the Jews, who were marched in chains in the procession of their captors and forced to lay the stones of this arch.”**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_085_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_085_r1.jpg)

[Credit 85](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img85a)

CLASSIC SHOT

T**he ancient Colosseum stands amid the clamor and bustle of traffic, tourists, vendors—even a couple taking a wedding photo. “My intent was to photograph the Colosseum in a way that celebrated the romantic notion of ancient Rome,” says photographer Matt Propert. “But once I arrived, I knew a more interesting portrayal would be the layers of modernity now surrounding it.” He points out the umbrella vendor on the left. “Think of all the different vendors who have sold their wares here over the last 2,000 years. All that history was going through my mind as I photographed the modern iteration of that age-old tradition.”**

~ MATT PROPERT

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_087_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_087_r1.jpg)

Vesuvius, looming over Naples (above), buried Pompeii under volcanic ash; it preserved intricate mosaics from the houses ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c02_r1.xhtml_img87)). [Credit 86](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img86a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_086_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_086_r1.jpg)

[Credit 87](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img87a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

ITALY

**Pompeii**

Everyday Roman life preserved in a buried city

A**UGUST 24, A.D. 79,** dawned like any other day in Pompeii, a bustling provincial city about 150 miles (241 km) south of Rome. But that morning, the nearby volcano Vesuvius erupted, spewing ash and then superheated gas onto the settlement of about 15,000 people.

While most residents fled to safety, the eruption killed more than 1,000 and destroyed the city. Two millennia later, we are the beneficiaries of this natural disaster. The eruption buried Pompeii under layers of volcanic ash that calcified, locking the city in time and obscuring it from humanity until the 18th century, when archaeologists rediscovered the city and began excavating it. Today, there’s no place better to see what life was like during the Roman Empire. Visitors should pick up a map as they enter the city, as buildings and streets regularly close for renovation. Guides are also available for hire.

Two thousand years ago, in the city’s main street, Via dell’Abbondanza, men in togas would have been going about their daily business. Some would have stopped for a quick snack at a street-front restaurant, where holes in the stone counter held cauldrons of soup. More than 100 bars and taverns have been identified in the city—few residents cooked at home because they lived in such packed quarters and small spaces.

Other notable public spaces include the thermae, or baths, which played an important role in daily life, for reasons both hygienic and social. The baths of the Forum had an elaborate double-floor heating system, along with locker rooms and toilets.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *Many disasters have befallen the world, but few have brought posterity so much joy.*
~ JOHANN WOLFGANG VON GOETHE, GERMAN WRITER

The city’s rich and poor lived in close proximity, but then as now, wealth brought privilege. Wandering through the homes shows what money could buy: statues, brightly colored wall frescoes, and, above all, space.

The House of the Faun, which covered an entire city block, has lavish decoration and is a window on how the Roman aristocrat lived. Visitors should also tour the Villa of the Mysteries, where frescoes showing religious rites have been recently restored to their former glory, with reds and greens and human forms again vivid and rich.

And the people who died here? Archaeologists discovered skeletons buried by mud and ash, and made plaster casts of them. They’re now displayed throughout the city, showing ordinary citizens caught in the final moments of flight or terror. It is a poignant record of disaster—prone children, a dog caught in its death throes, men dragging themselves forward blindly, and families huddled in fetal positions.

The casts are a stark reminder that fascinating as Pompeii is today, it is also a mass grave. A visit there demands gravity in recognition of the loss of life.

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[pompeiisites.org](http://pompeiisites.org)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Pompeii can be visited on a long day trip from Rome, but it’s easier to base in Naples or Sorrento. In Naples, Hotel San Francesco al Monte *([sanfrancescoalmonte.it](http://sanfrancescoalmonte.it))* offers pleasant lodging in a former hilltop convent. For a splurge, try Grand Hotel Cocumella *([cocumella.com](http://cocumella.com))* in sunny Sorrento. Or in Pompeii itself, Hotel Forum Pompeii *([hotelforum.it](http://hotelforum.it))* sits near the historic site.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Take the Circumvesuviana commuter rail line, which connects Naples with Sorrento. It’s an easy trip, but watch out for pickpockets. Then disembark at the Pompeii station and stroll past the souvenir vendors to the ruins. National Geographic also offers trips to Italy *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_088_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_088_r1.jpg)

Gardens outside the ruins of the city walls of Pompeii, Italy [Credit 88](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img88a)

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_089_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_089_r1.jpg)

A skeleton from Herculaneum still wore a sword and scabbard (left); the combination lock from a money box (top right); the hull of a boat washed up by the turbulent sea was found nearly intact (bottom right). [Credit 89](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img89a)

Exhuming Herculaneum’s Dead

O**nly a few skeletal remains were discovered during the early years of digging at Herculaneum, leading archaeologists to assume that most townspeople had escaped. In the early 1980s, however, excavations of the buried beachfront exposed dozens of skeletons of incalculable historical value. Many still clutched valuables like rings and coins that they probably grabbed in “a headlong flight for life…at a dead run,” as *National Geographic* editor Joe Judge wrote in 1982.**

**But as the skeletons were unearthed from the mud that had shielded them for more than 1,900 years, they were in danger of deteriorating. National Geographic sent ancient bone expert Dr. Sara Bisel to Herculaneum to help with the excavations. Bone by bone, she extracted more than two dozen skeletons, preserving them with an acrylic-resin solution so they could be studied and reconstructed. Among the finds: a man still armed with his sword and scabbard, and a lady with bony fingers adorned by gold rings.**

Hidden Journeys

ITALY

**Herculaneum**

Vesuvius’s lesser known victim

**HERCULANEUM, WHICH NEIGHBORS POMPEII**, was destroyed in the same volcanic eruption in A.D. 79\. Though in many ways it is the superior archaeological site, it has always been second in renown and visitation.

Instead of ash, Herculaneum was covered in feet of mud, which even better preserved the small port city. And because it was excavated later, much of the artwork and artifacts on display in Herculaneum are original, while many of Pompeii’s treasures were carted off to museums.

The end in this city was just as unexpected and tragic. Several dozen residents sheltered in boathouses but perished in the intense heat. Their skeletons are still huddled together, some even carrying keys, suggesting they hoped to soon go home.

In the Ad Cucumas wine shop, large jugs line the wall and a surviving fresco lists prices for a pitcher of wine. The city was also home to an order of Roman priests who maintained the Collegium of the Augustales. Inside its remains are two frescoes depicting the deeds of Hercules, the divine Greek hero.

The huge gymnasium complex, or palaestra, has an open area surrounded by a portico. The cruciform swimming pool is still buried, but a deep, rectangular pool is visible.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_090_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_090_r1.jpg)

The ruins of Herculaneum are even better preserved than those in Pompeii. [Credit 90](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img90a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[pompeiisites.org](http://pompeiisites.org)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Herculaneum is located in the middle of a modern city, Ercolano. While it’s an easy trip there from Naples or Sorrento, staying in Ercolano will put you just steps from the ruins. Try Hotel Herculaneum *([hotelherculaneum.com](http://www.hotelherculaneum.com/en/))*, with rooms overlooking the site, or Miglio d’Oro Park Hotel *([migliodoroparkhotel.it](http://migliodoroparkhotel.it))*, in an 18th-century villa.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: From Naples, take the Circumvesuviana rail commuter line toward Sorrento, and get off at the first Herculaneum stop, Ercolano Scavi. It’s about a 10-minute walk to the ruins.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_091_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_091_r1.jpg)

[Credit 91](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img91a)

CLASSIC SHOT

A **performance of *Thesmophoriazusae (Festival of the Women)* by Aristophanes is staged in Pompeii’s Great Theatre, constructed in the second century B.C. Photographer David Hiser caught this image while on assignment for a National Geographic book. “This was an especially rewarding assignment for me because I was able to stay for several weeks in a hotel just outside the gates and walk to work every morning,” he says. The historic setting had personal meaning for him, too: “Almost 20 years earlier, in my vagabond years, I had slept surreptitiously in the ruins while hitchhiking through Italy.”**

~ DAVID HISER

CZECHIA (CZECH REPUBLIC)

**Old Prague**

The ancient, eclectic capital of historic Bohemia

V**ISITORS OFTEN USE WORDS** like “fairy tale” and “romantic” to describe the “city of a hundred spires,” and they’re absolutely right. This city on the Vltava River, with impressive Gothic and baroque architecture, is captivating. Old Town Square hasn’t changed much since the 15th century; two of its most famous landmarks are a distinctive astronomical clock mounted on the 14th-century Old Town Hall and the Gothic, two-spired Church of Our Lady Before Týn.

A maze of cobbled lanes meander past ancient burgher houses, and the beautiful 14th-century Charles Bridge is lined with baroque religious statues. Prague Castle is a huge medieval fortress set high on a hill; one of its wings is owned by a royal family whose lineage dates back 700 years. The Lobkowicz family was once one of the most powerful in Central Europe, but it was forced to flee Czechoslovakia in 1948 under the Communists. Since the fall of the Iron Curtain, it has had many of its royal holdings restored.

There are modern flourishes in Prague, too. In Lucerna Palace off Wenceslas Square, a suspended statue features St. Wenceslas riding an upside-down and apparently dead horse. It’s one of local artist David Černý’s controversial public sculptures. There’s also Frank Gehry’s Dancing “Fred and Ginger” House, an arresting deconstructivist building with dynamic, curvy lines that evoke the iconic dancing Hollywood duo. Elsewhere, you’ll stumble on art deco, art nouveau, and cubist buildings—modern structures that retain a grace of their own amid their historic settings.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *Above all the ancient magnificence and beauty of the incomparable city of Prague itself made an ineradicable imprint on my mind.*
~ RICHARD WAGNER, GERMAN COMPOSER

Prague has been a center of progressive ideas since Charles University in Prague was established in 1348\. It is credited with developing much of the thought behind the Protestant Reformation. Artists and scholars have long been drawn here, Mozart, Dvořák, Kafka, and Einstein among them. And it’s here that a student protest on November 17, 1989, against communism led to the election of popular playwright Václav Havel as president that December 29.

The city continues to reinvent itself. Since emerging from its communist regime in 1989, Prague has blossomed into one of the world’s trendiest destinations, with hip bars, world-class restaurants, innovative music, and contemporary architecture among its attractions. That’s the allure of Prague: It retains a magnificent Old World dignity and charm while making room for a lively strain of modern expression. This is Bohemia, after all.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_094_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_094_r1.jpg)

Old meets new at the Black Angels bar on Prague’s Old Town Square. [Credit 94](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img94a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[prague.eu](http://prague.eu), [czechtourism.com](http://czechtourism.com)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Buddhas and moody lighting reign at the hip Buddha-Bar Hotel Prague *([buddhabarhotelprague.com](http://buddhabarhotelprague.com))*, in an art nouveau building near Old Town Square in Staré Město. In the Malá Strana neighborhood, the Augustine has served as a monastery since the 13th century. The monks have forged a unique partnership with the Rocco Forte Collection, turning some of their historic buildings into a luxury hotel *([augustinehotel.com](http://augustinehotel.com))*. Some rooms have views of Prague Castle.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Václav Havel Airport Prague is 30 minutes from central Prague by bus or taxi. National Geographic offers trips to Czechia *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_095_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_095_r1.jpg)

The 30-year anniversary of the Communist takeover of Prague was commemorated in 1978 in the Old Town Square. [Credit 95](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img95a)

A February Chill on a Lively City

M**ost travelers would choose to tour Prague in the spring, but *National Geographic* writer Peter T. White saw a different side of the city in the winter of 1978\. “Muted reds of roof tiles, the soft yellows of great vistas, the crooked lanes seeming to beckon and whisper—all bore a wintry touch of gray that deepened the mood of mystery, compelling reflection beyond what meets the eyes,” he wrote. He found a city still fueled by coal and lit by gas lamps, the facades of its medieval buildings darkened by soot—but within, lively residents who were passionate about drinking beer, watching hockey, and dancing waltzes and mazurkas.**

**Of course, White wrote, “politics permeates everything”—especially with memories of the 1968 Prague Spring uprising still fresh. In February 1978, he witnessed the 30-year commemoration of the Communist Party takeover of Czechoslovakia. Thousands thronged the Old Town Square beneath bright red banners and enormous portraits of Lenin and Marx.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_096_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_096_r1.jpg)

The Acropolis of Athens ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c02_r1.xhtml_img97)) is home to temple ruins from the fifth century B.C. that still crown the city (above). [Credit 96](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img96a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_097_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_097_r1.jpg)

[Credit 97](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img97a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

GREECE

**Acropolis and Agora, Athens**

Hilltop temples preserve the grandeur of ancient Greece

The Acropolis is a deeply resonant place for Western civilization. The Athens landmark is the site of the famous temple complex built by an empire that invented democracy and created a mythology still famous today. Below it, the Agora—the ancient marketplace—preserves the ground where some of the greatest thinkers in human history developed their philosophies. All together, it’s hard to overstate the importance of this patch of Mediterranean soil.

We can thank the Greek leader Pericles for the collection of monuments on the Acropolis, which he had constructed during Athens’s golden age in the fifth century B.C. Then, as now, the first building visitors to the Acropolis encountered was the Propylaea, the grand entrance gate. Just to the right stands the graceful Temple of Athena Nike. The building looks better now than it has in millennia. It was recently disassembled, cleaned, and rebuilt using many of the original stones. But the friezes are copies; the originals are housed in the new Acropolis Museum at the base of the hill. The museum also displays the caryatids, statues of maidens that once supported a porch on the Erechtheion, another Acropolis temple. The statues now adorning the building itself are replicas.

The Acropolis’s crowning monument is the perfectly proportioned Parthenon, which honors Athena and shows off the skills of its ancient architects. The center of the building’s base is slightly higher than the ends; this counters the optical illusion that makes long buildings seem to sag in the middle. It’s one of several structural innovations that make this former temple a masterpiece.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_098_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_098_r1.jpg)

The spacious colonnades of the reconstructed Stoa of Attalos in the Agora [Credit 98](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img98a)

Many of the Parthenon’s friezes and sculptures have been removed over the centuries. The most famed—and controversial—of the missing sculptures are the Elgin Marbles, so named for the British lord (Thomas Bruce, seventh Earl of Elgin) who coveted and whisked them away from Greece in the early 19th century. The sculptures now reside in London’s British Museum.

Beneath the Acropolis, the Greek Agora was the ancient downtown shopping and civic center. While much of it is rubble, visitors should seek out the Stoa of Attalos, a faithful reproduction of what was once a central market. The most impressive surviving structure, the Temple of Hephaestus, still has friezes that show scenes from Greek mythology.

But more than architecture, the Agora’s legacy is the people who once walked its stones. It was here that Socrates discussed ideas with his disciple Plato, creating the foundation for Western philosophy. Later, St. Paul came to preach about Christianity. Athens is indeed sacred ground.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *What you leave behind is not what is engraved in stone monuments, but what is woven into the lives of others.*
~ PERICLES, BUILDER OF THE PARTHENON

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[odysseus.culture.gr/​h/3/​eh355.jsp?obj_id=2384](http://odysseus.culture.gr/h/3/eh355.jsp?obj_id=2384), [theacropolismuseum.gr/​en](http://theacropolismuseum.gr/en)***

**• HOW TO STAY: A short walk from the Acropolis is the King George Hotel *([kinggeorgeathens.com](http://kinggeorgeathens.com))*, a 1930s-era boutique hotel. Located near the nightlife of the Plaka, along with the monuments and Acropolis Museum, the Athens Gate Hotel’s location is hard to beat *([athensgate.gr](http://athensgate.gr)).* While rooms are modest, views at the Central Hotel *([centralhotel.gr](http://centralhotel.gr/))* are top-rate, particularly from the rooftop bar.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Athens has a major international airport, connected by Metro line three and suburban rail with the city. You can transfer to Metro line two, the red line, to the Akropoli station, and then walk up the hill to the Acropolis. National Geographic also offers trips to Greece *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_099_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_099_r1.jpg)

The octagonal Tower of the Winds sits beneath the Acropolis in Athens, the ruins of the Agora to its right. [Credit 99](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img99a)

Tower of the Winds

A**t the foot of the Acropolis, amid the ruins of the Roman Agora (adjacent to the Greek Agora), lies the Tower of the Winds, an eight-sided marble tower built around 50 B.C. Scholars believe it housed an intricate water clock for Athenian merchants, but they couldn’t figure out how it worked.**

**Supported by a National Geographic research grant, Yale University professor and historian Derek J. de Solla Price set out to solve the mystery in 1966\. Using buckets, brushes, and a potato peeler, his team painstakingly uncovered grooves and channels in the tower floor. Combing the ruins of the Roman Agora, they discovered fragments of the clock’s structure that perfectly fit the grooves. They made a compelling case for an ingenious clock that drew water from a spring high up on the Acropolis and slowly turned an astronomical sundial with chains and pulleys.**

**The Athens antiquities department completed a restoration project in August 2016, reopening the tower to the public for the first time in nearly 200 years.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_100_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_100_r1.jpg)

[Credit 100](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img100a)

CLASSIC SHOT

T**he Acropolis rises behind Athens’s bustling Monastiraki Square. “I wanted to capture the feeling of being there, beneath the shadow of the Acropolis,” says photographer Lola Akinmade Åkerström. But a high afternoon sun and harsh shadows created a challenge for her. “I used the natural spotlight and dark shadows to set the scene,” she says. “You’ll notice there is a natural spotlight toward the left where key landmarks are highlighted, yet still relegated to the background against the hustle and bustle of the square. I did this by standing in the right position to put those landmarks in that lit-up position.”**

~ LOLA AKINMADE ÅKERSTRÖM

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_101_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_101_r1.jpg)

The main staircase at the Winter Palace in St. Petersburg [Credit 101](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img101a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_102_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_102_r1.jpg)

The turreted Cathedral of Sts. Peter and Paul at Peterhof [Credit 102](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img102a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

RUSSIA

**St. Petersburg Palaces**

Opulence fit for a tsar in the Russian imperial capital

T**HE AMBER ROOM** at Catherine Palace, just outside St. Petersburg, glitters with amber panels containing more than six tons (5.5 metric tons) of precious stone. Gilded friezes, mirrored pilasters, and Florentine mosaics add to the lavishness. This single shimmering room, installed in 1755, epitomizes the wealth that made the royal Russian capital of St. Petersburg one of the world’s most beautiful, rivaling the grandest European cities.

St. Petersburg was founded in 1703, and the tradition of an imperial way of life—and the raising of dozens upon dozens of palaces built by the best European architects—started soon after. One of the most luxurious of these is the Winter Palace, in the heart of St. Petersburg. Built on the site of Peter the Great’s Dutch-style wooden house of 1708, this mint green, white, and gold confection was the official residence of the Russian tsars until 1917\. Today, it houses the world-famous State Hermitage Museum. A quick glance at its state rooms will give you a sense of how the imperials lived, with beautiful parquet floors underfoot and ornate cornices and frescoed ceilings overhead.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_103_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_103_r1.jpg)

The 18th-century Catherine Palace was one of the summer residences of the Russian royal family. [Credit 103](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img103a)

Peter the Great also established a royal retreat on the Gulf of Finland, 18 miles (29 km) west of the city. That single modest residence would be expanded over the centuries to become the venerable Peterhof, a complex of palatial buildings and gardens often referred to as the Russian Versailles.

Nearby, the rococo, turquoise-and-white Catherine Palace in Pushkin (also known as Tsarskoye Selo), completed for Empress Elizabeth in 1756, is one of the 18th century’s most glorious works of art and architecture. Its gilded exterior is decorated with more than 220 pounds (100 kg) of gold. Inside, one room surpasses the next in sumptuousness, with the Amber Room the most decadent of all.

And the list of glittering palaces goes on: Yusupov, Yelagin, Alexander, Stroganov…

What’s not immediately apparent as you make the palace rounds is that World War II devastated St. Petersburg. Peterhof and Catherine Palace and many other suburban palaces were left in ruins. The beautiful buildings you see today were meticulously re-created down to the tiniest details (including the panels of the Amber Room—the originals were whisked away by the Nazis; their whereabouts are still a great mystery today). As such, the palaces are not only a testament to the lavish lifestyles of the imperials, they’re a credit to the modern artisans (and abundant government and private funds) that were dedicated to mastering nearly forgotten crafts so that a former way of life could be brought back for the benefit of the modern world.

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[saint-petersburg.com](http://saint-petersburg.com), [hermitagemuseum.org](http://hermitagemuseum.org)***

**• HOW TO STAY: The Belmond Grand Hotel Europe *([belmond.com](http://belmond.com))* on Nevsky Prospekt is a breathtaking art nouveau gem with beautiful historic suites. Another option is the Taleon Imperial Hotel *([taleonimperialhotel.com](http://taleonimperialhotel.com))*, a sumptuously restored palace also on Nevsky Prospekt, with suites decorated in imperial style.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Pulkovo International Airport is 12 miles (19.5 km) south of the city center. There are suburban trains/buses and *marshrutka* minibuses to Peterhof, or go by hydrofoil *([en.citycruises.ru](http://en.citycruises.ru))* from the Hermitage jetty. Catherine Palace is located in Tsarskoye Selo, 15 miles (24 km) south of St. Petersburg via **marshrutka** minibuses or suburban train/bus.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_104_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_104_r1.jpg)

The Great Hall of Catherine Palace (top); reproducing an elaborate ceiling painting whose original was destroyed in World War II (bottom) [Credit 104](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img104a)

Restoring the Russian Palaces

*N****ational Geographic* writer and photographer Steve Raymer visited St. Petersburg in 1992, shortly after the fall of the Soviet Union. He encountered a new Russian aristocracy of entrepreneurs and artists who held lavish parties and were nostalgic for the time when the city ruled supreme as a center of music, ballet, and literature.**

**“The Bolsheviks may have made Moscow the political capital of the Soviet Union, but St. Petersburg remained its cultural rival—a position Petersburgers are resolved to maintain,” Raymer wrote.**

**In the Catherine Palace, he witnessed some of the Soviets’ post–World War II restoration efforts. A four-man team was painstakingly reproducing the palace’s enormous ceiling paintings, such as “Triumph of Russia,” which at 9,000 square feet (836 m²) is the largest ceiling painting in Europe. “I hope to God they are able to finish their work,” said assistant director Nikolai Nagorsky, “because there isn’t another generation of geniuses who know this craft.”**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_105_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_105_r1.jpg)

A lioness surveys her domain in Serengeti National Park, Tanzania. [Credit 105](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img105a)

Trees grow in the ruined Roman amphitheater, which seated roaring crowds of thousands. [Credit 106](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img106a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

TUNISIA

**Carthage**

Remains of the powerful Phoenician trading empire

M**ARBLE COLUMNS JUT SKYWARD** from dusty, crumbled foundations on the western side of the Mediterranean in the North African country of Tunisia. Just north of La Goulette, port of the bustling modern city of Tunis, a complex of chipped tiled porticoes, funerary stelae, and the ruins of an enormous stone bathhouse are all that remain of Carthage, a city of great antiquity and power.

Founded by the seafaring Phoenicians in the eighth century B.C., Carthage was the center of Punic civilization and the wealthiest, most powerful city on the Mediterranean before the rise of Rome. It was at its height for about 400 years beginning in the sixth century B.C., but after three wars with Rome for control of the western Mediterranean, it finally fell in the second century B.C. The Romans destroyed Carthage when they conquered it, so little is known of the ancient Phoenician city, though a few remnants still speak to its might. The Cothon—the ancient Punic port—is marked by an oblong lagoon that sits adjacent to a larger, horseshoe-shaped one. The former basin was once rectangular and was used as the commercial port for merchant ships. The inner round harbor was a naval yard with more than 200 docks that could accommodate an entire fleet of deadly, oar-propelled warships that reduced enemies’ crafts to splinters at sea.

Adjacent to the Punic-era naval harbor, the Sanctuary of Tophet is one of the poignant sites dated to the Punic-era city. The former temple is shaded with palms and surrounded by hundreds of carved stone pedestals. In this area, archaeologists have uncovered the remains of thousands of children scholars believe were sacrificed to honor the Carthaginian gods Baal Hammon and Tanit.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_108_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_108_r1.jpg)

Schoolchildren on a visit to the National Bardo Museum, which contains Carthaginian jewelry and Roman mosaics [Credit 108](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img108a)

When Rome overtook Carthage in 146 B.C., the invaders lit a fire that consumed the city over the course of days. They later built a new Roman Carthage on the site of the ancient Phoenician city. It had villas, schools, porticoes, and all the trappings of a large Roman city, ruins of which still stand today.

The remains of Roman Carthage are scattered over a broad area. The most significant are the Antonine Baths, once a cavernous seaside social center with hot and cold baths, saunas, a swimming pool, and a gymnasium. Today, the basement, a series of archways, and a 50-foot (15 m) column remain; the latter demonstrates the original height of the vaults. Another of Carthage’s large remaining ruins is the Malga Cisterns, once connected to an impressive 80-some miles (129 km) of aqueducts, and used to store and supply water to the city and baths.

Smaller artifacts from Tunisia’s history are on display in the National Bardo Museum in Tunis, which is housed in a 15th-century palace. It has one of the world’s largest collections of Roman mosaics.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *To Carthage I came, where there sang all around me in my ears a cauldron of unholy loves.*
~ ST. AUGUSTINE

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[www.patrimoinedetunisie.com.tn/​eng/​sites/​carthage.php](http://www.patrimoinedetunisie.com.tn/eng/sites/carthage.php), [www.patrimoinedetunisie.com.tn/​eng/​musees/​carthage.php](http://www.patrimoinedetunisie.com.tn/eng/musees/carthage.php)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Just three miles (5 km) from the Roman ruins, the boutique Dar Said Hotel *([darsaid.com.tn/​gb/​index.php](http://darsaid.com.tn/gb/index.php))* has ocean views and a hammam.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: The Tunis Airport is connected to the city by bus and taxi, though many visitors arrive by cruise ship via La Goulette. The ruins of Carthage are connected to Tunis by a light rail line. Get off at the Carthage-Hannibal stop for the Roman sites, or the Carthage-Byrsa stop to explore Punic-era sites on foot.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_109_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_109_r1.jpg)

Archaeologists excavate the ruins of the Phoenician city Sarepta in Lebanon, found under a wheat field in 1970\. [Credit 109](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img109a)

Recovering a Lost Civilization

T**he powerful Phoenicians sailed the Mediterranean and traded with the Greeks, Romans, Persians, and Egyptians for a thousand years. In the 1970s, the National Geographic Society funded grants to explore their ancient world.**

**Researchers uncovered and lifted a complete Phoenician warship near Sicily, likely sunk during the First Punic War in 250 B.C. Archaeologists speculated that it was likely almost new and went down on its maiden voyage, in a storm or in battle. The shipwright’s Punic characters were still visible on the wood.**

**In Lebanon, Dr. James B. Pritchard’s excavations of the lost city of Sarepta unveiled a matchless record of the Phoenicians’ daily life, from the stone basins where they crushed olives for oil to the small temple where they worshipped Baal. “We have uncovered at least nine different cities, representing more than a thousand years of Phoenician history,” Pritchard said. “Every inch, every bit—600,000 fragments of pottery—has been measured and charted and recorded.”**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_110_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_110_r1.jpg)

[Credit 110](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img110a)

CLASSIC SHOT

A **symbol of Carthage’s chief goddess, Tanit, graces a Phoenician mosaic floor from the second century B.C. at the ruins of the port town of Kerkouane, Tunisia. “We were very close to the port of the historical city of Carthage,” says photographer Robert Clark, who was on assignment for a *National Geographic* story about the Phoenicians. “This was amazing, because you can still see the wooden and stone boat ramps.” Despite the sky’s brooding appearance, Clark shot this image under the bright sun of the day, using a strobe over ambient approach to focus attention on the mosaic.**

~ ROBERT CLARK

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_111_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_111_r1.jpg)

EGYPT

**Pyramids of Giza**

Massive tombs of Egyptian pharaohs in the Sahara

T**HE LONE SURVIVOR** of the original Seven Wonders of the World, the Pyramids of Giza have been astonishing visitors since they were first erected in the 26th century B.C. “Forty centuries look down upon you,” Napoleon Bonaparte declared to his invading army gathered at the base of the pyramids in 1798\. At a loss for words to describe his first impressions, 19th-century English novelist W. M. Thackeray jotted three giant exclamation points in his notebook.

Constructed as massive tombs for three 4th dynasty pharaohs—Khufu, Khafre, and Menkaure—the pyramids are awesome for many reasons, foremost their scale.

Largest and oldest of the trio, the Great Pyramid of Khufu looms 451 feet (137 m) above the Sahara. It stood at 481 feet (147 m) before it was stripped of its smooth white limestone casing to construct the palaces and the citadel of Cairo. The pyramid is built of some 2.3 million stone blocks with a total mass of 5.75 million tons (5.2 million metric tons).

Researchers continue to puzzle over how exactly such structures were designed and built, since the civilization that masterminded them had only rudimentary engineering knowledge and did not make use of wheels or pulleys. It must have also been an immense logistical challenge to muster as many as 25,000 men for years at a time for construction duty. But the one thing historians don’t dispute is the architectural sophistication and precise symmetry and stonework of these ancient tombs.

Archaeological excavations in modern times have revealed some of the pyramids’ secrets, like burial tombs hidden deep inside each one. Full-size wooden boats buried in adjacent pits were intended to expedite the pharaoh’s journey to the afterlife.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *As long as earth endures some vestige will remain of the pyramids.*
~ HERMAN MELVILLE, *JOURNAL OF A VISIT TO EUROPE AND THE LEVANT*

Another lingering mystery is the Sphinx, the giant human-lion carved from the native limestone of the Giza Plateau and one of the world’s largest monolithic statues (its paws alone are larger than a bus). Egyptologists generally agree that it was commissioned by Pharaoh Khafre and most likely bears his image. Its exact use has never been determined, though given its orientation to the east, the Sphinx was likely used in sun worship.

Four millennia after the first surge of construction on the Giza Plateau, another structure is rising just 1.2 miles (2 km) from the Great Pyramid of Khufu. When completed (as of this writing, it’s tentatively scheduled to open in 2018), the Grand Egyptian Museum will showcase more than 100,000 ancient Egyptian artifacts including everything on display in the old Egyptian Museum in downtown Cairo. Parks and gardens that evoke the ancient agricultural landscape of the Nile Valley and the temple gardens of Egypt will surround the building.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_113_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_113_r1.jpg)

The Great Sphinx—with paws the size of a bus—has watched the sun rise in the east for more than 4,000 years. [Credit 113](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img113a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[aeraweb.org](http://aeraweb.org), [beta.egypt.travel](http://beta.egypt.travel), [sca-egypt.org/​eng/​SITE_GIZA_MP.htm](http://sca-egypt.org/eng/SITE_GIZA_MP.htm)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Opened in 1886, the historic Mena House Hotel *([menahousehotel.com](http://menahousehotel.com))* lies within walking distance of the pyramids and Sphinx. Just down the road is Le Méridien Pyramids *([lemeridien-pyramids.com](http://lemeridien-pyramids.com))*, and many more hotels overlook the Nile in downtown Cairo.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: The pyramids are around an hour’s drive from downtown Cairo. Visitors are screened at three security checkpoints before entering the archaeological park. Given the desert climate, a hat, sunscreen, drinking water, and sunglasses are highly recommended.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_114_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_114_r1.jpg)

Reporters and gawkers gathered at King Tut’s tomb in 1923 to watch the opening of the royal sepulchre. [Credit 114](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img114a)

Unsealing Tut’s Tomb

E**gypt’s New Kingdom pharaohs hid their tombs in the Valley of the Kings, far up the Nile near Luxor. *National Geographic* writer Maynard Owen Williams witnessed the official opening of King Tutankhamun’s burial chamber on February 18, 1923.**

**Williams watched the pharaoh’s beds, chariot, and boxes of mummified fruit and meat being removed from the antechamber. Reporters clustered above the tomb to see archaeologist Howard Carter, his patron Lord Carnarvon, and the Queen of the Belgians enter the inner tomb with the unopened sarcophagus. “This super heated graveyard…was a silent place,” Williams wrote. “The correspondents spoke in whispers, as though the secrets of the spot would be violated by loud talk.”**

**The next day, Williams was permitted to view the sarcophagus. Though he could only glimpse a corner, he wrote, “The secret eyes looked out reproachfully at one…and a serpent helplessly vibrated his coils at convenient folds up near the top.”**

Hidden Journeys

EGYPT

**Saqqara Pyramid**

An early exercise in pyramid-building

**BUILT ABOUT A CENTURY** before its larger counterparts in Giza, the Step Pyramid of Saqqara is an architectural wonder in its own right, and a profound example of how Egyptian tomb design made a giant leap between the 3rd and 4th dynasties.

Commissioned by Pharaoh Djoser and designed by Imhotep, his chief minister, the tomb was originally intended as a massive mastaba—a flat, rectangular mausoleum in the style typical of previous rulers. A gifted architect and engineer, Imhotep decided to experiment with the design by placing a slightly smaller mastaba on top of the first and so on until the tomb comprised six tiers towering about 200 feet (61 m). Like the Giza Pyramids, it featured a warren of burial chambers.

Djoser’s tomb is considered the world’s first true pyramid. Such was its renown even during ancient times that Imhotep was deified after death and worshipped for many centuries after. His own tomb is most likely in Saqqara.

The archaeology park is entered via the Imhotep Museum and contains the pyramid of the pharaoh Teti, several ancient mastabas, and numerous nonroyal tombs. The Tomb of the Two Brothers—royal manicurists Niankhkhnum and Khnumhotep—is decorated with statues and exquisite wall paintings.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_115_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_115_r1.jpg)

The tomb at Saqqara is considered the world’s first true pyramid. [Credit 115](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img115a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[sca-egypt.org/​eng/​SITE_Saqqara_MP.htm](http://sca-egypt.org/eng/SITE_Saqqara_MP.htm), [beta.egypt.travel](http://beta.egypt.travel)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Most people visit Djoser’s step pyramid as a day trip from Cairo or Giza. However, there are several places to overnight in the Saqqara area, including a modern desert resort called the Sakkara Country Club *([sakkaracountryclub.com](http://sakkaracountryclub.com))* and the modest Blue Lotus Guesthouse *([bluelotusofsakkara.com](http://bluelotusofsakkara.com)).***

**• HOW TO GET THERE: About 20 miles (32 km) south of downtown Cairo, the Saqqara archaeological park is easily reached from the capital by private vehicle, tour bus, or taxi.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_116_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_116_r1.jpg)

[Credit 116](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img116a)

CLASSIC SHOT

T**he Pyramids of Giza tower over the Giza Plateau. Photographer Ken Garrett got this shot while working on a *National Geographic* magazine story, and he remembers marveling at things his lens couldn’t capture. “Every assignment in Egypt is an adventure,” he says. “Learning about the process of cutting and moving the huge limestone blocks. Learning about the lives and conditions of the workers. Learning what they ate and where they slept. Learning about the physical anthropology of the bones, which showed signs of hard labor, fractures, arthritis. Taking these photos is a privilege.”**

~ KEN GARRETT

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_118_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_118_r1.jpg)

Cut from stone, the Bieta Giyorgis (House of St. George, [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c03_r1.xhtml_img118)) and other churches of Lalibela still draw pilgrims (above). [Credit 117](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img117a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_117_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_117_r1.jpg)
