![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

ETHIOPIA

**Lalibela**

Medieval churches hewn from rock

R**ENOWNED FOR ITS** rock-cut churches, Lalibela arose between the 12th and 13th centuries as a “second Jerusalem” for worshippers of the Ethiopian Orthodox Church. More than eight centuries later, the northern Ethiopian city is still a place of pilgrimage and prayer.

Called Roha in its early days, the city was already the capital of the Ethiopian Zagwe kingdom when Gebre Mesqel Lalibela assumed command of the dynasty. A devout Christian, King Lalibela was deeply disturbed by the fall of Jerusalem to the Muslim general Saladin’s troops in 1187. He decided to metaphorically resurrect the holy city in the Ethiopia highlands.

Lalibela’s 11 landmark churches were carved directly into the basaltic scoriaceous stone by skilled artisans, and lie below ground level—they must be reached via precipitous stone stairs or narrow passageways. Exterior decoration is modest, but the church interiors are richly decorated with religious murals, icons, satin curtains, thick woolen carpets, and golden crosses.

Bieta Giyorgis (House of St. George) is the most renowned and probably the nation’s most photographed landmark. Carved in the shape of a Greek cross with a courtyard all around it, the church is illuminated by doors and windows also carved out of the stone. Bieta Medhane Alem (House of the Savior of the World) is the largest church, with a cavernous interior divided by five aisles and more than 35 massive pillars. Dedicated to the Virgin Mary, Bieta Maryam is one of the most ornamented, its stone columns and ceiling covered in geometric patterns and stars of David.

Hermit caves and graves are also chiseled out of the stone walls. And the countryside all around Lalibela is speckled with more rock-hewn churches, most notably the mountaintop Asheton Maryam monastery.

Mystery continues to enshroud construction of the churches. Some archaeologists believe that such marvels of architecture and engineering couldn’t have been created merely during King Lalibela’s lifetime. They theorize that construction continued for centuries after the monarch’s death or that some of the structures were originally built before his reign as palaces or forts. In general, scholars agree that the city was constructed in several phases between the 7th and 13th centuries.

All of the churches remain active places of worship today, with daily rites and rituals, visiting pilgrims, and resident priests. Thousands of pilgrims swarm the town and churches of Lalibela during Ethiopian Orthodox holidays, especially Gena, Meskel, and Timket when the holy books and golden crosses normally safeguarded in the Holy of Holies (inner sanctuaries) are on display.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_119_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_119_r1.jpg)

The three-day Ethiopian Orthodox holiday of Timket at Lalibela celebrates the baptism of Jesus with singing, dancing, and swimming. [Credit 119](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img119a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[ethiopia.travel](http://ethiopia.travel)***

**• HOW TO STAY: The Mountain View *([mountainview-hotel.com](http://mountainview-hotel.com))* is one of the best of the dozen or so hotels that cater to tourists rather than pilgrims. Hotels can arrange licensed local guides to take visitors around the churches. Visiting during a religious holiday is exciting, but those more interested in studying the rock-hewn architecture are advised to visit at times when the churches are not jam-packed.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Lalibela lies 419 road miles (674 km) north of Addis Ababa, but the town and its rock-cut churches can also be reached via a one-hour domestic flight from the capital. Three-wheeled *bajaj* taxis make it easy to get around town.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_120_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_120_r1.jpg)

Guides led a *National Geographic* author up a perilous rock chimney to a chain to climb to Abba Salama Monastery. [Credit 120](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img120a)

A Harrowing Pilgrimage

H**undreds of rock-carved churches are hidden in Ethiopia’s craggy northern highlands. *National Geographic* writer Georg Gerster spent much of the 1960s searching for these medieval stone shrines where Ethiopians still worship, many of them not listed in any records. He tracked down majestic Abba Yohani in Tigray, crowned by 10 cupolas, and tiny Makina Madhane Alam, framed by a massive grotto.**

**Abba Salama Monastery, however, was Gerster’s greatest prize. Its founder, Frumentius, brought Christianity to Ethiopia, and carved his church deep in the mountains atop a cliff, reachable only by “treading an infinitesimally narrow ledge above a dizzying drop, and scrabbling up and down smooth rock walls by hand- and toehold,” Gerster wrote. “When, shaking from the terrifying ascent, I gained the top of the cliff, I stood, I am convinced in my heart, where the man who began Ethiopia’s conversion to Christianity in the fourth century had spent his closing years.”**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_121_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_121_r1.jpg)

Within the Olduvai Gorge (above and [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c03_r1.xhtml_img122)), ash from volcanic eruptions preserved human footprints for millions of years. [Credit 121](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img121a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

TANZANIA

**Laetoli Footprint Trail**

3.6-million-year-old footprints

I**N HOT AND SHRUBBY** Olduvai Gorge in northern Tanzania, water and geologic activity wore away sediment built up over millions of years to reveal stunning clues about the origins of humanity. In 1978, at the archaeological site of Laetoli, archaeologists turned up stone tools, bone fragments, and the fossilized footprints of *Australopithecus afarensis*, our earliest known upright walking ancestors. About 70 indentations forming a trail 88 feet (27 m) long are a remarkable record of a walk these early humans took some 3.6 million years ago.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_122_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_122_r1.jpg)

[Credit 122](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img122a)

The Laetoli footprint trail rocked the scientific world when they were discovered. Paleoanthropologist Mary Leakey and her crew were uncovering the tiny forked toes of birds and the hops of small mammals when they discovered the hominid tracks. Team member Paul Abell stumbled across them entirely by accident; his find was as fortuitous as the unique conditions that sealed the prints in place for millions of years. Rains following a volcanic eruption created a thick, ashy paste those early humans walked over. Later, another eruption covered their prints with more sediment, preserving them for eons until erosion finally uncovered them.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_123_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_123_r1.jpg)

Casts of the Laetoli footprints bear witness to early humans on Earth 3.6 million years ago. [Credit 123](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img123a)

Since their discovery, the Laetoli footprints have been scrutinized and analyzed for invaluable details about *A. afarensis*, the same species as famed skeleton “Lucy,” and the only known hominin in the region at that time.

And what’s in a footprint? As it turns out, a lot. The gait of the hominids was shorter than a modern human’s, suggesting that while *A. afarensis* had a similar stride (rocking from heel to toe), it also had shorter legs and was slower moving. The presence of a forward-facing big toe in the print indicated these walkers may have spent more time on the ground than in trees, since a divergent toe would indicate a creature better suited to climbing and grabbing branches. Some scientists think the tracks could have been made by a small social group or family: A larger male walking with a smaller female, whose prints are more deeply indented on one side, perhaps from the weight of carrying a child on her hip. Others think the prints were made by four different individuals at different times.

Though visitors to Olduvai Gorge today won’t see the original prints—they’ve been carefully cataloged and reburied for posterity—it is possible to take guided tours of area archaeological sites, touch a cast of the footprints at the on-site museum founded by the Leakeys, and experience the electric sensation of walking across the same terrain as some of our earliest human ancestors.

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[ngorongorocrater.org/​oldupai.html](http://ngorongorocrater.org/oldupai.html), [olduvai-gorge.org](http://olduvai-gorge.org)***

**• HOW TO STAY: The site and the Olduvai Gorge Museum, run by caretakers from the local Maasai tribe, is often combined with safaris to the Ngorongoro Conservation Area and Serengeti National Park.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Kilimanjaro International Airport in Arusha, 164 miles (264 km) east of Olduvai, is the closest international hub (though most flights route through Dar es Salaam or Nairobi before heading on to smaller Arusha). Many safaris include airport transfers. For a dedicated trip to Olduvai, you’ll need a car, and for anything beyond the museum, you’ll need to hire a guide. National Geographic also offers trips to Tanzania *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_124_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_124_r1.jpg)

Mary Leakey, with her pet monkey Simon and Dalmatian guard dogs, hunts for fossils in Olduvai Gorge. [Credit 124](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img124a)

Combing the Olduvai Gorge

“T**he simple evidence of the footprints, so very much like our own, indicates to me that the Laetoli hominid stands in the direct line of man’s ancestry,” wrote Mary Leakey in 1979 on her team’s astonishing discovery. Leakey first visited the Laetoli beds in 1935 with her husband, Louis, but found only fragmented fossils. In the 1970s, National Geographic support enabled her to go back and probe deeper—and her team uncovered the remarkable 3.6-million-year-old footprints that proved hominids had walked erect a half million years earlier than previously believed.**

**By then, the Leakeys had spent decades in East Africa searching for evidence of the earliest man. In this photo from a 1965 *National Geographic* article, Mary searches for fossils in Olduvai Gorge. Her team in Africa was composed of more than archaeologists. “Dalmatians,” the article notes, “warn of leopards, lions, and other dangers.” Look closely and you’ll see Mary also has a pet Sykes’ monkey perched on her shoulder.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_125_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_125_r1.jpg)

Zebras are part of the region’s annual Great Migration. [Credit 125](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img125a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_126_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_126_r1.jpg)

A hippo with gaping jaws in the Serengeti [Credit 126](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img126a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

TANZANIA

**Serengeti National Park**

Great herds of African game thundering over the plains

O**N THE DRY**, windblown African plains of northern Tanzania, shaggy wildebeests pound across the landscape in huge columns up to 25 miles (40 km) long. Year after year, more than a million animals migrate in a slow-moving, clockwise, 300-mile (483 km) circuit in quest of water and food. Joining the wildebeests in this perennial journey are 400,000 Thomson’s gazelles, 250,000 zebras, and thousands of elands.

This is the Serengeti, Africa’s greatest and most iconic game park, comprising 5,700 square miles (14,760 km²) of wild grassy plains, acacia forest, and volcanic highlands. The greater ecosystem is twice as large, incorporating the vast Ngorongoro Conservation Area and five other reserves. Collectively, this region is home to the world’s Great Migration.

It is from start to finish a dramatic spectacle of mass, movement, noise, instinct, and downright survival. Lions, leopards, hyenas, and other carnivores hungrily anticipate the coming migration. Crocodiles lie in wait in the rivers the traveling herds must cross or drink from. Meantime, the Serengeti’s other wildlife—elephants, giraffes, warthogs, olive baboons, and black rhinos—go more or less about their daily business.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *No one can return from the Serengeti unchanged, for tawny lions will forever prowl our memory and great herds throng our imagination.*
~ GEORGE SCHALLER, AMERICAN CONSERVATIONIST

The trek begins in January or February in the grassy southern plains, where the wildebeests give birth. They move onward in April and May, up through the swampy Western Corridor with its killer crocodiles, and on to the park’s open woodlands and the soda lakes Ndutu and Masek. After spending the dry season in Kenya’s lion-filled Masai Mara, the circuit begins all over again. If it sounds entirely predictable, it’s not—the timing and route change a bit every year depending on rainfall. To watch part of it from an open-air jeep is one of the greatest experiences in the natural world.

Stewart Edward White was the first European to record the Great Migration, in 1913\. The big-game hunter killed dozens of lions in a three-month period, impacting the lion population single-handedly, which prompted the British colonial administration in 1929 to establish a game reserve. These lands became the basis for Serengeti National Park in 1951.

During his travels, White also met the Maasai, a proud, self-sufficient people who had been grazing their livestock in the open plains for more than 200 years. They had given these lands the name Siringet—“the place where the land runs on forever.” The Serengeti. When the park was established, the Maasai were relocated, a move that is still controversial today.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_127_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_127_r1.jpg)

An African elephant waits until a camera-wielding safari group has departed to cross the road. [Credit 127](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img127a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[serengeti.org](http://serengeti.org)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Accommodations range from high-end to budget safari lodges and basic campsites. A member of the National Geographic Unique Lodges of the World collection, Sayari Camp (*[natgeolodges.com](http://natgeolodges.com);* see [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml)) is one of the few lodges permitted in the northern Serengeti.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Fly into Kilimanjaro International Airport. Most safaris begin from the town of Arusha; the 200-mile (322 km) drive from there to the park takes eight hours. Small charter planes fly from Arusha to one of the park’s airstrips. Tour operators should pick visitors up from the airport and organize ground operations. National Geographic also offers trips to Tanzania *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_128_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_128_r1.jpg)

The van Lawicks’ son plays at their research center in Ngorongoro Crater (left); the authors take photos of Egyptian vultures from a Land Rover (top right); vultures brandish rocks to break eggs (bottom right). [Credit 128](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img128a)

Vultures Break Ground (and Eggs)

I**n 1968, Jane Goodall reported her astonishing discovery of vultures using rocks to break open ostrich eggs—a rare example of animals using tools. Goodall and her then husband Hugo van Lawick were on a National Geographic–funded safari in the Serengeti when they spotted small Egyptian vultures clustered around ostrich eggs. To their amazement, the birds were picking up rocks in their beaks.**

**“With excellent aim the bird threw the stone with a forceful downward movement of head and neck. He pecked at the shell, as though feeling for a crack, then picked up the stone again and flung it,” Goodall wrote. “Three minutes later a direct hit cracked the shell, and after a few more throws, the vulture buried his beak in the rich, nutritious yolk as it spilled on the ground.”**

**Goodall created fake eggs to test the vultures’ reactions to size, shape, and color. Larger eggs especially excited the birds; a red egg confused them; and a cube-shaped “egg” got no interest at all.**

Hidden Journeys

TANZANIA

**Selous Game Reserve**

A wild and fragile animal reserve

**SOUTH OF THE SERENGETI**, Selous Game Reserve is far off Tanzania’s northern safari circuit. Few would guess this remote parcel of land—measuring 19,305 square miles (50,000 km²)—is in fact Africa’s largest game reserve. It boasts a third of Tanzania’s savanna elephants, has Africa’s largest number of crocodiles, and one of the largest known hippo populations. The landscape is a realm of dense miombo woodlands, open grasslands, riverine forests, and swamps.

Selous was established during the colonial era as a model for sustainable hunting. Today, safaris are only allowed on about 8 percent of the reserve. Selous is the closest a modern visitor can come to seeing Africa as it was before phototourism became popular.

But sadly, thanks to poachers, populations of elephants have dropped an alarming 90 percent in less than 40 years, to a critical low of 15,000 animals in 2016; rhino populations are in peril as well. Visitors should be aware of the fragile state of this reserve, and the pressure Tanzania is under to save it.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_129_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_129_r1.jpg)

The Rufiji River threads its way through Selous Game Reserve. [Credit 129](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img129a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[selousgamereserve.net](http://selousgamereserve.net)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Lodges are found only in the park’s northern section. The super-luxurious Beho Beho *([behobeho.com](http://behobeho.com))* overlooking the floodplains is one of East Africa’s best lodges, with game often wandering through camp. Siwandu *([selous.com/​siwandu/​camp](http://selous.com/siwandu/camp))* on the banks of Lake Nzerakera has gorgeous tents and views of animals coming to drink.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Most safaris are fly-in packages from Dar es Salaam. There are scheduled charter flights between Dar es Salaam and both Jongomero and Selous.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_130_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_130_r1.jpg)

[Credit 130](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img130a)

CLASSIC SHOT

T**he world’s fastest mammal, caught in a still moment in Serengeti National Park by wildlife photographer Frans Lanting. Cheetahs have been clocked sprinting at remarkable speeds; they can also stop, accelerate, and change direction with great agility, making them formidable hunters. “Cheetahs made gazelles fast, and they in turn made cheetahs even faster—top speeds [of] 60 miles an hour, 0 to 60 in three seconds,” Lanting says. He points out that though they are superathletes, cheetahs exist in far fewer numbers in the Serengeti than lions or hyenas—in part because they compete with those other ferocious predators.**

~ FRANS LANTING

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_132_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_132_r1.jpg)

Victoria Falls, plunging in a sheet more than a mile (1.6 km) wide, can be viewed on foot ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c03_r1.xhtml_img132)), microlight plane (above), train, or boat. [Credit 131](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img131a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_131_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_131_r1.jpg)

[Credit 132](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img132a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

ZAMBIA

**Victoria Falls**

The world’s largest curtain of plummeting water

S**COTTISH EXPLORER** David Livingstone first heard about a massive waterfall in southern Africa four years before he actually saw it. He was intrigued by what the local Kololo referred to as Mosi-oa-Tunya, “the smoke that thunders,” an apt description given that you can see the mist rising from the thundering cataract more than 12 miles (19.5 km) away.

When Livingstone finally came upon the falls in 1855, he described the “most wonderful sight I had witnessed in Africa.” His discovery captured the imagination of Victorian England and catapulted him to explorer fame. But of course, he wasn’t the first human to lay eyes on the falls: The Kololo had long been awed by them, and archaeologists found stone tools and jewelry indicating that early man was living there two million years ago.

Today, the falls, which Livingstone named after Queen Victoria, remain on record as the world’s largest curtain of falling water. Africa’s fourth largest river, the Zambezi, originates about 1,000 miles (1,609 km) to the north. It rounds a bend at the Zambia–Zimbabwe border and plummets off a basalt plateau in one continuous sheet more than a mile wide (1.6 km). The best place to view this natural spectacle is from Knife-Edge Bridge in Zambia’s Mosi-oa-Tunya National Park. Standing there, high above Boiling Pot and taking in the booming, churning intensity below, it’s clear why Livingstone was mesmerized by the scene.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *Scenes so lovely must have been gazed upon by angels in their flight.*
~ DAVID LIVINGSTONE, SCOTTISH EXPLORER

Visitors can also walk across Victoria Falls Bridge to a vantage point high above the Zambezi River just after its plummet. This parabolic arch bridge was built in 1905 as part of Cecil Rhodes’ grand scheme to connect Cape Town and Cairo by railroad—and though the railroad was aborted, it was the impetus behind the luxurious Victoria Falls Hotel. Larger-scale tourism followed in the 1960s, with amenities for travelers sprouting up on both sides of the falls. Microlight flights take visitors high over the landscape. There are gorge swings and helicopter rides and cliff hikes and bungee jumping. The truly adventurous can take a plunge in Devil’s Pool, on the lip of the falls.

The vintage *Royal Livingstone Express* train rumbles through the bush, revealing a stunning perspective of the falls from Victoria Falls Bridge, just as the sun drops behind them. Sipping a sundowner, admiring this famous scene in the heart of Africa, is an ineffable, humbling experience.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_133_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_133_r1.jpg)

The steam train *Royal Livingstone Express* times its departures to reach Victoria Falls at sunset. [Credit 133](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img133a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[zambiatourism.com](http://zambiatourism.com), [zimbabwetourism.net](http://zimbabwetourism.net), [royal-livingstone-express.com](http://royal-livingstone-express.com), [zimparks.org](http://zimparks.org)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Zambia’s safari lodges are known for their hospitality and wildlife. One of the most romantic is Sindabezi *([tongabezi.com/​sindabezi](http://tongabezi.com/sindabezi))*, on a private island in the middle of the Zambezi River. The Royal Livingstone *([royal-livingstone.anantara.com](http://royal-livingstone.anantara.com))*, in the town of Livingstone, offers colonial-style luxe just above the falls.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Tourists visit from either Victoria Falls (Zimbabwe) or Livingstone (Zambia); each town is accessible by road, rail, and air. National Geographic also offers trips to Zambia *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_134_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_134_r1.jpg)

Victoria Falls as seen from Cataract Island in 1911 [Credit 134](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img134a)

Drenched at Danger Point

*N****ational Geographic* writer Louis Livingston Seaman had seen spectacular waterfalls all around the world, from Niagara Falls and Yosemite’s Rainbow Falls, to cascades in the Himalaya, Alaska, and South America. But Victoria Falls, he wrote in 1911, “possesses, in its exquisite setting of emerald and tropic luxuriance, its grottoes and rain forests, its dancing cascades and raging cataracts, its roseate sprays and frolicking rainbows, a diversity of charms surpassing them all.”**

**The incredible torrent of water was at last accessible to tourists via a new branch of the (never completed) Cape to Cairo Railway. The 1905 Victoria Falls Bridge spanned the Zambezi River just below the falls.**

**Seaman’s favorite spot was Danger Point. “So dense here at times is the mass of vapor hurled from the seething cauldron that the sun’s rays can no longer penetrate it, and complete darkness envel-ops one…while the terrific thunder of the Falls drowns all other sounds,” he wrote.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_135_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_135_r1.jpg)

[Credit 135](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img135a)

CLASSIC SHOT

P**hotographer Annie Griffiths flew into Zambia expecting to find Victoria Falls roaring, but the Zambezi River was running low. “I wasn’t sure how to show the power and beauty of the falls,” she says. A local told her about a swimming hole accessible at the top of the falls during the dry season. She found this swimmer standing there, at the very edge. It is a remarkable shot in more ways than one: “Apart from being at the edge of this extraordinary waterfall, it is very unusual for anyone to swim in the Zambezi River because it is full of crocodiles and hippos. However, those animals would never venture this close to the edge, so we could swim safely.”**

~ ANNIE GRIFFITHS

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_136_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_136_r1.jpg)

Bright houses in Cape Town’s Bo-Kaap neighborhood [Credit 136](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img136a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_137_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_137_r1.jpg)

The city sits in the shadow of Table Mountain. [Credit 137](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img137a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

SOUTH AFRICA

**Cape Town**

South Africa’s beautiful, eclectic Mother City

W**HEN DUTCHMAN** Jan van Riebeeck was sent by the Dutch East India Company in 1651 to the Cape of Good Hope, he was under orders to establish a supply post—not a colony—for ships passing around the southern tip of Africa. At first, he did just that. But after tensions developed between his party and the native Khoikhoi, van Riebeeck’s men had to begin farming to produce their own food. They brought slaves from Batavia (now Jakarta), Malaysia, Ceylon, Madagascar, and elsewhere for labor. The settlement grew into four streets of buildings, with a fort the sailors referred to as “Cape Town.” Within a decade, the would-be supply post had become a flourishing colony with a hospital and a mill.

From early on, this society was deeply diverse. The cape’s vernacular architecture, traditional cuisine, music, and fashion all bear the influences of a mélange of cultures and traditions, from the Khoikhoi and San people to the multiethnic slaves to the Dutch and British settlers.

The diversity that makes this city beautiful has also wrenched it over the centuries. There were early wars between the Dutch and Khoikhoi and the Afrikaners and British, and, more recently, apartheid imposed racial segregation on Cape Town. The District Six Museum in Cape Town provides excellent context on the latter.

When apartheid ended in 1994, Cape Town—like the rest of South Africa—descended into an identity crisis. But Capetonians have slowly come to terms with their turbulent past, following Nelson Mandela’s commitment to reconciliation and forgiveness. Cape Town is in the midst of a cultural renaissance, reflected in innovative cuisine (evident on the menu at Luke Dale-Roberts’s Test Kitchen) and the art scene (on display at the V&A Waterfront’s Watershed).

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_138_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_138_r1.jpg)

A combination biker shop/coffeehouse/film production studio in Cape Town reflects the city’s modern energy. [Credit 138](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img138a)

Perhaps the city’s multicultural quality is best demonstrated in the Bo-Kaap, a Muslim neighborhood inhabited by descendants of slaves brought to Cape Town from many other countries and called Cape Malays. Their cuisine blends the best of Indonesian, Indian, and Afrikaner influences. Here, small houses stand shoulder to shoulder, each painted its own cheery hue: lime, sunflower, cherry, royal blue. Dynamic and historic as this neighborhood is, some worry that gentrification is endangering Bo-Kaap’s distinct Cape Malay character.

And those who’d like a striking vantage point on the whole of Cape Town? The exotic, flat-topped Table Mountain sits just south of the city. Travelers can ascend it—and should—with the help of a cable car or their own two feet.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *During the many years of incarceration on Robben Island, we often looked across Table Bay at the magnificent silhouette of Table Mountain…[it] was a beacon of hope.*
~ NELSON MANDELA

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[capetown.travel](http://capetown.travel)***

**• HOW TO STAY: The Belmond Mount Nelson Hotel *([belmond.com](http://belmond.com))* is an elegant grande dame of 1899\. The Cape Heritage Hotel *([capeheritage.co.za](http://capeheritage.co.za))* occupies two restored 18th-century buildings in Heritage Square. Several of National Geographic’s Unique Lodges of the World collection are in South Africa; Grootbos Private Nature Reserve in Western Cape is closest (see [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml)).**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Cape Town is served by Cape Town International Airport, about 12 miles (19.5 km) from the town center; Uber is a popular form of transport to/from the airport, as well as around town. National Geographic also offers trips to South Africa *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_139_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_139_r1.jpg)

Johannes helped extract venom from serpents for antivenom. A puff adder bite once nearly cost him his life. [Credit 139](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img139a)

Handle With Care

A**fter a month-long crossing of the Atlantic Ocean by freighter, *National Geographic* writer W. Robert Moore had an eerie entry to Cape Town during World War II. “We anchored in a full blackout,” he wrote in the magazine in 1942\. “Over a muffled radio we heard news announcing a ship sinking by a raider on the sea path over which we had just come.”**

**Cape Town was a crucial wartime port. With the Mediterranean blocked, the Allies turned to the old sea route around the Cape of Good Hope. Constant ship traffic carried soldiers and munitions to battlegrounds in the Middle East and Asia and brought back raw materials.**

**All Cape Town joined in the war effort—even in the most unusual ways. In this image, a man named Johannes handles a hooded cobra in Port Elizabeth. Johannes helped capture and “milk” poisonous reptiles to extract venom used to make antivenom, which the Army used to treat South African soldiers fighting in the north.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_140_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_140_r1.jpg)

[Credit 140](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img140a)

CLASSIC SHOT

T**his image of a beach surfer awaiting a killer wave near Cape Town was an easy moment—for the photographer. “I only had to stand there with a long lens,” Heather Perry says of the shot. But that came after a week working in challenging conditions. The waters surrounding the Mother City are frigid and home to great white sharks. “On the last day of shooting, I stopped at this local beach to watch the surfers working the break. The wind was up and the water was cold and choppy. But I could feel their drive to be out there, riding the energy of the water and waves,” she says. “They will tolerate any conditions to be in the sea.”**

~ HEATHER PERRY

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_141_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_141_r1.jpg)

New York’s Brooklyn Bridge spans the East River to link Brooklyn and Manhattan. [Credit 141](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img141a)

Twilight falls on Yellowstone National Park. [Credit 142](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img142a)

Bighorn sheep ramble about the park’s rugged terrain. [Credit 143](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img143a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

WYOMING, U.S.A.

**Yellowstone National Park**

Bears, bison, and bubbling springs in the first national park

E**VEN THE ROUGH MEN** who conquered the American West and rode fearlessly over its terrain realized there was something special about Yellowstone Country.

Perhaps it was the geysers and bubbling mud pools, the giant canyon and its double-tiered falls, or the profuse and fascinating wildlife, but in 1872 the U.S. government decided to protect Yellowstone, and President Ulysses S. Grant signed the bill that made it the world’s very first national park.

Sprawling across nearly 3,500 square miles (9,065 km²), Yellowstone is also one of the largest parks in the United States. Most of the reserve is tucked up in the northwest corner of Wyoming, with small sections spilling over into Montana and Idaho. More than four million visitors have flocked to the park annually in its busiest years, though most stick close to the roadways. But a few hardy souls head out into a vast backcountry that remains virtually unchanged from when the park was first created.

Yellowstone’s hydrothermal features are the stuff of legend, a seething ensemble of more than 10,000 geysers, hot springs, mudpots, fumaroles, and travertine terraces. Old Faithful Geyser, one of the globe’s most photographed natural features, shoots a forceful stream of water into the air every 60 to 110 minutes (the visitor center staff maintains statistics and predictions on eruptions). The equally photogenic Grand Prismatic and Emerald Springs are nature’s charming equivalent of tie-dye. These hydrothermal features derive from a still active supervolcano that lurks beneath about a third of the park.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *The wildest geysers in the world, in bright, triumphant bands, are dancing and singing in [Yellowstone] amid thousands of boiling springs.*
~ JOHN MUIR, *OUR NATIONAL PARKS*

Tumbling more than 400 feet (122 m) down double cascades, the Yellowstone River carves a tremendously deep canyon through the middle of the park; a multicolored gorge more than 20 miles (32 km) long. Draining into the river is Yellowstone Lake, the largest high-elevation lake in the lower 48 states, and a haven for boaters, fishermen, and swimmers (those who don’t mind chilly water).

And then there’s the wildlife: Large bison and elk herds, reintroduced wolves, and the formidable grizzly bear live in the park; so do nearly all of North America’s other large predators, including cougars, lynx, coyotes, bobcats, badgers, black bears, and wolverines. In fact, Yellowstone has the nation’s largest concentration of mammals outside of Alaska. More than 300 avian species have been spotted darkening Yellowstone’s skies, and about 150 bird species nest in the park.

Yellowstone is paradise for the outdoor enthusiast, a place to hike, camp, and horseback ride during the warmer seasons, or cross-country ski, snowshoe, and snowmobile during winter.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_144_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_144_r1.jpg)

A visitor to Yellowstone takes in the dramatic hoodoo formations in one corner of the park. [Credit 144](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img144a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• HOW TO STAY: With 12 campgrounds and 9 hotels, the park offers plenty of overnight options including the historic Old Faithful Inn and Mammoth Hot Springs Hotel *([yellowstonenationalparklodges.com](http://yellowstonenationalparklodges.com))*. More accommodation is available in Montana gateway towns like West Yellowstone and Gardiner. Members of the National Geographic Unique Lodges of the World collection are in Big Sky, Montana, and Jackson Hole, Wyoming (see [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml)).**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Those who don’t drive can fly into airports at Jackson Hole or Cody, both in Wyoming, or Bozeman, Montana, and rent a car. Both the Yellowstone National Park Lodges and Yellowstone Association offer in-park transportation and touring options. National Geographic also offers trips to this region *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_145_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_145_r1.jpg)

A grizzly cub, measured and tagged by researcher John Craighead, is released back into Yellowstone National Park. [Credit 145](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img145a)

Catching Grizzlies—Cautiously

G**rizzlies were a fast dwindling species in the contiguous United States by the mid-20th century. Ensuring their survival meant studying their populations and movements—a tricky endeavor given that the bears outweigh people by hundreds of pounds, have claws inches long, and jaws that can crush a skull. National Geographic funded a study to track the grizzlies in 1960, and brothers and biologists Frank and John Craighead chronicled part of “Operation Grizzly” in Yellowstone for the magazine.**

**The Craigheads tranquilized bears, tagged their ears, and released them back into the park to observe at a safe distance—they hoped. One evening, Frank turned to see seven grizzlies approaching at a full gallop. “Heading almost directly for me, they gave the impression of irresistible power, like an onrushing train…I doubt if a horse could cross such terrain, certainly not at this breakneck speed.” Frank watched in awe, and alarm, but luckily the bears diverted their course.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_147_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_147_r1.jpg)

At Mesa Verde, petrogylphs ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c04_r1.xhtml_img147)) decorate the walls of elaborate cliff dwellings like Cliff Palace (above). [Credit 146](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img146a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_146_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_146_r1.jpg)

[Credit 147](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img147a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

COLORADO, U.S.A.

**Mesa Verde National Park**

The cliff-hanging dwellings of a vanished culture

“T**HE FALLING SNOWFLAKES**, sprinkling the piñons, gave it a special kind of solemnity. It was more like sculpture than anything else,” said novelist Willa Cather of the extraordinary sandstone cliff dwellings of Mesa Verde.

This national park sits on part of a plateau that rises 8,530 feet (2,600 m) above the Montezuma Valley, in the high desert country of present-day southwest Colorado. Centuries before the Europeans discovered America, the ancestral Puebloans (formerly called the Anasazi) moved from living on top of the mesa down into alcoves in the canyon walls, beneath overhanging cliffs. Here they built multistoried cliff houses and apartments; sophisticated structures with efficient ventilation systems and round semisubterranean chambers called kivas where they are thought to have worshipped. They cultivated maize, squash, and beans on top of the mesa. And mysteriously, by A.D. 1300, they had abandoned their cliff villages altogether.

During an 1888 snowstorm, cowboys searching for lost cows stumbled upon traces of a remarkably preserved ruin filled with stone tools, beautifully patterned pottery, and other artifacts. They gathered objects in the hopes of selling them to a museum and sought government protection for the ruins, which was initially denied. Finally, in 1906, President Theodore Roosevelt signed the legislation that would create Mesa Verde National Park, the first park to preserve human works in addition to natural landscape.

The Mesa Verde people left behind more than 600 cliff dwellings scattered over 52,074 acres (21,072 ha); an additional 4,300 archaeological sites have been discovered as well. To see several of the dwellings, visitors climb ladders and scramble through tunnels on guided tours. More than 100 people lived in the 150 rooms of Cliff Palace, North America’s largest cliff dwelling, constructed of natural sandstone, wooden beams, and soil-water-and-ash mortar. Balcony House, with 40 rooms, kivas, and plazas, is a beautifully preserved midsize dwelling. Archaeologists believe bright reds, yellows, pinks, browns, and whites once plastered the walls of the cliff dwellings, and some of their ceilings are still charred with wood smoke.

The ancestral Puebloans called Mesa Verde home for more than 700 years, from A.D. 550 to 1300, and they seem to have only built and resided in the cliff dwellings for the last 75 to 100 years. It’s not entirely clear why they finally left the plateau altogether—possibilities include warfare, drought, depletion of their resources, and village spats. But the Mesa Verde people left behind one of America’s premier archaeological wonders and abundant evidence of their ancient society, from which modern Pueblo tribes are believed to have descended.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_148_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_148_r1.jpg)

This reconstructed kiva in the Spruce Tree House complex is accessible by ladder. [Credit 148](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img148a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[visitmesaverde.com](http://visitmesaverde.com), [nps.gov/​meve](http://nps.gov/meve)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Far View Lodge (*[nps.gov/​meve](http://nps.gov/meve)*, closed mid-Oct.–mid-April), the park’s only accommodation, offers fabulous views of Mesa Verde and into three states; it’s the starting point for many guided tours. The park also has one campground, Morefield. Otherwise, stay in Cortez or Durango, both in Colorado.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: The entrance to Mesa Verde National Park is 10 miles (16 km) east of Cortez off U.S. 160 and 35 miles (56 km) west of Durango; begin your visit at the Mesa Verde Visitor and Research Center. Both Cortez and Durango have small airports; Albuquerque, New Mexico, a four-hour drive away, is the nearest major airport.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_149_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_149_r1.jpg)

Mesa Verde has attracted tourists for over a century. These turn-of-the-20th-century visitors packed in on horseback. The image was captured on a glass plate. [Credit 149](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img149a)

Digging Up Wetherill Mesa

I**n 1958, the National Park Service and the National Geographic Society embarked on a project to study the cliff homes of Wetherill Mesa. They wanted to understand why the inhabitants had gone from living on the top of the mesa to building elaborate dwellings in the caves below, before disappearing altogether.**

**The extensive, five-year dig yielded nothing conclusive of the fate of the ancestral Puebloans, but the archaeologists uncovered fascinating details about the people and their lives. From 700-year-old fecal matter, they learned about the people’s diet and parasitic diseases. From tree rings in the timber beams of some of the structures, they were able to get an idea about the weather. And they also made poignant human discoveries: Under the floor of the dwelling called Long House, they discovered the mummified remains of a tiny infant, wrapped lovingly in a feather blanket and with a mummified turkey buried beside her, possibly for her sustenance in the next world.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_150_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_150_r1.jpg)

Arizona’s Wigwam Motel ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c04_r1.xhtml_img151)) and Oklahoma’s Pops Soda Ranch (above), two of the retro stops along old Route 66 [Credit 150](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img150a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_151_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_151_r1.jpg)

[Credit 151](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img151a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

WESTERN U.S.A.

**Route 66**

“America’s Main Street,” emblem of American pop culture—and migration

I**N AN AGE OF** interstates and autobahns and airplanes, it seems quaint that Americans once went streaming down a two-lane highway. But Route 66 was a really big deal when it was created between the two World Wars.

Running over 2,200 miles (3,541 km) between Chicago and Los Angeles, it was the first paved auto route linking the West Coast with the rest of the nation. And while it may not have gotten the drumroll that accompanied the completion of the transcontinental railroad, Route 66 had an equally significant impact on Western development and the national psyche.

Within years of the first Model T Ford rolling off a Michigan assembly line in 1908, motorists were calling for better paved roads. But it wasn’t until the 1920s that the federal government turned in earnest to developing a national highway system. Cyrus Avery, an Oklahoma road advocate who advised the Bureau of Public Roads, was dubbed the “father of Route 66” for his tireless efforts to promote the highway.

What made Route 66 different from previous highways was the fact that it didn’t follow a direct path east to west; instead, it took a diagonal route from the Great Lakes to the Pacific Coast. It linked not only cities but also thousands of small farming communities, expediting transportation between urban areas and helping farmers get their products to market.

The final stretches were paved in 1938\. It had taken more than a decade to finish the entire route across eight states, a period in which the Great Depression and the Dust Bowl had afflicted the nation. Thousands of unemployed citizens were put to work building the highway, and it became a pilgrimage route for people packing up and heading West to seek new opportunities in California. The road became ingrained in American popular culture, from works like John Steinbeck’s classic novel *The Grapes of Wrath* to the Bobby Troup tune “(Get Your Kicks on) Route 66.”

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_152_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_152_r1.jpg)

The landmark Santa Monica Pier in California—western terminus of Route 66—juts into the Pacific. [Credit 152](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img152a)

By 1970, Route 66 had been entirely bypassed by modern interstates. But in recent years, nostalgia has fueled a campaign to save remnants of the old road and preserve its classic American architecture and roadside kitsch.

Oklahoma, where the idea of the cross-country road first took root, preserves some of the longest and best remnants, including a stretch through Arcadia (between Tulsa and Oklahoma City) flanked by the famous Round Barn and Pops restaurant. The excellent Oklahoma Route 66 Museum in Clinton details the history and cultural significance of the highway to the music of the era. In New Mexico, Central Avenue (aka Route 66) through downtown Albuquerque preserves dozens of classic neon signs and art deco architecture from the highway’s heyday.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *Highway 66 is the main migrant road…the long concrete path across the country, waving gently up and down on the map…the mother road, the road of flight.*
~ JOHN STEINBECK, *THE GRAPES OF WRATH*

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[national66.org](http://national66.org), [route66world.com](http://route66world.com), [oklahomaroute66.com](http://oklahomaroute66.com), [rt66nm.org](http://rt66nm.org), [illinoisroute66.org](http://illinoisroute66.org), [azrt66.com](http://azrt66.com), [route66ca.org](http://route66ca.org)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Many hotels, motels, and campsites are available along Route 66\. Among the most nostalgic places to sleep are the Wigwam Motel *([sleepinawigwam.com](http://sleepinawigwam.com))* in Holbrook, Arizona; Boots Court *([bootsmotel.homestead.com](http://bootsmotel.homestead.com))* in Carthage, Missouri; and the Blue Swallow Motel *([blueswallowmotel.com](http://blueswallowmotel.com))* in Tucumcari, New Mexico.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Those who want to drive the entire route should start from the Santa Monica Pier in Los Angeles County or the intersection of Adams Street and Michigan Avenue in Chicago. Riding Route 66 *([ridingroute66.us](http://ridingroute66.us))* is a great resource for guided and self-drive journeys along the highway.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_153_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_153_r1.jpg)

Dust Bowl trucks by a weathered farmhouse near Guymon, Oklahoma. Said Will Rogers, “We are the first nation in the history of the world to go to the poorhouse in an automobile.” [Credit 153](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img153a)

The Hard Times Highway

T**o some, Route 66 may summon images of a carefree highway, or nostalgia for the old American West. It had a much different connotation for the millions of Oklahomans who used it to migrate west during the Dust Bowl and Depression years of the 1930s. In 1984, *National Geographic* writer William Howarth and photographer Chris Johns retraced the dusty route those uprooted homesteaders followed, to see where it had taken them and how they had fared.**

**They found everything from sharecroppers who’d migrated west then come back home after World War II to live frugally again in their home state (“I eat cornbread three times a day,” one told Howarth); to farmers who’d stuck it out through the hard years in Oklahoma and gone on to become prosperous; to Okies who’d followed 66 west and never looked back. Said one, “[I] only went back to Oklahoma once. Things didn’t look right. Old place was gone…Everyone I knowed had gone to California.”**

Hidden Journeys

ALASKA, U.S.A./CANADA

**Alaska Highway**

A wilderness road trip through Canada and Alaska

**RUNNING THROUGH SOME OF** the most rugged terrain on the planet, the Alaska Highway is an epic 1,387-mile (2,232 km) journey through boreal forest, remote mountain ranges, and alluvial landscapes carved by rivers and ancient glaciers.

Constructed during World War II as a strategic link between Alaska and the lower 48 states, the route stretches from Dawson Creek in northeastern British Columbia through Yukon to Delta Junction near Fairbanks, Alaska.

Once gravel, the entire length of the highway is now paved, though stretches can still be potholed and rough. The major obstacle to driving the highway is extreme northern weather—it can snow even at the height of summer this far north. Grizzlies, moose, and other critters are also apt to wander across the road.

Drivers should expect a general lack of amenities. Many businesses are seasonal, and even during the best weather, traffic is extremely light along much of the highway.

The weeklong journey on the Alaska Highway offers one of the world’s most rewarding wilderness road trips. Along the way are Muncho Lake, historic Whitehorse in Yukon, and vast Kluane National Park and Preserve. The best online resource for driving the highway is The Milepost *([themilepost.com](http://themilepost.com)).*

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_154_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_154_r1.jpg)

Kathleen Lake in Yukon, Canada, along the Alaska Highway [Credit 154](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img154a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[northtoalaska.com](http://northtoalaska.com), [travelyukon.com](http://travelyukon.com), [travelalaska.com](http://travelalaska.com), [hellobc.com](http://hellobc.com), [cruiseamerica.com](http://cruiseamerica.com)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Motels, lodges, cabins, and campgrounds are found along the length of the Alaska Highway, but the drive time between roadside services can be hours.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: The Alaska Highway officially runs between Delta Junction, Alaska, and Dawson Creek, British Columbia, but the most popular starting points for driving the route are Fairbanks, Alaska, and Edmonton, Alberta. Airports in both are serviced by flights from many North American cities. Most people drive their own vehicles, but Cruise America offers one-way Alaska Highway RV rentals.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_155_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_155_r1.jpg)

[Credit 155](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img155a)

CLASSIC SHOT

H**ot-air balloons are lined up and ready to launch at the Balloon Fiesta in Albuquerque, New Mexico, a major stop on old Route 66. Says photographer Ralph Lee Hopkins, “In the soft twilight before sunrise, while the balloons are still tethered to ground, the pilots flash their burners in unison and light up the colorful balloons from within.” And though the day is still dim, that’s an opportunity for a photographer. “To our eyes, the scene looks too dark to make an image,” explains Hopkins. “But with the slow shutter and longer exposure, the camera does an amazing job of rendering the scene. Once the sun becomes too bright, it’s game over.”**

~ RALPH LEE HOPKINS

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_156_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_156_r1.jpg)

A view of Niagara Falls seen from Prospect Point [Credit 156](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img156a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

ONTARIO/NEW YORK

**Niagara Falls**

The most famous torrent in the Americas

N**IAGARA FALLS** is so rapid and powerful and loud that it’s hard to fathom even as you stand in front of it. It has lured honeymooners since Napoleon’s brother brought his bride here in 1804\. The famous painter Frederic Edwin Church captured its drama in an enormous 1857 masterpiece that was exhibited to great acclaim in the United States and Europe. And daredevils who just can’t resist a good brush with death have plunged over its edge in a barrel, or tight-roped across the yawning chasm.

When a visitor approaches the railing overlooking the rainbow-hued, blasting torrent of water, nothing can have prepared him for the spectacle.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_157_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_157_r1.jpg)

Old Fort Niagara at the mouth of the Niagara River has a 300-year history. [Credit 157](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img157a)

Niagara Falls straddles the U.S.–Canada border and comprises three falls on the Niagara River: the American Falls and smaller Bridal Veil Falls, on the U.S. side, and Horseshoe Falls, the largest and most famous, on the Canadian side. This trio of cascades originated from an ancient landscape during the last ice age 12,500 years ago. As giant ice sheets melted and formed the Great Lakes, the newly formed Niagara River carved a path through the Niagara Escarpment. Five spillways once flowed between Lakes Erie and Ontario, and over time these were reduced to just one, the original Niagara Falls.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_158_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_158_r1.jpg)

Visitors don ponchos to approach the base of Horseshoe Falls on the *Maid of the Mist* tour boat. [Credit 158](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img158a)

When Franciscan missionary-explorer Louis Hennepin first spotted the falls in 1678, he guessed they were 600 feet high (183 m). They actually measure only 188 feet (57 m), but they give off that impression. Some 3,000 tons (2,722 metric tons) of water spill over the falls every second—enough to fill a million bathtubs a minute. The volume and velocity of the water creates a heavy mist that rests over the falls like a low-lying cloud, and the roar of the cascade can be heard miles away.

Niagara Falls is infamous for its tacky tourist veneer, notably on the Canadian side. But these beautiful falls played an important role in America’s early conservation movement. Environmentalists successfully lobbied for the creation of the Niagara Reservation in 1885 to reclaim the falls from mills and factories. Central Park landscape designer Frederick Law Olmsted designed much of what is now Niagara Falls State Park, a magnificent 400 acres (162 ha) of winding footpaths, wooded areas, and scenic overlooks, with the falls at its heart.

Today, visitors should get away from the crowded overlooks and experience the park and falls up close. The *Maid of the Mist* boat tour has been taking tourists to the base of the falls since 1846. The Niagara Gorge Rim Trail is a nice, easy hike. Or those seeking solitude can simply find a quiet spot and take in the mesmerizing views, as Olmsted intended.

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[niagarafallstourism.com](http://niagarafallstourism.com), [visitniagaracanada.com](http://visitniagaracanada.com)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Most people recommend staying on the Canadian side because there is more to see and do, and the views are better; but be sure to walk across Rainbow Bridge to the more peaceful American side. Greystone Manor B&B *([greystone-manor.ca](http://greystone-manor.ca))* offers four gracious rooms in a 1908 Niagara Falls home.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Niagara Falls is on the U.S.–Canada border, near Niagara Falls, New York, and Niagara Falls, Ontario. Buffalo, New York, is 30 minutes away by car, and Toronto, Ontario, about 1.5 hours. The closest major airport is Buffalo Niagara International. If you cross the U.S.–Canada border, be sure to bring a valid passport.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_159_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_159_r1.jpg)

Niagara Falls frozen to ice in winter. This image accompanied a 1916 article on Niagara’s World War I role. [Credit 159](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img159a)

Niagara Fights World War I

N**iagara Falls is known mostly for being a spectacle of nature, but less known is the fact that it played an essential part in powering the U.S. military during World War I. *National Geographic* magazine published a 1917 article on how the falls contributed to the war effort. Waxed author William Joseph Showalter, “Aided by science, the [United States] has transformed the silvery sheen of its whitened waters into the fateful furies of the artillery duel and the infantry charge.”**

**How? Showalter explained that industrial plants hydro-powered by Niagara Falls produced artificial abrasives that were used to make artillery shells and vehicles that the U.S. shipped over to Europe. Niagara-powered plants also produced steel alloys that made bullets harder and axles stronger, and a silicon metal that helped produce gas to keep hydrogen balloons—used for observation and to locate enemy targets—in the skies over the Western front.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_160_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_160_r1.jpg)

[Credit 160](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img160a)

CLASSIC SHOT

A **woman takes a damp selfie in front of Horseshoe Falls at Niagara Falls. Every photo has a story, and in this case photographer Mike Theiss noticed that the woman had a hard time getting her shot because her phone was wet and she kept fumbling. Theiss was better prepared. “Everything was so wet and cold,” he says. “I put protective housing on my camera because the mist was so thick.” He was there on a personal assignment to cover Niagara from air, land, and sea, and the spray didn’t faze him. A storm and extreme weather photographer, Theiss has shot in far more inclement conditions.**

~ MIKE THEISS

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_161_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_161_r1.jpg)

Upper Old Quebec and Château Frontenac overlook the St. Lawrence River. [Credit 161](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img161a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_162_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_162_r1.jpg)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

QUEBEC, CANADA

**Quebec City**

A walled North American city evoking old and new France

T**HE LAST WALLED** North American city north of Mexico, Quebec City is renowned for its dramatic bluff-top perch overlooking the St. Lawrence River and its distinctive French and British colonial architecture. Split into upper (Haute-Ville) and lower (Basse-Ville) towns, the old quarter is a mosaic of historic churches and fortifications, outdoor cafés and moody bars, and more than a dozen museums and historic sites.

Quebec City was founded in the summer of 1608 when Samuel de Champlain established a small fur trading post along the St. Lawrence at the behest of the French crown. By 1620, Fort St. Louis had been constructed on the tallest bluff, marking the start of several centuries of military construction that would turn Quebec into the “Gibraltar of America.” It also grew into a thriving riverside city that revolved around the plaza Place-Royale and Notre-Dame de Québec Basilica-Cathedral.

The British took control of Quebec City in 1759 following a pitched battle on the Plains of Abraham just outside the city walls. The redcoats bolstered the city’s defenses with the star-shaped Citadelle (the largest British fort in North America), Artillery Park, and Dufferin Terrace atop the old French walls. However, the city’s most distinctive structure didn’t appear until the 1890s, when the Canadian Pacific Railway erected the distinctive fortresslike Château Frontenac hotel on a cliff overlooking the St. Lawrence River.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *The impression made upon the visitor by this Gibraltar of America: its giddy heights; its citadel suspended, as it were, in the air…is at once unique and lasting.*
~ CHARLES DICKENS, *AMERICAN NOTES*

Both the upper and lower levels of the town have preserved their warrens of cobblestone streets, squares, and promenades, and many historic buildings are now occupied by museums, restaurants, hotels, and art galleries. Modern structures—like the Museum of Civilization and the new Pierre Lassonde Pavilion at the National Museum of Fine Arts—are skillfully fit into their historic environs.

The old city walls surround most of Old Quebec City and contain gates, barracks, and guard posts, all built between 1608 and 1871. Visitors can explore the walls and fortifications on their own, or join one of Parks Canada’s guided tours.

Beyond the city walls, modern Quebec beckons, too. The Grande Allée is a worthy restaurant row, and the art deco Palais Montcalm is a small but lively theater venue. Down in the flatlands, the revived St.-Roch neighborhood has hip microbreweries and the chic shops of rue St.-Joseph.

The city also has the 272-foot (83 m) Montmorency Falls, a monstrous gush of water that passes through a black-rock gorge beside the St. Lawrence. A nearby bridge leads to the bucolic Île d’Orléans, with petite wineries and cheese farms.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_163_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_163_r1.jpg)

Footpaths, an aerial tram, and a suspension bridge offer views of Montmorency Falls, a short drive from Old Quebec City. [Credit 163](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img163a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[quebecregion.com/​en](http://quebecregion.com/en), [quebecoriginal.com/​en](http://quebecoriginal.com/en), [ville.quebec.qc.ca/​en/​touristes/​index.aspx](http://ville.quebec.qc.ca/en/touristes/index.aspx)***

**• HOW TO STAY: The Old City is full of atmospheric hotels, from the cliff-top Fairmont Le Château Frontenac *([fairmont.com/​frontenac-quebec](http://fairmont.com/frontenac-quebec))* to Le Germain Hotel Quebec *([legermainhotels.com/​en/​quebec](http://legermainhotels.com/en/quebec))* in an old bank building and Auberge Saint-Antoine *([saint-antoine.com/​en](http://saint-antoine.com/en))* in a historic waterfront warehouse.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Quebec City is a two- to three-hour drive from Montreal and a five- to six-hour road trip from Portland, Maine. The airport offers direct nonstop flights from several East Coast cities. VIA Rail *([viarail.ca](http://viarail.ca))* from Montreal, Toronto, or the Maritime Provinces is another option.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_164_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_164_r1.jpg)

Music at the Château Frontenac during the Winter Carnival (top left); curling (top right); tobogganing down a 1,400-foot (427 m) runway to the château (bottom) [Credit 164](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img164a)

Winter Carnival

I**n winter 1958, *National Geographic* staffer Kathleen Revis visited Quebec City and found a chilly scene: “Great drifts almost clogged side streets…On the St. Lawrence River, 300 feet below, nothing moved but jagged, sullen ice floes. Winter, I thought, has conquered Quebec.” But enter the Winter Carnival, a Quebec City tradition since 1894, that enlivened the bleak winter.**

**Events included curling, costume balls, and ice sculpture-carving. There was also an ice canoe race that tested the skills learned by farmers living on isolated islands in the St. Lawrence. Their only link to the mainland in winter was with canoes with cutter-edged bows that could navigate the solid-ice river. Revis gamely took part in an ice canoe run and—to her dismay—became stranded on a drifting ice floe. Her teammates came to the rescue, but not before her hands were too stiff to operate her camera. “My teeth—and, I think, even my bones—were chattering,” she wrote ruefully.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_165_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_165_r1.jpg)

The Cabot Trail meanders along the Gulf of St. Lawrence. [Credit 165](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img165a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_166_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_166_r1.jpg)

The Fortress of Louisbourg was an early French settlement on Cape Breton. [Credit 166](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img166a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

NOVA SCOTIA, CANADA

**Cape Breton**

Old World charm in the wind-tossed North Atlantic

“O**N THE FIRST DAY** God created Cape Breton,” locals proclaim. “On the second day, he threw rocks at it.”

Though geologists demur, this could go a long way to explaining the wild, remote landscape of this island off Nova Scotia’s northern tip. The Cabot Trail, named for the 15th-century Italian explorer John Cabot, encircles this spectacular, 3,981-square-mile (10,310 km²) jumble of rocks in the North Atlantic. For 186 miles (299 km), the road swoops and rolls and climbs along the edge of vertiginous granite headlands that fall away into piles of boulders far below, washed by the ocean. Along the way are dark forests, fishing villages, lighthouses, highlands, and, inevitably, a moose or two. Much of the island is preserved as a national park, and hiking trails amble through sweet-smelling boreal forest and offer views over shimmering, whale-dotted St. Lawrence Bay.

Cape Breton’s natural beauty alone would render it one of the world’s timeless places. But there’s much more to it than that. On certain evenings in tiny Ingonish on the island’s eastern side, or even tinier Mabou on the west, locals gather in parish halls and pubs to step dance to stirring Celtic tunes with fiddles and bagpipes. These are ceilidhs (KAY-lees), one of many Scottish traditions (whisky distilling, clan tartans, and Gaelic are others) still very much alive on Cape Breton Island. Many of today’s residents descend from 18th-century Scottish immigrants.

On the island’s western side, the town of Chéticamp is home to French Acadians descended from families who migrated here after the British deportation of Acadians from Acadia in the late 18th century. Their ancestral culture comes alive through a French patois (vernacular dialect), a unique late February or March Acadian carnival called Mi-Carême, a rug-hooking museum, and roadside restaurants that whip up traditional food such as *chiard* (grated potato pie) and *fricot* (chicken stew).

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_167_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_167_r1.jpg)

A cow moose in Cape Breton Highlands National Park in Nova Scotia [Credit 167](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img167a)

Elsewhere around the cape, the Mi’kmaq, descendants of the island’s original inhabitants, live in five different communities. They celebrate their ancestors with traditional art, storytelling, music, and handicrafts that have been handed down from one generation to the next. Wagmatcook Culture and Heritage Centre in Wagmatcook provides excellent insight into their customs.

Cape Breton has transfixing natural attractions, but visitors should be sure not to miss the distinctive character of the towns and people. Nestled between Canada’s largest inland sea (Bras d’Or Lake) and the island’s ocean-side cliffs are vital communities that still embody the long-ago cultures that preceded them.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *I have seen the Canadian and American Rockies, the Andes and the Alps and the Highlands of Scotland, but for simple beauty, Cape Breton outrivals them all.*
~ ALEXANDER GRAHAM BELL

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[cabottrail.com](http://cabottrail.com), [novascotia.com](http://novascotia.com), [pc.gc.ca](http://pc.gc.ca)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Historic Keltic Lodge at the Highlands (*[kelticlodge.ca](http://kelticlodge.ca)*, closed mid-Oct.–mid-May) is famous for its golf and for breathtaking views over North and South Bays and Cape Smokey.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Most travelers to Cape Breton arrive via Halifax, Nova Scotia’s capital. If flying into Halifax International Airport, you can rent a car and drive three hours to Cape Breton. Sydney, on the island’s east coast, also has a small airport. National Geographic also offers trips to the Canadian Maritimes *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_168_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_168_r1.jpg)

Alexander Graham Bell built this enormous round kite out of many tetrahedral kites; it could fly several hundred yards high. [Credit 168](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img168a)

A Kite for Mankind

N**ational Geographic’s second president, scientist Alexander Graham Bell, had an estate on Cape Breton Island where he conducted experiments. Writer Catherine Dunlop Mackenzie remarked on the broad range of his research in a 1920 *National Geographic* article. Among his innovations: a high-speed hydroplane boat (it went 70 miles/113 km an hour), an outlook tower composed of stacked tetrahedral cells, and special twin-bearing sheep stock.**

**But perhaps most remarkable, Mackenzie wrote, was Graham’s collection of huge, man-bearing kite structures. Graham was an aviation pioneer, fascinated by the idea of mechanical flight since his boyhood. In 1907, he sent one of his giant kites 168 feet (51 m) aloft over Bras d’Or Lake on Cape Breton Island, with U.S. Army officer Lt. Thomas E. Selfridge attached. Though Selfridge survived that experiment, he died just months later in one of Orville Wright’s flying machines, earning him the grim distinction as modern aviation’s first fatality.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_169_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_169_r1.jpg)

[Credit 169](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img169a)

CLASSIC SHOT

P**hotographer Michael Melford loves to drive when on assignment to explore a region’s lesser known places. “Being on my own, I can move at my own pace, which often means stopping by the side of the road and watching the scene for hours, or going for a hike,” he says. When photographing a story about Cape Breton for *National Geographic Traveler* magazine, he was driving the Cabot Trail when he came across this hidden lake. “I was lucky as this couple went out for a canoe ride,” he says. “There was no wind and the scene reflected nicely on the lake’s surface.”**

~ MICHAEL MELFORD

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_171_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_171_r1.jpg)

Parks are a good way to experience New York Harbor, including Battery Park in lower Manhattan ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c04_r1.xhtml_img171)) and Brooklyn Bridge Park (above). [Credit 170](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img170a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

NEW YORK, U.S.A.

**New York Harbor**

The historic gateway to America

O**NE OF THE WORLD’S** largest natural harbors, New York’s “front porch” has been an object of fascination to the outside world since 1524, when the Italian explorer Giovanni da Verrazano sailed into the body of water and claimed it for France.

The harbor is divided into inner and outer portions on either side of the Verrazano–Narrows Bridge. Brooklyn makes up the eastern shore, Staten Island and New Jersey the west, and at the northern end, the Hudson and East Rivers flow around Manhattan and into the harbor.

These days, the eye is drawn to the immense One World Trade Center skyscraper looming over the lower end of Manhattan, but the Statue of Liberty remains the city’s most iconic landmark. A gift from France to honor the centennial of American independence, Lady Liberty first assumed her prominent stance over New York Harbor in 1886, and she has been an emblem of the free world ever since.

Just north in the Upper Bay is Ellis Island, where more than 12 million immigrants took their first steps on U.S. soil between 1892 and 1954\. Nowadays, the rectangular isle honors the memory of those migrants and helps their descendants find their own roots.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *The magnificent facade of the homeland, the harbor of New York.*
~ F. SCOTT FITZGERALD, *TENDER IS THE NIGHT*

On the opposite side of the Upper Bay, the 172-acre Governors Island was transformed from a longtime military base and Coast Guard station into an eclectic visitor experience that opened to the public in 2003. In addition to historic attractions like Fort Jay and Castle Williams, the island now boasts art exhibits, a food court, biking and hiking paths, and picnic areas with jaw-dropping views of the Manhattan skyline.

The adjacent Brooklyn waterfront has also undergone tremendous change in recent years, the decrepit docks transformed into waterfront parks, gardens, sports facilities, playgrounds, outdoor bars, and shoreline restaurants. During the summer, one of the great lawns becomes a venue for outdoor movies with Manhattan as a backdrop.

One of the best ways to explore the waterway is absolutely free—the Staten Island Ferry. The workhorse vessels were launched in 1817 and carry 70,000 people per day on the 25-minute journey between Manhattan’s Battery Park and Staten Island’s St. George Terminal.

New York Harbor is still a hardworking port that handles more than 2,200 cargo ships and 3.6 million cargo containers each year, but it has gradually also evolved into a recreation hub with sailing, fishing, and even swimming. Five units of Gateway National Recreation Area frame the Lower Bay including Breezy Point, Great Kills Park, and Sandy Hook.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_172_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_172_r1.jpg)

The Hudson and East Rivers flow around Manhattan and into New York Harbor. [Credit 172](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img172a)
