**• RESOURCES: *[nps.gov/​stli](http://nps.gov/stli), [libertyellisfoundation.org](http://libertyellisfoundation.org), [siferry.com](http://siferry.com), [brooklynbridgepark.org](http://brooklynbridgepark.org), [govisland.com](http://govisland.com)***

**• HOW TO STAY: The Ritz-Carlton Battery Park *([ritzcarlton.com](http://ritzcarlton.com))* has telescopes in its rooms so guests can eyeball the harbor. Or try the 1 Hotel Brooklyn Bridge *([1hotels.com](http://1hotels.com))* or the Hyatt Regency Jersey City *([jerseycity.regency.hyatt.com](http://jerseycity.regency.hyatt.com))* on one of the old finger piers.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: The Staten Island Ferry plies the Upper Bay more than a hundred times each day. There is also daily ferry service from Battery Park and Liberty State Park (New Jersey) to Liberty and Ellis Islands. Seastreak *([seastreak.com](http://seastreak.com))* runs ferries from Manhattan to Sandy Hook.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_173_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_173_r1.jpg)

Third-class arrivals to America look across New York Harbor at Lady Liberty. [Credit 173](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img173a)

A New World Far From Home

I**n 1904, *National Geographic* published a report on immigration into the country that year, remarking foremost that there had been a lot of it. More than 606,000 people had passed through Ellis Island from all over the world, the vast majority of them hoping to stay. A few years later in 1907, almost 900,000 passed through.**

***National Geographic*’s coverage of Ellis Island came full circle in 1990, when editor Alice J. Hall wrote about the conversion of the island’s Main Building—empty since 1954—into a museum. Immigration had ebbed during World War I and in the 1920s; during World War II, Ellis Island had been used as a detention center for criminal aliens already in the country.**

**Though many immigrants ultimately found better lives in America, notes scrawled in the Main Building indicate they were weary and homesick by the time they arrived there. Hall wrote of one plaintive note, penned in Italian, that lamented, “Damned is the day I left my homeland.”**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_174_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_174_r1.jpg)

The Old World grace of the Garden District (above) and the flamboyance of Mardi Gras ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c04_r1.xhtml_img175)) are both faces of New Orleans.[Credit 174](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img174a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_175_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_175_r1.jpg)

[Credit 175](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img175a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

LOUISIANA, U.S.A.

**New Orleans**

The irrepressible soul of the Big Easy is Caribbean, African, French, and American

L**IKE THE GUMBO SERVED** across the city, New Orleans is a delicious mix of ingredients: European, Caribbean, and African influences all have a robust presence. You can hear it in the jazz that was born here, taste it in the rich spicy creole cooking, and experience it in the bacchanal of Mardi Gras. Founded by the French in 1718, the Gulf Coast settlement is one of the most spirited, resilient, historic cities in the American South.

New Orleans isn’t a museum piece or a tarted-up tourist attraction. The world saw its mettle in 2005 when the storm surge from Hurricane Katrina swept away levees and flooded the city. In a matter of days, it began to rebuild, and today a visitor might never know how close we came to losing this treasure.

To experience the city, visitors should hop on the St. Charles Streetcar line, which has clanged across town since 1835\. The route passes through the Garden District, a 19th-century neighborhood of graceful Victorian gingerbread homes and mansions built by the city’s elite, and still home to many of them.

It’s also where to find Commander’s Palace, one of the nation’s most famous restaurants, known for its jazz brunch and dishes like crawfish étouffée and bananas foster, a caramelized dessert made tableside. For others, the city means Bourbon Street, the avenue originally named for the French dynasty, where for centuries liquor has flowed and inhibitions evaporated. However, the French Quarter isn’t just for partying and imbibing. Around the corner at Preservation Hall, every night there is jazz, a music that developed on these streets and spread across the globe. A perfect end to the night is an amble down to Café du Monde, a 24-hour spot famous for café au lait and beignets, sugar-dusted fried doughnuts.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_176_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_176_r1.jpg)

Music, drinking, and dining are part of Bourbon Street’s draw in the French Quarter of New Orleans. [Credit 176](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img176a)

Another essential stop is St. Louis Cemetery No. 1, the city’s oldest burial ground and the final resting place of New Orleans leaders, scoundrels, and legends like Marie Laveau, its famed voodoo priestess. The elaborate mausoleums stand like landmarks in a city of the dead.

But, above all, the Crescent City is about embracing life, which is always on display at places like Tipitina’s. For more than a quarter century, the performance hall—named for a song by bluesman Professor Longhair—has hosted a Sunday night Cajun dance party called a fais-dodo. Couples of all ages take to the floor, swinging and dipping their way across the room to the infectious sounds of accordion and fiddle. Watching the scene, it’s clear the city embraces its unofficial French motto: *“Laissez les bons temps rouler*—Let the good times roll.”

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *Every time I close my eyes blowing that trumpet of mine—I look right in the heart of good old New Orleans.*
~ LOUIS ARMSTRONG

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[neworleansonline.com](http://neworleansonline.com), [neworleanscvb.com](http://neworleanscvb.com)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Hotel Monteleone *([hotelmonteleone.com](http://hotelmonteleone.com))* is an oasis of calm in the French Quarter. Louisiana’s demagogue governor Huey Long once held court in the Roosevelt *([therooseveltneworleans.com](http://therooseveltneworleans.com)).* In the Garden District, the tiny Henry Howard Hotel *([henryhowardhotel.com](http://henryhowardhotel.com))* offers modern accommodations with southern charm.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Louis Armstrong International Airport is easily reached with nonstop flights from dozens of U.S. cities. Amtrak offers service from New York, Los Angeles, and Chicago.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_177_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_177_r1.jpg)

The masked Mistick Krewe of Comus swirl their partners—dressed in their finest ball gowns—during the Mardi Gras ball. [Credit 177](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img177a)

Colorful Comus Ball

N**ew Orleans has always been a flamboyant city. “In [its] early years, masquerade balls and street pageants were almost spontaneous,” wrote *National Geographic* writer Carolyn Bennett Patterson in a 1960 story about the Big Easy. Though no one knows exactly when Mardi Gras—or Fat Tuesday—got its start in New Orleans, the first organized revelries there began in 1857 when a secret society, Mistick Krewe of Comus, put on an annual torchlit parade to honor Comus, the Greek god of merriment. The Civil War put that tradition (and others) on hold, but the festivities were back in full swing by 1871, and other Comus balls followed.**

**In this image of the Comus ball on Mardi Gras in 1960, masked members of the all-male krewe dance in capes and plumed helmets with their partners. Comus and his queen sit at the head of the dance. At twilight, the krewe lifted their masks to reveal their identities—but Comus never revealed his to the crowd.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_178_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_178_r1.jpg)

[Credit 178](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img178a)

CLASSIC SHOT

W**hen photographer Tyrone Turner was searching for powerful ways to depict the culture of his own hometown New Orleans, and the reasons why the city needed to rebuild after Hurricane Katrina, he turned to Preservation Hall in the French Quarter. “The experience there is like going to church for jazz,” he says. “You sit on benches and there is no food or drink. You are immersed in music in this spartan environment.” The musician pictured is Kenneth Terry, whose gravelly voice is reminiscent of Louis Armstrong’s. “He continues to amaze me. It reminds me why we need New Orleans,” Turner says.**

~ TYRONE TURNER

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_179_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_179_r1.jpg)

Maya innovation and ingenuity resulted in monumental temples like El Castillo (above) and detailed stone reliefs ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c04_r1.xhtml_img180)). [Credit 179](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img179a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_180_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_180_r1.jpg)

[Credit 180](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img180a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

MEXICO

**Chichén Itzá**

The ruins of a great Maya city deep in the Yucatán jungle

C**HICHÉN ITZÁ** is one of Mexico’s most visited ancient sites. The nearly two-square-mile (5 km²) complex, in the northern interior of Mexico’s Yucatán Peninsula, was a central city of the powerful Maya Empire from A.D. 750 to 1200\. The Itzá were the main ethnic group of Maya affiliation that once dominated this region, and Chichén Itzá means “the mouth of the well of the Itzá.”

Maya civilization began to decline in the 10th century A.D., and though Chichén Itzá was occupied for centuries after that, the city was mostly abandoned by the time the Spanish arrived in the 16th century.

Chichén Itzá contains the remnants of a sophisticated civilization. The Temple of Kukulkan (also known as El Castillo) is an enormous step pyramid nearly 79 feet (24 m) high, including its altarlike crowning structure. During each spring and fall equinox, sun, stones, shadow, and ingenious architecture bring an ancient Maya spectacle to life. The stone tiers of the temple cast a rippling shadow across its north facade that simulates the powerful serpent god Kukulkan slithering down its edifice. When Kukulkan has descended all the way down the side of the pyramid’s ceremonial staircase, the shadow joins the frightening stone serpent heads at its base and the illusion is complete.

The Maya preoccupation with astronomy and celestial objects is threaded through the ruins of Chichén Itzá. The Temple of Kukulkan has 365 steps, one for each day of the year, and the monumental structure was used for astronomical observation. El Caracol, or the Snail, is a domed structure that resembles a modern observatory and has windows that align with the path of Venus.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_181_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_181_r1.jpg)

The enigmatic, reclined Chacmool figure crowns a temple at Chichén Itzá. [Credit 181](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img181a)

Another intriguing remain is the Temple of the Warriors, a low-rise, elongated complex surrounded by stone columns. At the summit of its pyramid rests a Chacmool—an enigmatic reclined figure with head facing out and hands in lap. It may have served as a form of sacrificial altar, or as a ceremonial throne.

Finally, one must not miss the open air Ball Court, where stone rings are suspended from walls 20 feet (6 m) off the ground. Players on this court kept a rubber ball in the air with their hips, and very occasionally cast it through the stone rings. Though these games may often have been played just for sport, a relief on the stone benches that line the court show a player being decapitated at the conclusion of a match. Whether a winning or losing player was a more fitting sacrifice to the gods is a point of modern debate, but one thing is certain: Select contests were loaded with grave ceremonial import.

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[chichenitza.com](http://chichenitza.com), [visitmexico.com](http://visitmexico.com), [travelyucatan.com](http://travelyucatan.com), [yucatantoday.com](http://yucatantoday.com)***

**• HOW TO STAY: The Mayaland Hotel and Bungalows *([mayaland.com](http://mayaland.com))*, and the Lodge at Chichén Itzá are walking distance from the ruins.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Many visitors make day trips from Cancún, about 122 miles (196 km) away, and other beach cities including Tulum and Playa del Carmen. National Geographic also offers trips to Mexico *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_182_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_182_r1.jpg)

A member of the Carnegie team stands on the steps that ascend the Temple of Kukulkan at a 45-degree angle. [Credit 182](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img182a)

The Sacrificial Wells of the Maya

S**tarting in 1924, the Carnegie Institution of Washington spearheaded a 10-year excavation of Chichén Itzá. In 1925, *National Geographic* author Sylvanus Griswold Morley covered part of this effort.**

**In this image, a member of the excavation team poses on the west face of the Temple of Kukulkan. On its top was a temple in which the Maya began sacrificial ceremonies to appease wrathful gods in times of drought or other extremity. The priests and victims paraded from the temple to the Cenote Sacra (sacred well), where young Itzan maidens were flung in as offerings. “The very spectacularity of this cruel rite—the maidens at the brink of the dark pool, the incensing of the priests, the sides lined with waiting thousands, a push, perchance a startled cry, a splash below, and silence—all combined to arouse a general interest in the ceremony,” Morley wrote. Pilgrims came from miles to witness the spectacle and to throw their own personal treasures into the well.**

Hidden Journeys

GUATEMALA

**El Mirador**

An early Maya city buried in the jungle

**THE REMAINS OF EL MIRADOR** lie deep in the Guatemalan jungle. Often called the cradle of Maya civilization, El Mirador began its rise to prominence around the sixth century B.C. and peaked about 300 B.C. Its population hit perhaps a quarter of a million people before it started to decline.

La Danta is El Mirador’s most significant structure and one of the largest pyramids in the world. It rises 230 feet (70 m) from the jungle floor in three tiers, a configuration researchers believe represented Maya astrological beliefs.

The leading theory for the city’s demise is actually related to the architecture that made it so grand. Walls, pyramids, and palaces were decorated in a lime stucco produced by burning limestone. To fuel those fires, the people deforested the land and erosion then filled the region’s vast swamps—from which the Maya had collected mud to fertilize their agricultural fields—with clay. Deprived of their food source, the Maya abandoned the city around the ninth century A.D.

The jungle reclaimed El Mirador, and though a surveyor reported seeing ruins in the 1880s, it was another 40 years before the city was officially discovered. Much of the sprawling 14-square-mile (36 km²) complex remains overgrown.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_183_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_183_r1.jpg)

La Danta pyramid at El Mirador is one of the largest on Earth. [Credit 183](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img183a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[visitguatemala.com](http://visitguatemala.com), [toursguatemala.com](http://toursguatemala.com)***

**• HOW TO STAY: There are few accommodations near El Mirador. Some travelers arrange tours from hotels in Flores, about 198 miles (319 km) away. Hotel Petén *([hotelesdepeten.com](http://hotelesdepeten.com))* and Los Amigos Hostel *([amigoshostel.com](http://amigoshostel.com))* are two options.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: El Mirador remains remote and difficult to access. Hotels in Flores arrange tours, many passing through Carmelita village, a few days’ hike or mule ride to the ruins. Helicopter tours save time and difficulty. The Comision de Turismo Cooperativa Carmelita *([turismocooperativacarmelita.com](http://turismocooperativacarmelita.com/))* can provide tours and guides.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_184_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_184_r1.jpg)

[Credit 184](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img184a)

CLASSIC SHOT

A **towering pyramid still bears witness to the powerful Maya city of Chichén Itzá. But what’s not evident in this photo are four sacred natural sinkholes, or cenotes, where the Maya performed human sacrifices to appease their rain god, Chaak. Though he shot this photo aboveground, the meat of this *National Geographic* assignment was underground, and photographer Paul Nicklen had to undergo intensive cave-diving instruction before he undertook it. Exploring the upper and lower Maya realm, Nicklen rappelled into the cenotes with all his gear, discovering pottery, beads, human bones, and other offerings.**

~ PAUL NICKLEN

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_186_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_186_r1.jpg)

Vintage cars ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c04_r1.xhtml_img186)) and salsa dancing on the cobblestone Plaza Catedral (above) lend Habana Vieja its Old World charm. [Credit 185](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img185a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_185_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_185_r1.jpg)

[Credit 186](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img186a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

CUBA

**Habana Vieja**

A historic Caribbean city frozen in the 1950s

F**EW PLACES IN THE WORLD** offer a more romantic sense of decay than Habana Vieja, the once walled, ancient heart of Havana, capital of Cuba, the Caribbean’s largest island. Faded pastel-hued stucco facades crumble, brick bases are exposed, corners are edged with black from the humid weather. Laundry dries on column-adorned balconies, their French doors and window panes cracked, opening into worn-wooden interiors that catch lazy harbor breezes.

Ironically, there would be no Habana Vieja without Cold War tensions between the United States and the former Soviet Union, and without the ensuing embargo put in place in the time of John F. Kennedy. Shortly before he was deposed in 1958, Cuban dictator Fulgencio Batista y Zaldívar began an urban renewal program, with plans to raze much of the Old City and replace it with modern structures aimed at foreign tourists. Fortunately, this never came to be and Cuba went into a period of economic stagnation, leaving the city center locked in the ’50s.

Now, despite the end of the embargo, Habana Vieja remains a time capsule from half a century ago. Russian Ladas and 1950s American Chevys cruise the Malecón, the roadway and promenade along the Caribbean sea, taking tourists to clubs like the Tropicana cabaret, or El Floridita, the bar where Papa Hemingway lazily whiled away his days.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *La Habana Vieja captivates me—not for what’s there, but for what’s not there: the missing, the little silences, the ineffable.*
~ RICHARD BLANCO, POET

The city was founded in the early 1500s by Spanish conquistador Diego Velázquez on one of the Caribbean’s best formed natural harbors. Likely named for native chief Habaguanex, who once ruled the area, Havana grew into one of the empire’s most important ports. A series of castles protected the harbor, including 16th-century Castillo de los Tres Reyes Magos del Morro, where dissident writer Reinaldo Arenas was imprisoned by President Fidel Castro, and Fortaleza de San Carlos de la Cabaña, where Argentine-born revolutionary Ernesto “Che” Guevara once had an office (now a museum in his honor). Every night at 9 p.m., a ceremonial cannon is lit by a soldier in 18th-century military garb—in the days of Spanish rule, this signaled that the harbor and city gates were closing. In early days, a chain was also stretched across the inlet to keep out enemy ships.

Cuba’s once strong American ties are embodied by the district’s most imposing structure, Capitolio Nacional and its iconic dome, a 1929 replica of Washington’s own Capitol Building. With those ties reforming, Habana Vieja and all of Cuba have become a destination for vacationing Americans, meaning massive changes are ahead for the city.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_187_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_187_r1.jpg)

Baseball has long been Cuba’s sport, and little leaguers in Havana carry on the tradition—and the talent. [Credit 187](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img187a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[cubatravel.cu](http://www.cubatravel.cu/), [cu.usembassy.gov](https://cu.usembassy.gov/), [infotur.cu](http://www.infotur.cu/)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Spanish hotel chain Meliá *([luxurymeliacuba.com](http://luxurymeliacuba.com))* has properties all over Cuba, including Havana. The 1930s Spanish colonial Hotel Nacional *([hotelnacionaldecuba.com](http://hotelnacionaldecuba.com))* is an icon of prerevolution Cuba. The city’s most famous hotel, the Habana Libre *([hotelhabanalibre.com](http://hotelhabanalibre.com))*, the former Hilton, symbolized Cold War tension between Cuba and the United States. Opened just before the revolution, it was soon seized by Castro.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: As of this writing, restrictions on visiting Cuba are rapidly loosening. Companies like Marazul *([marazul.com](http://marazul.com))* can make arrangements easily. Flights travel to Havana from Miami and other U.S. cities. National Geographic also offers trips to Cuba *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_188_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_188_r1.jpg)

In Havana in 1989, preservationists restore a mural suffering centuries of neglect (top left); a torchlit celebration in Old Havana (top right); decaying buildings along the Malecón (bottom). [Credit 188](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img188a)

Hemingway’s Haunts

B**y the mid-1980s, Old Havana was in a state of serious decay—some of its buildings dated back 400 years. In 1982, Fidel Castro initiated a 20-year restoration project to save almost 200 buildings in an effort to increase tourism.**

***National Geographic* editor Joe Judge reported on the progress of the work in 1989, remarking incredulously of Cuba’s 1950s-era landscape: “In its material culture, Cuba is 90 miles away and 30 years ago.” He wandered about the Old City, looking in on the work being done on colonial palaces, 18th-century murals, and private homes.**

**At one point, he ventured into the oldest known house in the city to talk to the 80-year-old resident, Lina Zambrana. She remembered Ernest Hemingway from his days in residence at Havana’s Ambos Mundos Hotel, where he worked on *For Whom the Bell Tolls.* Zambrana recalled, “He was always around. The first time I saw him, I asked him, ‘Are you Hemingway?’ He said, ‘Sure, let’s have a drink.’ ”**

Hidden Journeys

CUBA

**Santiago de Cuba**

Colonial architecture, lively culture, a distinct identity

**CUBA’S SECOND LARGEST CITY**, Santiago de Cuba, is best known for its baroque and colonial architecture. Far fewer visitors find their way here than to Havana, but those who do find a historic, culturally rich city. Like Havana, it was built on an inlet. It was protected by the Castillo de San Pedro de la Roca del Morro, a sprawling, multiterraced 17th-century fortress five miles (8 km) southwest of the city, among the New World’s best preserved Spanish fortresses. Its cannon-decked ramparts offer stellar views of the bay and the verdant surrounding hills.

Parque Céspedes serves as the ancient heart of Santiago de Cuba and is flanked by its most important buildings. Butter yellow Catedral de Nuestra Señora de la Asunción, adorned with crosses and angels, dates originally from the 16th century. Rebuilt through the years after numerous earthquakes, it reigns majestically over the plaza. The earliest conquistadores viewed the square from the Andalusian-style Casa de Diego Velázquez, built by the city’s founder in the 16th century. Santiago de Cuba is also home to Moncada Barracks, where Fidel Castro began the Cuban Revolution. Nearby San Juan Hill, site of pivotal battles of 1898’s Spanish-American War, shows the deep ties Cuba has long had with the United States.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_189_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_189_r1.jpg)

Parque Céspedes is the heart of Santiago de Cuba. [Credit 189](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img189a)

**• HOW TO STAY: Spanish hotel chain Melia *([luxurymeliacuba.com](http://luxurymeliacuba.com))* has properties in Santiago de Cuba.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: What makes Santiago de Cuba a less visited locale is its remote location on the eastern edge of the island. It is a 16-hour bus or 11-hour car ride from Havana over rough roads, and train service from Havana is subject to severe delays. Cubana *([www.cubana.cu](http://www.cubana.cu))* offers flights connecting both cities. Various cruise companies dock in Santiago de Cuba, and flights are available from Miami and New York.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_190_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_190_r1.jpg)

[Credit 190](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img190a)

CLASSIC SHOT

P**hotographer Kike Calvo has spent more than seven years photographing ballerinas in iconic settings that are undergoing a cultural transformation. In this image he captures dancers from Alicia Alonso’s Cuban National Ballet mid-leap on Havana’s famous seaside promenade, the Malecón. “All images capture not only the beauty of the dance, but they are uniquely created as a whole,” he says. “Reducing a composition to its essence is not an easy task, but one that challenges me constantly.” To Calvo, this moment was also a prelude to broader movement—all three ballerinas migrated to dance professionally in other places after the shot was taken.**

~ KIKE CALVO

CHILE

**Easter Island**

Enormous stone busts on a remote Pacific island

C**ENTURIES AGO**, Easter Island’s Polynesian society erected hundreds of huge, masterfully carved stone statues. But why did islanders undertake the enormous work of building and moving the towering volcanic stone busts? They made a subsistence living by farming, clearing the heavily forested land for agriculture. And so why—as some speculate—did they continue the strenuous construction on the statues when it became clear they were deforesting their home, disrupting its fragile ecosystem, and threatening their own society?

Before those questions can be examined, there’s an even larger one: How did the islanders even reach this tiny speck of land? Located a full 1,300 miles (2,092 km) from the nearest inhabited island (Pitcairn), it’s one of the most remote places on Earth.

Historians have only some of the answers. The people who built the massive statues (known as moai) likely made a treacherous cross-ocean journey in canoes from the Marquesas Islands somewhere from A.D. 800 to 1200. They called their new home Rapa Nui.

Over the centuries, they began to construct the long-faced moai as a form of ancestor worship. The enormous heads averaged 14 tons (12.7 metric tons), and 13 feet (4 m) tall. Some had coral eyes and hats or topknots of contrasting red stone.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *We could hardly conceive how these islanders, wholly unacquainted with any mechanical power, could raise such stupendous figures.*
~ CAPTAIN JAMES COOK

By the 17th century, the island’s population had become embroiled in civil war and destruction. When a Dutch explorer—the first known European visitor—arrived on the island on Easter Sunday in 1722, he found a population of several thousand. But within a few decades, most of the islanders had died, probably from infighting and smallpox, and nearly every statue had been toppled.

Only in the last century have the moai been re-erected, and today much of the island is administered as a national park. The most striking sight is on a beachfront called Tongariki, where 15 somber-faced figures stare inland into an empty distance, with the surf at their backs. Another must-see is Rano Raraku, the quarry where the moai were cut from the tufa stone and carved. Workers somehow hauled the enormous statues across the island, perhaps using timber as skids.

The surviving Rapa Nui people are reinstituting their culture. Every February, they celebrate a two-week festival called Tapati, and visitors can hear the island’s Polynesian language on the radio and at Catholic church services.

But it’s easy to find solitude here. Trails cross the north side of the island, and with little effort visitors find themselves alone, staring out to the ocean and contemplating the silent, steadfast stone heads that alone have witnessed the passing centuries.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_194_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_194_r1.jpg)

Easter Island was once densely forested, but its ancient inhabitants cleared the land for agriculture. [Credit 194](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img194a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[chile.travel/​en](http://chile.travel/en)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Explora *([explora.com](http://explora.com))*, South America’s leading boutique chain of eco-lodges, has a stunning all-inclusive resort, offering 20 different island excursions to guests. An on-site gourmet restaurant specializes in fresh seafood and Chilean wines. While not nearly as posh, Hare Noi *([noihotels.com](http://noihotels.com))* offers rustic luxury with resort amenities, including a pool and spa. On a more intimate scale, Cabañas Christophe *([cabanaschristophe.com](http://cabanaschristophe.com))* provides a comfortable budget option with bungalow rooms and charming hosts in the island’s only town, Hanga Roa.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Airlines serve Easter Island from Santiago, Chile, and Tahiti. Cruise ships also make occasional visits. National Geographic also offers trips to Easter Island *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_195_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_195_r1.jpg)

This excavated statue on Easter Island was deemed unusual, with the forms of hands resting below its middle. [Credit 195](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img195a)

Stone Warts

I**t took three years of research and planning, a custom-made 90 foot (27.5 m) schooner, and a 13-month voyage at sea for husband and wife team W. Scoresby and Katherine Routledge to reach Easter Island from England in 1913\. A 1921 issue of *National Geographic* magazine published an account of their time among the ancient statues. It was not comfortable—the Routledges contended with cockroaches, flies, and mosquitoes. And unearthing the half-buried statues was grueling.**

**But the Routledges found the skill with which the giant heads had been carved remarkable. Nearly as interesting were the large number of figures they found discarded. Sometimes this was due to flaws the sculptors belatedly found in the stone as they carved the statues. Their tools could not work around hard veins in the rock, and so—as the Routledges noted with amusement—“As the work could not go down to posterity with a large wart on its nose, or excrescence on its chin, it had to be stopped.”**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_196_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_196_r1.jpg)

Nazca boobies, distinctive seabirds found on the Galápagos Islands [Credit 196](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img196a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_197_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_197_r1.jpg)

Bartolomé Island in the Galápagos archipelago [Credit 197](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img197a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

ECUADOR

**Galápagos Islands**

Iguanas, sea lions, giant tortoises, and flocks of flamingos

I**N THE EASTERN PACIFIC**, about 620 miles (997 km) from mainland Ecuador, lies the Galápagos archipelago, known for its abundant wildlife and renowned as the place that helped Charles Darwin formulate his theories of natural selection and evolution.

Lava fields and live volcanoes, giant sea cliffs, and white-sand strands are significant geological attractions. But wildlife is the main reason the islands are a bucket list item for world travelers. Conservation efforts date back to the 1950s, so many of the animals have little or no fear of humans. This translates into close-up sightings of birds, sea lions, marine and land iguanas, penguins, giant tortoises, and the other creatures that inhabit the Galápagos and surrounding waters.

The archipelago was formed over millions of years by volcanic activity and plate tectonics, and comprises 20 islands and more than 100 smaller bits and bobs. Nearly the entire land area lies inside Galápagos National Park, and the surrounding waters are protected within a massive marine reserve that extends 40 nautical miles offshore. The 30,000 human inhabitants are spread across four islands and occupy only 3 percent of the land area.

The islands were uninhabited when Bishop Tomás de Berlanga (the bishop of Panama) and his maritime party came across them by accident during a 1535 voyage between Panama and Peru. Ecuador claimed the islands in 1832\. Three years later, the naturalist and biologist Darwin stepped ashore on a visit that would revolutionize natural science and spark ongoing religious debate about the origins of life. In 1978, the Galápagos archipelago was designated one of the first UNESCO World Heritage sites, owing to its unique geology, flora, and fauna.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_198_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_198_r1.jpg)

The Galápagos marine iguana survives on algae and seaweed and sneezes often to expel salt collecting near its nose. [Credit 198](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img198a)

Puerto Ayora on Santa Cruz; Puerto Baquerizo Moreno on San Cristóbal; Puerto Villamil on Isabela; and Puerto Velasco Ibarra on Santa María are the archipelago’s only towns. With nearby airfields and busy seaports, they have evolved into tourist hubs that cater to the 200,000 or so visitors who arrive each year. But the landscape of the islands is really much more intriguing than the towns.

Isla Isabela is by far the largest island in the Galápagos archipelago. Six volcanoes joined to create this jagged landmass; one of these is the massive Volcán Wolf, which last erupted in 2015\. With its own active volcano, Fernandina Island is a massive lava field, with a shoreline populated by seabirds, crabs, and marine and land iguanas. Genovesa Island formed around a collapsed caldera and provides a nesting spot for thousands of seabirds, while Wolf and Darwin Islands are renowned for their bountiful shark populations.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *The archipelago is a little world within itself.*
~ CHARLES DARWIN, *THE VOYAGE OF THE BEAGLE*

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[galapagos.org](http://galapagos.org), [darwinfoundation.org](http://darwinfoundation.org)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Most people explore the islands on commercial yachts or small expedition cruise ships, on voyages that last anywhere from a few days to several weeks. Small hotels in Puerto Ayora, Puerto Villamil, and Puerto Baquerizo Moreno cater to land-based travelers who explore the islands via maritime day trips. Charles Darwin Research Station near Puerto Ayora doubles as the archipelago’s national park visitor center. Finch Bay Galápagos Hotel, a National Geographic Unique Lodge of the World member, is on Santa Cruz Island (see [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml)).**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: The only ways to reach the Galápagos are commercial flights from mainland Ecuador, cruise ships, or private yachts. National Geographic also offers trips to the Galápagos *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_199_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_199_r1.jpg)

The brigantine *Yankee* was too wide to fit through this 30-foot (9 m) channel in the Galápagos. The crew swam through instead. [Credit 199](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img199a)

Sailing Through the Galapagos

T**he wildlife of the Galápagos Islands delighted *National Geographic* writers Irving and Electa Johnson when they sailed through the archipelago on the brigantine *Yankee* in 1959\. As they cruised among turquoise coves with lava rock shores, they had thrilling encounters with giant tortoises, iguanas, cormorants, and sea turtles. A group of penguins greeted the ship near Isabela Island: “As we hove to, a delegation came alongside like a reception committee, curious, friendly, and fascinating to watch,” they wrote.**

**Near Buccaneer Cove on San Salvador Island, they glimpsed a flamingo flock—“flecks of rose pink against the deep, shadowed blue of the pond,” Irving wrote. “Barely breathing, we watched the elegant creatures wading with measured bridesmaid steps.”**

**At Kicker Rock, *Yankee* nosed into the 30-foot-wide (9 m) channel between towering rock walls, but the opening was too narrow for the yards on the masts. The crew swam through the channel instead with a group of sea lions.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_200_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_200_r1.jpg)

[Credit 200](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img200a)

CLASSIC SHOT

P**hotographer Joel Sartore shot this image of a flamingo while serving as a photo expert on a National Geographic Expedition to the Galápagos. “The calmness of the morning made the water smooth as glass, and yielded a perfectly-mirrored reflection,” he says. “This flamingo was like all other wildlife we encountered on the Galápagos; very tame. It didn’t even look up to watch us.” Galápagos wildlife is generally quite tolerant to human presence. “I found that to be true across the board, from sea lions to penguins to this flamingo,” said Sartore. “This is a photographer’s dream, to have repeated, close-in access to wildlife of all kinds in a pristine landscape.”**

~ JOEL SARTORE

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_202_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_202_r1.jpg)

The ruins of the Inca citadel Machu Picchu sit high in the Andes. [Credit 201](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img201a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

PERU

**Machu Picchu**

Ruins of an Inca fortress high in the Andes

T**HE AWESOME RUIN** of Machu Picchu, nicknamed the “lost city of the Inca,” is perched high in the Andes Mountains between the peaks Huayna Picchu and Cerro Machu Picchu. On a mountain plateau some 8,000 feet (2,438 m) above sea level, the 20-acre (8 ha) complex includes the remains of ancient houses, temples, pathways, and dizzying stone staircases that wind through agricultural terraces. The Urubamba River roars through the valley below the ruins.

Machu Picchu means “old mountain” in Quechua, and historians still speculate as to its purpose. It may have been built as a summer retreat for Inca emperor Pachacuti in the mid-15th century, during a golden age of expansion for the Inca Empire.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_201_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_201_r1.jpg)

A Peruvian mother and her child in Urubamba Valley (aka Sacred Valley) [Credit 202](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img202a)

When the Spanish conquistadores plundered and destroyed other Inca cities in the 16th century, they overlooked this one, and the mountain citadel is now among the most intact Inca sites remaining. Despite the fact that the Spanish never seem to have reached it, the Inca abandoned Machu Picchu sometime in the 16th century, only about 100 years after they built it. It was left to crumble, and the dense, surrounding cloud forest promptly overtook much of it.

Monumental as the ruin is, Machu Picchu was virtually unknown to outsiders until 1911 when a local guide led American explorer and Yale University professor Hiram Bingham III to it. Bingham made subsequent archaeological visits with funding from the National Geographic Society to catalog the ruins and clear them of overgrowth.

The site contains marvels like the *intihuatana* stone, an altarlike structure with a stone slab that served as a sundial and calendar. Overlooking the rock-cut niches of the Royal Mausoleum, where mummies have been stored, is the majestic Temple of the Sun, dedicated to sun god Inti. The semicircular temple is built over a natural rock outcropping that may have served as an altar—its surface is lined with rivulets, where water or sacred liquids could have flowed, and a trapezoidal window is built into the wall at the edge of the rock. Much of the temple has been rebuilt in the past several decades, to give an impression of its appearance during the Inca Empire. Mortar-less stones, mostly granite with some limestone, are so accurately cut and stacked that not even a credit card or knife slips through their jointures.

In 1983, Machu Picchu was protected as a UNESCO site, but huge numbers of tourists still threaten to degrade the ancient ruin. It is at its most breathtaking at sunrise and sunset, when crowds of day-trippers from Cusco—47 miles (75 km) to the southeast and the main gateway to the site—are at their thinnest.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_203_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_203_r1.jpg)

No longer a “lost city,” Machu Picchu is reachable by rail from local towns in Peru. [Credit 203](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img203a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[machupicchu.com](http://machupicchu.com), [peru.travel/​en](http://peru.travel/en)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Tickets to Machu Picchu must be booked in advance *([machupicchu.gob.pe](http://machupicchu.gob.pe)).* The convenient but pricey option for lodging is Belmond Sanctuary Lodge *([belmond.com/​sanctuary-lodge-­machu-picchu](http://belmond.com/sanctuary-lodge-machu-picchu))*, at Machu Picchu’s gates. Or stay in Aguas Calientes, the village at the bottom of the mountain. You can also make a day trip from Cusco, about four hours away. A National Geographic Unique Lodge of the World property, Inkaterra Machu Picchu Pueblo Hotel is in Aguas Calientes (see [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml)).**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: On your own, make a day trip from Cusco via train, with PeruRail, and then bus up to the ruins from Aguas Calientes. National Geographic also offers trips to Peru *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_204_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_204_r1.jpg)

Hiram Bingham’s caravan on a newly repaired road on the way to Machu Picchu [Credit 204](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img204a)

Wrestling Machu Picchu From the Jungle

A **year after Hiram Bingham brought Machu Picchu to the world’s attention, the National Geographic and Yale University funded his 1912 expedition to excavate the site. It was no easy task: “This ancient city is in the heart of a region most wonderfully defended by nature; the most inaccessible part of the Andes,” he wrote.**

**Sheer cliffs soared thousands of feet above rushing rivers with no bridges—Bingham’s team had to build their own. They hiked up slopes so steep they were almost vertical. The trail was too rough even for mules; everything had to be carried up by hand. For seven months the team conducted backbreaking work—the city was completely overgrown with cane jungle and thorny mesquite trees. There were brushes with malaria and dysentery. A topographer almost fell off Huayna Picchu.**

**But it was worth it. *National Geographic* editors praised the “splendid record” Bingham’s team produced, which charted the archaeology and topography of the Inca city in detail.**

Hidden Journeys

PERU

**Choquequirao**

The true lost city of the Inca

**YOU CAN COME EARLY** and leave late at Machu Picchu in the hopes of having the sacred ruins to yourself, or you can visit Choquequirao, about 27 miles (44 km) away as the condor flies, or about 62 miles (100 km) via walking path. (The latter entails a nine-day journey that many people use to explore the complex of Inca trails in the region.) Bingham discovered this ruin, too, when locals led him to Machu Picchu.

Vastly undervisited compared to its sister site, Choquequirao sometimes sees as few as 30 visitors a day. Yet it is an even larger complex than Machu Picchu, and most of it is still being excavated and cataloged. About a third of the site has been restored since serious work began on it in the 1970s.

The settlement was built in the same time period as Machu Picchu, roughly the middle of the 15th century, and is thought to have been a place of final refuge for the Inca resisting the Spanish. The site’s most iconic feature is the limestone-and-granite terrace stairway that leads to its main plaza. The steps are inlaid with white rock llamas, showing the path they took to supply workers with food and construction materials. Other buildings and structures in the complex are thought to have been planned with astrological significance.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_205_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_205_r1.jpg)

The main plaza and houses at the remote, less visited Inca city Choquequirao [Credit 205](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img205a)

**• HOW TO STAY: Many visitors base themselves in the town of Abancay, 35 miles (56 km) from the trailhead village of Cachora, starting point for the trek to Choquequirao. Abancay’s hotels are aimed at backpackers and budget-conscious travelers. One moderate option is El Peregrino Apart Hotel *([aparthotelperegrino.com](http://aparthotelperegrino.com)).***

**• HOW TO GET THERE: The best maintained trail to Choquequirao originates at Cachora; from there it is about a two-day trek to the site. Dangers include increasingly rare Shining Path rebel attacks. Trekking tours include Crooked Trails *([crookedtrails.org](http://crookedtrails.org))* and Llama Path *([llamapath.com](http://llamapath.com)).***

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_206_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_206_r1.jpg)

[Credit 206](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img206a)

CLASSIC SHOT

“T**he ruins [at Machu Picchu], of course, get all the attention,” says photographer Jim Richardson. “But if anything stands out for me, it’s the wondrous setting. In all directions there are mountains, and far below the river sweeps in an encompassing arc.” He visited with a National Geographic Expedition. “The day was dark and foreboding. But then I noticed the storm clouds swirling toward the plaza below, and just about then the llamas came by and I couldn’t believe my good luck.” The llamas—gentle but temperamental creatures—were preening, Richardson noted. “They pose, they gesture, and then they are gone,” he observed with humor.**

~ JIM RICHARDSON

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_207_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_207_r1.jpg)

The colorful Old City under the dome of the San Pedro Claver Church [Credit 207](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img207a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

COLOMBIA

**Cartagena**

An ancient Spanish colonial city with views to the sea

C**ARTAGENA DE INDIAS’** Ciudad Vieja, or Old City, is among South America’s most ancient Spanish colonial settlements. Founded in 1533, its wealth and beauty were built on the horrors of slavery, gold extraction, and sugar harvesting in Colombia’s tropical climate. Other European powers attacked the city over the centuries seeking its riches, most famously England’s Sir Frances Drake, who sacked it in 1586.

To protect this important center, Spain built one of its largest marine and land defense systems, with more than seven miles (11 km) of stone walls and ramparts along Cartagena’s harbor and adjacent areas. The largest of the outer forts is the mountainous Castillo de San Felipe de Barajas, which took decades to construct. Today, the walled fortresses offer a tranquil way to explore the city and take in views of the sea. It’s easy to forget these were once vital defenses in bloody battles.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_208_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_208_r1.jpg)

A tranquil beach in Cartagena [Credit 208](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img208a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_209_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_209_r1.jpg)

The Castillo San Felipe de Barajas, just outside the walled city of Cartagena, took decades to build. [Credit 209](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img209a)

Long Colombia’s most important tourism destination, Ciudad Vieja’s streets remain a mix of gentrified districts for foreign and domestic visitors and humbler enclaves where locals work and shop. Balconies, windows, and gardens everywhere overflow with blooming tropical flowers, especially in the deepest summer months of December, January, and February. Night and day, music fills the streets with a mix of *cumbia, mapalé*, and other Afro-Caribbean sounds. Tourists and locals meet in the bars, shops, and clubs that surround Plaza de Los Coches, shadowed by an imposing yellow clock tower. This plaza was once the site of the old slave market.

Cartagena has many worthy museums—Plaza de Bolívar is home to both the sprawling National Historical Museum and the exquisite Gold Museum, or Museo del Oro Zenú. The Inquisition Museum, also known as the Palacio de la Inquisición, details the hideous tortures once inflicted on victims throughout the Spanish Empire.

Nearby, overlooking the Plaza de San Pedro Claver, are the stately convent and church of San Pedro Claver. They are named for the priest—later canonized a saint—who was one of the lone souls to minister and show compassion to the slaves who arrived in the city’s port on ships. His remains are in a coffin under the altar of the church, and part of the adjacent convent is open as a museum, with displays of pre-Columbian artifacts and religious art.

Just a few minutes’ walk north is Plaza de Santa Domingo, where people dine alfresco steps from the sea. “La Gorda,” the “Fat One,” a bulbous bronze sculpture by Fernando Botero, reclines in the center of this colorful square.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *All of my books have loose threads of Cartagena in them.*
~ GABRIEL GARCÍA MÁRQUEZ

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[colombia.travel](http://colombia.travel), [cartagenadeindias.travel](http://cartagenadeindias.travel)***

**• HOW TO STAY: The city’s most luxurious hotel is Sofitel Legend Santa Clara Cartagena *([sofitel.com](http://sofitel.com))*, originally a convent dating from 1621\. The structure boasts gardens, pools, and colonial decor, and rooms look out to the sea and the city.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Direct flights to Cartagena are available from Miami and other American cities, or via the Colombian capital, Bogotá. National Geographic also offers trips to Colombia *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com))*.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_210_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_210_r1.jpg)

A food vendor across from the fort of San Felipe de Barajas (bottom); Cartagena’s Bocagrande peninsula (top left); a Spanish guitar (top right) [Credit 210](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img210a)

Cartagena by Lamplight

*N**ational Geographic*** **editor Bart McDowell discovered Cartagena’s alluring side in 1989 when he explored the city *de noche y en coche*—by night and by coach.**

**“Like an aging beauty, Cartagena best recaptures her voluptuous past in the dim light of candles and coach lamps,” he wrote. “To the cadence of horse hoofs, on streets so narrow balconies almost collide, visitors see baroque facades and castellated bastions…a shadowy, lived-in museum with street life and laughter and the gossip of history.”**

**Residents ventured outdoors as sweltering afternoons gave way to peaceful, breezy evenings. To the sound of Spanish guitar in the plazas, children played games with bottle caps, friends chatted at cafés, and lovers lingered in the shadow of the great cathedral.**

**“Other towns have more culture,” said Jesús Meza, a sorcerer or “spiritual counsel” who had fashioned amulets for locals for 40 years. “But people here have the *instinct* for happiness.”**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_211_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_211_r1.jpg)

[Credit 211](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img211a)

CLASSIC SHOT

M**usic pulsates throughout Cartagena, Colombia, encouraging passersby to pick up the beat. “I just love the blur and energy of the dancer and the glee of the man,” says photographer Pete McBride. He was on a personal trip to the city when he stumbled across this lively scene. He was struck by the festive nature of the place, and the exuberant dancing in the streets. “The spontaneity of the place made it hard to predict what might happen next,” he says. “I just had to be ready.” And the second after he got the shot? “I joined in the dancing. The music was too good.”**

~ PETE MCBRIDE

Rio de Janeiro and Sugar Loaf Mountain on Guanabara Bay as seen from Corcovado [Credit 212](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img212a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_212_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_212_r1.jpg)

Copacabana Beach [Credit 213](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img213a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

BRAZIL

**Sugar Loaf, Rio**

A distinctive monolith, signature of Rio de Janeiro

B**RAZIL’S RENOWNED** waterfront mountain is an emblem of both Rio de Janeiro and South America. It’s as much a part of the city’s urban mosaic as samba, soccer, and white sand.

Formed approximately 600 million years ago, the granite monolith has double summits looming up from a narrow peninsula that partially divides Guanabara Bay from the open Atlantic. The upper peak is called Pão de Açúcar (Sugar Loaf), the lower one Morro da Urca. Towering to a height of 1,299 feet (396 m), Sugar Loaf’s near-vertical flanks are largely naked rock. But the two summits and the saddle between them are carpeted in the kind of lush, tropical Atlantic forest that once shaded this entire coast.

Sugar Loaf was christened during Brazil’s 16th-century sugar boom, and one theory holds that its name derived from the conical clay molds *(pãos de açúcar)* used to ship raw sugar across the ocean.

Rio de Janeiro was born at the bottom of Sugar Loaf, a small settlement that was founded in 1565 by Portuguese soldier Estácio de Sá. As the city grew up around its base, Sugar Loaf was used primarily as a navigation aid for sailors. Intrepid British nanny Henrietta Carstairs made the first recorded ascent by a European when she climbed the mountain solo in 1817 and planted a Union Jack on top. That set off a nationalistic flurry of Brazilian attempts.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *Rio has edged in between the hills and the sea, and on that boldly chosen strip of land has met and trounced the jungle.*
~ PETER FLEMING, *BRAZILIAN ADVENTURE*

When the famous Bondinho aerial tramway was completed in 1912—only the third of its kind on the entire planet—gazing down on Rio from the top of Sugar Loaf became one of Brazil’s “must-do” attractions. After 60 years of operation, the original wooden cable cars were replaced by modern Italian-designed, bubble-shaped units in 1972.

From the double summit, there are sweeping views of Copacabana beach (Ipanema is just beyond); Corcovado peak and the Christ the Redeemer statue; Guanabara Bay; and the Atlantic Ocean. The summits themselves have a surprising array of more pedestrian attractions like restaurants, shops, a heliport for aerial tours, and an aerial tramway museum. There’s even an open-air amphitheater where concerts and carnival events are staged.

A 1980s project called Operação Vegetação regenerated much of the natural vegetation on the summits. And nowadays visitors can meander through the mountaintop forest on trails, keeping a lookout for indigenous wildlife like birds, lizards, and marmosets, small monkeys that resemble squirrels.

Not surprisingly, Sugar Loaf is also a popular rock-climbing venue. Approximately 60 different routes traverse its granite facades, some relatively easy and others challenging even for the most experienced climbers.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_214_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_214_r1.jpg)

Visitors take in spectacular views from the top of Sugar Loaf, Rio’s landmark monolith. [Credit 214](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img214a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[bondinho.com.br](http://bondinho.com.br), [visit.rio/​en](http://visit.rio/en)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Many visitors to the city stay at hotels along Copacabana Beach, most of them a 10- to 30-minute taxi ride from the bottom cable car station. Or visitors can join Rio Hiking *([riohiking.com.br](http://riohiking.com.br))* for a guided ascent via a steep, 2.5-hour trail.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Operating daily from 8 a.m. to 9 p.m., Bondinho aerial tramway takes visitors from Praia Vermelha to Morro da Urca, where they transfer to a second set of cable cars bound for Pão de Açúcar. Both journeys take about three minutes *([bondinho.com.br](http://bondinho.com.br)).* National Geographic also offers trips to Brazil *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_215_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_215_r1.jpg)

Workers in a wartime mine in Minas Gerais dug for quartz crystals for radios; here they push waste up the terraces. [Credit 215](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img215a)

Rio Pitches In

I**n 1942, Brazil joined the Allies in World War II after Brazilian ships were torpedoed by German U-boats. A year later, *National Geographic* writer W. Robert Moore journeyed south to see how war had changed Rio.**

**“The statue of Christ still stood benignly atop precipitous Corcovado,” Moore observed as he flew over the city. “But searchlights which once made it stand out like a white beacon at night had been blacked out.”**

**Busy, crowded Rio was running full throttle in support of the war effort. Gas shortages meant that all private cars had been ordered off the streets. Residents hung onto packed trolleys, or rode in “charcomobiles”—cars equipped with charcoal burning units to save fuel.**

**Production of the country’s vast resources—rubber, iron ore, mica, quartz crystals—was in overdrive. Flawless crystals were essential for radio and detection devices; every fighter plane, tank, and walkie-talkie used them. In Rio, Moore watched women inspect crystals mined deep in Brazil’s interior.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_216_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_216_r1.jpg)

Salt flats in the Atacama Desert [Credit 216](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img216a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_217_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_217_r1.jpg)

Llamas recline at twilight in rocky terrain in the Atacama. [Credit 217](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img217a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

CHILE

**Atacama Desert**

At the foot of the Andes, a landscape where rain barely falls

S**AVE FOR THE WINTRY** deserts at Earth’s poles, this stretch of northern Chile is the driest place on the planet. Hemmed between the snowcapped Andes and the Pacific Ocean, the Atacama has been sculpted by wind and baked in sun for more than 10 million years. The vast desert stretches over 40,000 square miles (103,600 km²), an expanse larger than the country of Greece. In the heart of this arid landscape, you won’t find even a single blade of grass. In the centuries that humans have been measuring it, some parts have never even seen rain.

The Atacama is deeply inhospitable to life. Creatures that wander through and lose their way back to water are preserved for eons. The lack of rain and cool temperatures (even in summer it rarely tops 80°F/26.5°C) have helped preserve ancient artifacts and even the remains of the pre-Columbian Chinchorro people who eked out an existence in the desert’s margins. Their oldest mummies predate those in the Nile Valley by 2,000 years.

But not everything in the Atacama is blasted and dead. Birds have the advantage of being able to wing it to safety, and species like coastal oystercatchers, Peruvian pelicans, terns, and gulls as well as burrowing owls and turkey vultures are sometimes spotted in the skies. On the periphery of the desert, especially where Chile abuts neighboring Bolivia, snowmelt from the Andes creates oases and feeds vast salt flats, and life thrives. In Los Flamencos National Reserve, a series of several flats not far from the town of San Pedro de Atacama, species such as the vicuña (an alpaca relative), rabbitlike viscacha, and Andean fox are common. Blue lagoons attract three different species of flamingos, creating an electric spectacle of pink and blue set against a dusty red backdrop.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_218_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_218_r1.jpg)

The geyser field El Tatio in the Atacama Desert is one of the largest in the Southern Hemisphere. [Credit 218](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img218a)

In places, the landscape itself feels alive. In the foothills of the Andes 50 miles (80 km) north of San Pedro de Atacama, more than 80 geysers shoot jets of water as high as 20 feet (6 m) into the crisp morning air. El Tatio is among the largest active geyser fields in the Southern Hemisphere.

At night, hundreds of thousands of stars light up the skies over the Atacama Desert. The air is crisp and evening clouds are virtually nonexistent. Some of the world’s most sophisticated telescopes train their lenses on the vast expanses of night sky over the central Atacama. Though visitor access to the telescopes atop high hills is largely restricted, many trek to the Moon Valley (Valle de la Luna) or Death Valley (Valle de la Muerte), within view of the triangular Licancabur Volcano, for spectacular stargazing.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *[The Atacama] is an immense, illusive enigma…I couldn’t understand its language: that is, its silence.*
~ PABLO NERUDA, CHILEAN POET

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[chile.travel/​en/​where-to-go/​north-and-the-atacama-desert](http://chile.travel/en/where-to-go/north-and-the-atacama-desert), [sanpedroatacama.com](http://sanpedroatacama.com)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Many people make San Pedro de Atacama their base. The high-end hotel Awasi *([awasiatacama.com](http://awasiatacama.com))* has daily access to personal desert guides, but cheaper options like Hotel Tulor are closer to the town’s church and central plaza. National Geographic Unique Lodge of the World member Tierra Atacama Hotel & Spa is on the outskirts of San Pedro de Atacama (see [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml)).**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: LATAM and Sky Airlines offer direct flights from Santiago to Calama, 90 minutes via shuttle bus or rental car to San Pedro de Atacama. National Geographic also offers trips to Chile *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_219_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_219_r1.jpg)

A 1,000-year-old mummy near Camarones Cove (top left); Chinchorro spearheads, point, and hook (top right); curators in Santiago repack mummies (bottom). [Credit 219](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img219a)

Chinchorro Mummies

T**housands of years before the Inca Empire, prehistoric fisherfolk known as the Chinchorro lived along the Pacific coast at the edge of the Atacama Desert. The evidence they left behind: wooden carvings, fish hooks fashioned from mother-of-pearl, and the eerie mummies of their dead.**

**In 1995, Chilean archaeologist Bernardo Arriaza described his findings for *National Geographic* magazine. The Chinchorro, who practiced mummification 2,000 years before the ancient Egyptians, devotedly preserved adults and babies alike. Skeletons were padded with reeds and ash paste, stuffed with earth, and rewrapped in the original skin. Vivid red or black painted masks covered the face of the mummified remains.**

**Mummies were displayed before burial, helping the community grieve and honor their ancestors in the afterlife. Arriaza envisioned the gathering: “The mummy of a beloved relative gleams in the moonlight, filling the Chinchorro with hope and reassurance by evoking the protective powers of the supernatural.”**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_220_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_220_r1.jpg)

[Credit 220](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img220a)

CLASSIC SHOT

G**iant dishes in Chile’s Atacama Desert scan the heavens for clues about the universe. “The dishes danced together in homage to the night sky, sometimes swinging about quickly, other times slowly, intently tracking distant curiosities,” photographer Dave Yoder has written about this assignment. “Remember the moment,” he told himself. “You’re likely not to see anything like this again.” Access to this area is highly guarded; Yoder was authorized entry only for the *National Geographic* story he was photographing. The shoot was threatened by freezing conditions; Yoder had to use a coffee cup warmer to keep his lens frost-free.**

~ DAVE YODER

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_222_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_222_r1.jpg)

Sunrise lights up the peaks of Torres del Paine. [Credit 221](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img221a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_221_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_221_r1.jpg)

Large and flightless, a lesser rhea roams the grassy Patagonian steppe. [Credit 222](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img222a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

CHILE/ARGENTINA

**Patagonia**

South America’s cold rugged wilds over two countries

O**NE OF THE WORLD’S** largest wilderness areas, South America’s Patagonia region is shared by Argentina and Chile, and it encompasses a range of rugged landscapes. Its northern end edges against the grassy Argentine Pampas, and it stretches south to the untamed, remote islands of Tierra del Fuego scattered within the Beagle Channel. Whales frolic in the Atlantic waves breaking against Patagonia’s eastern edge, and the Andes Mountains loom over glacial lakes on the Chile–Argentina boundary. The region’s roads and vistas have been traversed and explored by legendary figures like Ernesto “Che” Guevara, Bruce Chatwin, and Charles Darwin.

Península Valdés, called by some a cold Galápagos, juts into the Atlantic, its beaches a playground for seals, sea lions, penguins, and other marine life. Pods of whales and orcas migrate just off the peninsula’s shores at Punta Norte, and in the Golfo Nuevo. Welsh pioneers settled here over a century and a half ago and founded the town of Puerto Madryn. Their descendants still live on the peninsula; in small towns, some even continue to speak Welsh.

A serene, alpine center of chocolate-making and ski lodges awaits in San Carlos de Bariloche, Patagonia’s Teutonic heart, with architecture that’s a homage to Bavaria. Patagonia is raw, rugged, and at its most spectacular on the Chilean border along the Andes Mountains. At the heart of this landscape is the famous pinnacle Monte Fitz Roy. Locals call it Cerro Chaltén, a fusion of Spanish and Tehuelche words meaning “smoking mountain,” for the misty ring around its 11,073-foot (3,375 m) summit. Its sheer granite surface looms over Lago Viedma on the northern edge of Parque Nacional Los Glaciares, home to nearly 50 major glaciers.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *There are moments when I think with profound longing of those wonderful areas in our south. Perhaps one day, tired of circling the world, I’ll return to Argentina.*
~ CHE GUEVARA, *THE MOTORCYCLE DIARIES*

The most impressive glacier of all is Perito Moreno, a three-mile-wide (5 km) wall of aquamarine-colored ice that rises almost 200 feet (61 m) above Lago Argentino and emits a grinding roar as it shifts. This is one of Patagonia’s essential sights, and viewing platforms are crowded with visitors awed by bus-size chunks of ice shearing off the glacier and crashing into the lake’s water. Others strap on spiked crampons and climb over the glacier’s white, wrinkly, lunar surface.

South of the park, Torres del Paine, a dramatic triplet of mountain summits, straddles the Argentina–Chile border. Patagonia’s end-of-the-earth feeling is strongest in Ushuaia, the world’s southernmost city, pressed between the soaring tail of the Andes and the violent waves of the Beagle Channel. Its lonely port is a base for ships leaving for Antarctica.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_223_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_223_r1.jpg)

Huge chunks of ice shear off Perito Moreno Glacier and crash into Lago Argentino. [Credit 223](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img223a)

**• HOW TO STAY: The Llao Llao *([llaollao.com](http://llaollao.com))* is one of Bariloche’s best hotels. El Calafate, the gateway to Parque Nacional Los Glaciares, offers a variety of hotels. Hotel Península Valdés *([hotelpeninsula.com.ar](http://hotelpeninsula.com.ar))* in Puerto Madryn overlooks Golfo Nuevo. National Geographic Unique Lodge of the World member Tierra Patagonia Hotel & Spa is in Chile on the edge of Lago Sarmiento (see [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml)).**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Flights from Buenos Aires connect the gateway cities of Patagonia. National Geographic also offers trips to Patagonia *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_224_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_224_r1.jpg)

The fossilized track of the three-toed dinosaur *Limayichnus major* in Argentine Patagonia. [Credit 224](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img224a)

Patagonia’s Dinosaurs

I**n 1997, *National Geographic*’s Jamie Shreeve headed south to report a story on paleontology in South America. The dry, windswept plains of Patagonia hid an unexpected treasure: hundred-million-year-old dinosaur bones. Farmers and oil workers in southern Argentina regularly stumbled across them.**

**“The oldest, most primitive predators come from Argentina,” paleontologist Rodolfo Coria told Shreeve—such as the terrifying, 42-foot-long (13 m), aptly named carnivore *Giganotosaurus.* Plant-eating sauropods were even bigger: the 100-ton (90-metric ton) titanosaur *Argentinosaurus* was perhaps the heaviest land creature ever. It took seven years to excavate only a partial skeleton. “God forbid we should find a complete *Argentinosaurus,”* Coria joked. “I would be digging it out for the rest of my life.”**

**A sauropod nest containing thousands of eggs (some with developing embryonic skeletons inside) was discovered in 1997, just before the *National Geographic* article went to press.**

Hidden Journeys

VENEZUELA/THE GUIANAS/BRAZIL

**Guiana Highlands**

Flat-topped mountains disappearing into mist

**MYSTERIOUS TABLETOP MOUNTAINS**, shrouded with mist and lush with exotic greenery, give the Guiana Highlands the sense of a land time forgot, tucked into a remote corner of Venezuela. The highlands stretch into the Guianas and sections of Brazil and seem a real life Jurassic Park, in flora if not fauna. Mount Roraima, 8,986 feet (2,739 m) tall and the most impressive of the flat mountains (called *tepuis* in the native Pemon language), was the inspiration for Sir Arthur Conan Doyle’s *The Lost World*, a dinosaur-era adventure novel.

Eroded over millions of years, the sandstone tepuis, called mesas (tables) by the Spanish, are one of South America’s oldest mountain ranges, rising like giant anvil tops above the surrounding landscape of jungle and savanna. It was only in 1935 that sections of the highlands became known to the Western world, when American pilot Jimmy Angel discovered the spectacular waterfall now bearing his name, Angel Falls. This is the highest waterfall in the world, with a 3,212-foot (979 m) cascade plummeting off of Auyán Tepuí, also called Devil Mountain. The torrent is 17 times the height of Niagara Falls. It is the highlight of Parque Nacional Canaima, one of the largest national parks in the world, the tiny village of Canaima its gateway.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_225_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_225_r1.jpg)

A waterfall pours off of Mount Roraima. [Credit 225](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img225a)

One that does is Lost World Adventures *([lostworld.com](http://lostworld.com))*, with a tour into Parque Nacional Canaima.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_226_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_226_r1.jpg)

[Credit 226](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img226a)

CLASSIC SHOT

A **dusky dolphin swims in Golfo Nuevo in Argentine Patagonia. “We were down there trying to capture images of the dusky dolphin’s feeding strategy,” says photographer Luis Lamar, who was on assignment with underwater photojournalist Brian Skerry. “The gulf is often very rough; relentless, cold Patagonian winds blowing off the stark desert. We searched for a month,” he says. “When the feeding begins, chaos ensues, with hundreds of penguins, sea lions, shearwaters dive-bombing around you, sharks all around and orcas potentially lurking.” In his downtime, Lamar spent time in the water with the tribe, and he caught some more tranquil images, like this one.**

~ LUIS LAMAR

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_227_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_227_r1.jpg)

Small villages like Tilcara are folded into the Quebrada de Humahuaca. [Credit 227](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img227a)
