ARGENTINA

**Quebrada de Humahuaca**

Remains of ancient cultures in a colorful Andes valley

R**UTA** **9** **SHOOTS NORTH** of San Salvador de Jujuy in Argentina’s lonesome, northwestern reaches. This region evokes a beautiful last-frontier quality with its long, cactus-dotted valley carved over millennia by the Río Grande. It’s the coloring that stands out first, craggy rock walls banded in vibrant reds, yellows, oranges, pinks, and blues, as if a crazy artist went to work with a broad brush and a madcap vision. The Cerro de los Siete Colores (Hill of Seven Colors), just outside Purmamarca, and Serranía de Hornocal, near Humahuaca, with vivid, wave-shaped rock formations, are two striking examples of this phenomenon.

But arid natural beauty is not the foremost reason for this region’s fame. Remote as it seems, the valley was used as one of the world’s oldest trade routes, and people have lived and traveled and exchanged ideas in it for 10,000 years. Stone-walled terraces, built by early agricultural societies and thought to be 1,500 years old, are still in use today at Coctaca. Later generations of indigenous people also learned the art of ceramics, and cultivated crops like corn, potatoes, and quinoa.

In the 15th century, the Inca folded the region into their empire, and then the treasure-seeking Spaniards invaded it in the 16th century. There are still traces of both occupiers: Among the Inca vestiges are three mummified children found in a perfect state of preservation 22,110 feet (6,739 m) up Llullaillaco mountain, where they had been sacrificed in a religious ritual. They’re part of a poignant display at Salta’s excellent High Mountain Archaeological Museum. The Spanish left behind distinctive colonial architecture like neat adobe houses and squat Hispanic churches. The golden altar at Capilla de San Francisco de Paula in Uquía is particularly stunning.

Despite foreign invasions, the original cultures of this region were never obliterated. Just poke into the string of villages lining the Río Grande canyon—Purmamarca, Maimará, Tilcara, and Humahuaca—and you’ll discover traces of that indigenous heritage everywhere, in age-old festivals, spirituality, arts, healing practices, and language. Even the cuisine reflects long-ago traditions.

The best time to experience the legacy of the native people of Quebrada de Humahuaca is August 1, when Pachamama (Mother Earth) is celebrated for her year-round beneficence with gifts left on roadside altars. The best potatoes, corn, and meat are cooked in a stew that is buried in the ground, along with cigarettes, coca leaves, and alcohol. This is a gesture of feeding the earth, upon which the people rely for sustenance.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_229_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_229_r1.jpg)

Within the Quebrada de Humahuaca, the Quechuan village of Purmamarca overlooks the multihued Hill of Seven Colors. [Credit 229](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img229a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[welcomeargentina.com](http://welcomeargentina.com), [argentina.travel/​en](http://argentina.travel/en)***

**• HOW TO STAY: The valley’s villages, especially Tilcara and Humahuaca, offer accommodations with touches of *quebrada* style. Hotel El Manantial del Silencio *([hotelmananti­aldelsilencio.com](http://hotelmanantialdelsilencio.com))* in quieter Purmamarca is a whitewashed colonial-style resort and spa with splendid views of the Cerro de los Siete Colores. Casa Colorada *([casacolorada.com.ar](http://casacolorada.com.ar))* hides away high up on a roughly 10,000-foot (3,048 m) peak. And Hotel Huacalera *([hotelhuacalera.com](http://hotelhuacalera.com))* is a colonial-style ranch.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Aerolíneas Argentinas has daily direct flights from Buenos Aires to San Salvador de Jujuy. Or LAN Airlines has daily flights from Buenos Aires into nearby Salta, about 60 miles (96 km) south of the city of San Salvador de Jujuy.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_230_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_230_r1.jpg)

Jean Shor is dwarfed by a blooming cactus in the high desert above Humahuaca in Argentina. [Credit 230](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img230a)

Meandering Through Quebrada

H**usband-and-wife team Franc and Jean Shor encountered stunning desert scenery with 30-foot (9 m) cacti during their travels to Quebrada de Humahuaca for *National Geographic* in 1958.**

**“Yellow wild orchids hung from the trees, and butterflies swarmed across the road,” Franc wrote. “North of Jujuy, we came out into a great open valley with bare countryside on either hand, broken only by an occasional cactus or desert shrub.” The town of Humahuaca looked like the “setting for an Argentine version of the movie *High Noon,”* wrote Franc, describing its few streets of red dirt lined with identical adobe and stucco houses.**

**Trees were not abundant, and thus lumber was not a readily available building material. The original caption to this image noted that instead, “People of [this] area use the woody skeletons of cacti for furniture, floors, and window frames.” This particular cactus put out its exquisite yellow blossoms in October—spring in the Southern Hemisphere.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_231_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_231_r1.jpg)

A green sea turtle swims around coral in the clear waters of French Polynesia. [Credit 231](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img231a)

Paths encircle the base of huge, monolithic Uluru (above and [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c06_r1.xhtml_img233)), a landform that is sacred to Australia’s Aboriginal people. [Credit 232](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img232a)

AUSTRALIA

**Uluru**

Ancient symbol of Australia’s Aboriginals

T**HE ORANGE-RED SANDSTONE** monolith Uluru rises from sandy, desiccated scrub in sparsely populated Northern Territory. Protected within Ulur_u-Kata Tjut_a National Park (Australians call this region the Red Centre), the ancient behemoth is the symbol of Australia to the world. It has been revered for tens of thousands of years by the local Aboriginals, known as the Anangu, who believe the actions of ancient, giant godlike animals and warriors are recorded on every crevice, mark, and ripple of its weather-worn surface.

The rock is double-named Uluru and Ayers Rock. There is no literal translation for Uluru, the Anangu name. The English moniker was bestowed by British explorer William Christie Gosse to honor Sir Henry Ayers, then chief secretary of South Australia.

The national park also encompasses the less known geological formation Kata Tjuta (Mount Olga), a name that combines the Pitjantjatjara Aboriginal tribe word for “many heads” and the name of Queen Olga of Württemberg. The formation of Uluru and Kata Tjuta began more than 500 million years ago, when the area was covered by sea. The water eventually disappeared, and tectonic activity and erosion heaved and weathered the underlying rock into the monoliths we see today.

The 1873 European discovery of Uluru, which has a circumference of 5.8 miles (9.4 km), marked the beginning of a struggle between the British and the Aboriginal traditional landowners, the Pitjantjatjara and Yankunytjatjara peoples, over land rights and survival in this harsh, dry terrain.

Since Uluru opened to tourism in the 1930s, visitors have scaled the 1,142-foot-high (348 m) rock, which is nearly as tall as the Empire State Building. Though climbing is considered a desecration by Aboriginals and now frowned on, chains were installed in 1964 to reduce the number of deaths from falling.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_234_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_234_r1.jpg)

Bright-beaked zebra finches in the national park; the Anangu tell stories of great travels with these bird ancestors. [Credit 234](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img234a)

The Aboriginal religious system the Dreaming, possibly the oldest continuous cultural belief in the world, assigns meaning to virtually every part of the giant formation. The best and most socially responsible way to see Uluru from the Anangu perspective is to take a tour that explains the Tjukurpa, or creation period, and how the local people, animals, plants, and physical features of the giant rock interrelate. Rock paintings and carvings mark the lower surfaces and crevices of Uluru; they are among the oldest art created by humankind.

April’s Tjungu Festival highlights local Anangu culture, and at any time of year, the passing sun makes Uluru’s surface radiate with myriad colors. Those who rise early enough to see Uluru at sunrise will be best rewarded.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *Somehow you feel certain that this large, brooding, hypnotic presence has an importance to you at the species level.*
~ BILL BRYSON, *IN A SUNBURNED COUNTRY*

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[uluru-australia.com](http://uluru-australia.com), [northernterritory.com](http://northernterritory.com), [australia.com](http://australia.com), [parksaustralia.gov.au/​uluru](http://parksaustralia.gov.au/uluru)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Ayers Rock Resort is only 12 miles (20 km) from Uluru, with a variety of accommodations fitting all budgets, from the luxurious Sails in the Desert to Ayers Rock Campground *([ayersrockresort.com.au](http://ayersrockresort.com.au)).* The National Geographic Unique Lodges of the World member Longitude 131° is in the Red Centre (see [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml)).**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Alice Springs is the largest main city near Uluru, 286 miles (460 km) and nearly six hours away. Many people make a day trip in from Sydney and other points via Ayers Rock Airport, 15 miles (24 km) away from the site. National Geographic also offers a number of trips to Australia *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_235_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_235_r1.jpg)

Windili, a 12-year-old Aboriginal boy, accompanied *National Geographic*’s author on his fieldwork in central Australia. [Credit 235](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img235a)

Australia’s Aboriginals

I**n 1940, anthropologist Charles Mountford, his wife, and a camel wrangler traveled 70 hours by car and train to the Australian desert straddling the Northern Territory and South Australia. He was there to study a tribe of 300 Aboriginals who’d had little contact with the outside world. Their environs were a wasteland, “Yet the aborigines,” he wrote for *National Geographic*, “without a stitch of clothing and with only five implements to aid their bare hands” had made a living there for generations.**

**They ate grubs, lizards, ants, kangaroos, emus, grasses, and seeds. The grubs tasted like roast pork, Mountford noted; “Lizard tastes like chicken, and kangaroo like delicately flavored beef.”**

**The boy pictured accompanied the author on his journey through central Australia. “His chest is scarred from burns suffered while carrying fire sticks to keep him warm on the march,” Mountford wrote. Carrying blazing wood was common practice among the Aboriginals, and Mountford resorted to it himself.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_236_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_236_r1.jpg)

An enormous variety of life clings to the Great Barrier Reef. [Credit 236](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img236a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

AUSTRALIA

**Great Barrier Reef**

The world’s largest reef system along the Australian coast

T**HE GREAT BARRIER REEF**, Australia’s aquatic marvel, sprawls across an area about the size of Germany and stretches 1,430 miles (2,300 km) down the Queensland coast. Though known for its posh private island resorts and iconic scuba diving spots, the reef has been an integral part of the region’s indigenous human culture, economy, and spirituality for millennia.

The only living thing on our planet that can be seen from outer space, the Great Barrier Reef comprises about 900 islands and 3,000 individual reefs arrayed between the Torres Strait (which separates Australia from New Guinea) and just north of Fraser Island. In addition to intricate coral structures, the Great Barrier Reef Marine Park safeguards an amazing range of natural habitats, including mangrove estuaries, sea-grass beds, sponge gardens, and deep ocean areas.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_237_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_237_r1.jpg)

Brittle star, a sea creature with long, thin arms, camouflaged on coral [Credit 237](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img237a)

Even more astounding is the number and variety of life forms the reef supports: 600 coral species and more than 1,500 different types of tropical fish; 30 species of whales and dolphins, as well as sea turtles and the threatened dugong; about 3,000 different types of mollusks—including giant clams that can reach 120 years of age—and more than 100 jellyfish species. The skies above the reef teem with more than 200 bird species. And scattered across the islands are nearly two dozen species of reptiles, including the deadly saltwater crocodile, an excellent swimmer. This aggressive creature is lightning quick, fond of large meals, and should be assiduously avoided by people. An average male can grow 17 feet long (5 m) and 1,000 pounds (450 kg).

Aboriginals and Torres Strait Islanders used the reef and its myriad natural resources for thousands of years before the arrival of the first Europeans in the early 17th century. French explorer Louis-Antoine de Bougainville, who sailed past in 1768, was the first to describe the reef. Two years later, Captain James Cook and the *Endeavour* famously ran aground on a portion of the reef near Cape Tribulation. Captain William Bligh was another early European visitor.

But for the most part, the Europeans who settled Australia ignored the reef, other than sporadically mining it for guano and conducting hydrological surveys. It wasn’t until the 20th century that it came to be viewed as an incredible natural phenomenon and tourism attraction. The Great Barrier Reef Marine Park was created in 1975.

Despite vigorous protection, the reef is not immune to threats from beyond its waters, including pollution caused by mining and farming on the mainland, crown of thorns sea star infestations, and rising ocean temperatures caused by climate change.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_238_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_238_r1.jpg)

A school of diagonal-banded sweetlips, one of the 1,500 varieties of tropical fish supported by the reef [Credit 238](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img238a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[greatbarrierreef.org](http://greatbarrierreef.org), [gbrmpa.gov.au](http://gbrmpa.gov.au)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Among the reef’s better known holiday hubs are the Whitsunday Islands, Heron Island, Fraser Island, and Lizard Island. Day trips are available from cities on the Queensland mainland to scuba/snorkel spots like the Agincourt and Opal reefs. Coral Expeditions *([coralexpeditions.com](https://www.coralexpeditions.com/))* offers weeklong voyages along the reef in catamaran cruise ships. National Geographic has a Unique Lodges of the World property on Lizard Island (see [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml)).**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Cairns and Townsville, both served by daily flights from Sydney and Brisbane, are the main gateways to the Great Barrier Reef. It can also be accessed from Cooktown, Port Douglas, Gladstone, Mackay, and Thursday Island in the Torres Strait. National Geographic offers trips to the Great Barrier Reef *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_239_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_239_r1.jpg)

This mudskipper climbed a rock to catch some rays, which it can do thanks to a curiously adapted “lung.” [Credit 239](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img239a)

Strange Inhabitants of the Reef

I**n a 1940 *National Geographic* story, Australian zoologist T. C. Roughley introduced readers to the unusual creatures of the Great Barrier Reef. Some that particularly interested him included the suckerfish, with a fin that acted as a suction cup, allowing it to hitch a ride on sharks and turtles and glean their leftovers; giant clams that had reputedly trapped divers (“the power of a man is far inadequate to force the shells apart”); and the dugong, which early sailors improbably mistook for a mermaid.**

**This image shows a mudskipper, an odd creature with a primitive “lung” that allowed it to stay out of water for long periods of time. The fish could skip across mud and water with surprising rapidity. It vexed the author, staying just out of his reach as he slogged doggedly after it through knee-deep mud. “After about an hour, when I was perspiring, panting, and nearly exhausted, one of them left its run a little too late, and the game was over,” he triumphed at last.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_240_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_240_r1.jpg)

The Sydney Harbour Bridge (above) and the Sydney Opera House ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c06_r1.xhtml_img241)) are two of the most photogenic landmarks on the city’s harbor. [Credit 240](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img240a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_241_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_241_r1.jpg)

[Credit 241](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img241a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

AUSTRALIA

**Sydney Harbour**

The symbol of Australia’s cosmopolitan capital

G**ETTING OUT ON THE** water in Sydney, whether by kayak, sailboat, or ferry, is a requisite part of visiting the city. There, visitors will experience the heart and soul of what many proclaim to be the world’s most beautiful natural harbor. The water glitters. Seabirds swoop. Countless coves, waterfront parks, and hidden beaches beckon. And it’s all backed by the glamorous city skyline, a reminder that this is one of the most cosmopolitan cities on the planet. It’s easy to understand what Australian author Miles Franklin meant when she wrote, “A month would not be long enough to imbibe such beauty.”

Sydney Harbour comprises more than 149 miles (240 km) of spectacular meandering shoreline between the Heads (the entrance to the harbor) and the Parramatta River. For millennia, the Aboriginal Gadigal peoples fished and lived along its fertile headlands and deep channels. But in 1788, a fleet of ships laden with convicts and marines and their families arrived from England—and this was the start of Australia’s first city. It’s said even the convicts cheered when they beheld the beauty of their new home.

The city began at the Rocks, today a neighborhood with tiny cobblestone lanes and historic stone houses that buzz with new life as pubs, restaurants, and inns. Around the corner, Darlington Harbour recalls the city’s industrial past, its revitalized buildings along the wharves now bustling with restaurants and shops.

But Sydney Harbour’s iconic reputation wasn’t secured until two architectural marvels came along to complement it.

The first is Sydney Harbour Bridge. Lovingly dubbed the “coat hanger,” this steel-arched bridge has connected the northern and southern shores of Sydney Harbour since 1932\. Measuring 1,650 feet across (503 m), it originally reigned as the world’s longest single span steel arch bridge. Anyone daring enough to climb to the tip-top of the arch will look out on the city’s most spectacular views, all the way to the Blue Mountains and Pacific Ocean.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *One of the finest, most beautiful, vast, and safe bays the sun ever shone upon.*
~ JOSEPH CONRAD

The second icon is the even more renowned Sydney Opera House, rising in pearly splendor just northeast of the Circular Quay. A masterful feat of architecture, the building’s profile resembles billowing sails, an illusion achieved with multiple interlocking shells set on a pedestal.

The harbor is beautiful any time of day, but a nighttime ferry ride from Darling Harbour to Circular Quay is truly magical. Beneath a star-pricked, licorice-black sky, the ferry passes beneath the lit-up Harbour Bridge, with the luminescent Opera House beyond. It’s like no other scene in the world.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_242_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_242_r1.jpg)

An aerial view of Sydney Harbour; its distinctive bridge is fondly referred to by locals as the “coat hanger.” [Credit 242](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img242a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[sydney.com](http://sydney.com), [bridgeclimb.com](http://bridgeclimb.com), [sydneyoperahouse.com](http://sydneyoperahouse.com), [australia.com](http://australia.com)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Many hotels in the Central Business District have fabulous harbor views. Some rooms at the five-star Park Hyatt Sydney *([sydney.park.hyatt.com](http://sydney.park.hyatt.com))* have Opera House vistas from private balconies. Pullman Quay Grand Sydney Harbour *([pullmanquaygrandsydneyharbour.com](http://pullmanquaygrandsydneyharbour.com))* has stunning views; the Star hotel The Darling *([star.com.au](http://star.com.au))* in Pyrmont offers a perspective from Darling Harbour.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Sydney’s harbor bustles around the Circular Quay, the main hub for ferries *([transport.nsw.gov.au/​customers/​ferries/​sydney-ferries](http://transport.nsw.gov.au/customers/ferries/sydney-ferries)).* Morning, lunch, afternoon, and evening cruises arrive and depart from here. King Street Wharf is another main stop on Darling Harbour.**

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_243_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_243_r1.jpg)

A surfer “shoots the tube” on a breaker at a beach northeast of Sydney. [Credit 243](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img243a)

A Yankee Down Under

I**n 1979, *National Geographic* writer Ethel A. Starbird traveled to Sydney, Australia, to report on the city’s exuberant character and its eccentric characters. “Tempering the ancestral stiff upper lip with a warm and ready Aussie smile, three million Sydneysiders—among them Aborigines, Europeans, and recent Asian immigrants—mingle trend and tradition, propriety and impropriety, in epic Australian proportions,” she wrote.**

**She admired Sydney’s bays and beaches; its harbor and wharfs. She watched rugby, rode along with the boats that scooped tons of trash out of the harbor, visited the X-rated neighborhoods frequented by furloughed servicemen, and stopped in at the Sydney Opera House to hear the Israeli Philharmonic. She talked to artists, veterans, Aboriginals, political activists, boozers, heiresses, aristocrats, immigrants, and surfboarders. “Howyalike Sydney?” one Sydneysider asked Starbird. She wrote: “ ‘She’s the big curl, Fred,’ I answered honestly. ‘She’s beaut.’ ”**

Hidden Journeys

AUSTRALIA

**Hobart, Tasmania**

Sydney’s quieter, quainter sister

**HOBART, TASMANIA’S CAPITAL**, was established in 1804 on the Derwent River, and grew into one of the world’s whaling capitals by the 1830s. Like Sydney, it possesses a stunning harbor. But unlike Sydney, this diminutive town in the shadow of kunanyi/Mount Wellington possesses a rustic allure that feels more provincial than urban. Change does not happen quickly here. And that’s a huge part of its charm.

Colonial-era buildings at Sullivans Cove and Franklin Wharf give the waterfront an Old World feel. Nearby at Salamanca Place, sandstone warehouses have been converted into boutiques and sunny cafés. A lively farmers market is held on the plaza out front on Saturday mornings.

Dozens of Hobart’s buildings—exquisite examples of unembellished Georgian architecture (mostly built by convicts)—have been placed on the Heritage Register. There are a preponderance of these on Macquarie and Davey Streets.

But what makes Hobart truly special is its location beneath kunanyi/Mount Wellington; even Charles Darwin climbed this 4,170-foot (1,271 m) peak. Visitors can drive or hike to the top, where an observatory shelter offers a bird’s-eye view over Hobart, snuggled prettily down on the banks of the Derwent.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_244_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_244_r1.jpg)

Kunanyi/Mount Wellington offers broad vistas of Hobart and beyond. [Credit 244](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img244a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[discovertasmania.com.au](http://discovertasmania.com.au), [discoverhobart.com](http://discoverhobart.com), [australia.com](http://australia.com)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Hobart’s Brunswick Hotel *([brunswickhotelhobart.com.au](http://brunswickhotelhobart.com.au))* was built by convicts 20 years after Hobart was settled. Hadley’s Orient Hotel *([hadleyshotel.com.au](http://hadleyshotel.com.au))* recalls the grandeur of the Victorian era.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Hobart has an airport that handles international and domestic flights. It’s a two-hour flight from Sydney. You can also take the *Spirit of Tasmania* overnight ferry *([spiritoftasmania.com.au](http://spiritoftasmania.com.au))* from Melbourne; or rent a car or take the bus *([tassielink.com.au](http://tassielink.com.au))* to reach Hobart from Devonport, in Tasmania’s north.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_245_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_245_r1.jpg)

[Credit 245](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img245a)

CLASSIC SHOT

W**hile the building structure and its iconic billowing sails are the subjects of most photographs of Sydney’s Opera House, photographer Mattias Klum—who was on site in Sydney to do lectures for National Geographic—was taken with this little boy. “The architecture is obviously the big draw to this place,” he says, “but the little boy is more captivated by the stairs.” This observation led to an unexpected shot—and intimate angle—of the famous structure on Sydney Harbour.**

~ MATTIAS KLUM

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_246_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_246_r1.jpg)

The island Motukokako is better known as Hole in the Rock. [Credit 246](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img246a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L01_r1.jpg)

NEW ZEALAND

**Bay of Islands**

Rocky islets, turquoise sea, and golden beaches

I**N THE SOUTHERN HEMISPHERE**, vacationers head north for sunny beaches. And in New Zealand that means the sheltered Bay of Islands, with lighthouse-topped promontories, forested islets, and wave-lapped golden-sand beaches. Sheltered in an eastern nook of New Zealand’s North Island, these 114 picturesque islands enjoy a mild subtropical climate and support a bevy of marine and bird life that includes populations of dolphins, kiwis, little penguins, and migrating whales.

Tribal Maori settled here as early as the 10th century, though Captain James Cook is credited with “discovering” the region and naming it in 1769\. The tiny village of Russell, whose bulk juts out on the westernmost end of the Te Rawhiti Inlet, was New Zealand’s first European seaport. Once a catch-all settlement of whalers, missionaries, and escaped convicts from Australia, it still boasts some of the country’s oldest buildings. The cream-colored, clapboard Te Whare Karakia o Kororareka (Christ Church), New Zealand’s oldest surviving church, was built in the mid-1830s with funding from visitors like Charles Darwin. He passed through the area aboard the H.M.S. *Beagle* in 1835 collecting specimens of fish, shells, and insects.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_247_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_247_r1.jpg)

Christ Church at Russell on North Island, the oldest church in New Zealand [Credit 247](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img247a)

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_248_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_248_r1.jpg)

Urupukapuka Island is protected as a recreational reserve and reachable only by boat. [Credit 248](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img248a)

Today, visitors come to the Bay of Islands to experience the laid-back island life. They cruise from Cape Brett Peninsula to the uninhabited island Motukokako (Hole in the Rock), which has a famous wave-worn gap in one of its sheer cliffs, large enough for catamarans to pass through. Many tourists base themselves for days in the central boutique town of Paihia on North Island, but nature seekers camp along the shores of remote Urupukapuka Island, where the only way in and out is by boat. Hikers climb the easterly island’s gently sloping grassy hilltops for stunning views of the island-specked bay.

Kayakers round Tapeka Point north of Russell to spot penguins, and charter boats head to the waters beyond in search of marlin. In Kerikeri in Northland, vacationers tip back crisp cupfuls of Pinot Gris and Sauvignon Blanc with regional cuisine at dozens of wine-forward restaurants. It’s said New Zealand’s budding international wine industry originated here, with the planting of the country’s first vineyards.

At the Waitangi Treaty Grounds, visitors can learn about the signing of a document in 1840 between the British and Maori tribes that paved the way for New Zealand’s nationhood. To commemorate the event, each February 6, the day the Treaty of Waitangi was signed, a 115-foot (35 m) long war *waka* (canoe) is launched from nearby Hobsons Beach.

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[visitboi.co.nz](http://visitboi.co.nz)***

**• HOW TO STAY: Plan to spend at least three days exploring the Bay of Islands. Though the weather is pleasant year-round, the area is most popular in the warmest months between September and April. Just outside Paihia, Copthorne Hotel and Resort Bay of Islands *([millenniumhotels.com/​en/​bay-of-islands](http://millenniumhotels.com/en/bay-of-islands))* occupies 60 garden waterfront acres within the Waitangi National Trust Reserve.**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: It’s a three-hour drive from Auckland to Bay of Islands. Daily flights land in Kerikeri. Regional transit includes water taxis and boating charters from most major towns, passenger ferries between Russell and Paihia, and vehicle ferries between Russell and Opua. Local tour companies offer bespoke explorations. National Geographic also offers trips to New Zealand *([natgeoexpeditions.com](http://natgeoexpeditions.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_249_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_249_r1.jpg)

A gale washed *Teddy* ashore at Bay of Islands (bottom); the author’s son Tony secured to deck (top left); a waterspout over calm seas (top right). [Credit 249](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img249a)

Adventures in the South Seas

E**rling Tambs and his wife embarked on a 17,000-mile (27,360 km) cruise halfway around the world in 1928, and wrote of it for *National Geographic.* They sailed on the cutter *Teddy* without instruments or charts, and with only an old card compass for navigation. Passing ships gave them bearings between ports of call in South America, Tahiti, and Australia.**

**Their son (top left) was born in the Canary Islands and went to sea at six weeks of age. A rope secures him to deck lest he become “an unwilling meal for monsters of the sea.” Sharks swam about the ship at intervals, ending impromptu ocean dips and ushering in bucket baths on deck.**

**The Tambses had other close calls: At the Bay of Islands, a gale washed *Teddy* ashore; a waterspout approached over calm seas in the South Pacific; and leaving Tahiti, the family had to outrun a storm that swamped *Teddy* with five tons (4.5 metric tons) of water. “One half-hour longer at sea would have finished us,” Tambs wrote.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_250_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_250_r1.jpg)

[Credit 250](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img250a)

CLASSIC SHOT

Y**oung Maori paddlers perform the *tutu ngarahu haka*, or war dance, before heading onto the bay in their war canoes. This ritual commemorates the signing of the British–Maori treaty in 1840. Photographer Amy Toensing was struck by the Maori’s deep connection to land, family, and ancestry. She points out the faint tattoo on the back of the second man from the left: “Many members of this tribe had intricate tattoos that told stories of their ancestors,” she says. A fun detail that’s always intrigued her is the missing green flip-flop on the boy second from left—it’s nowhere to be seen in the frame.**

~ AMY TOENSING

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_251_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_251_r1.jpg)

The symbols of French Polynesia: tall palms, thatched roofs, and inviting waters (above), and the national flower, the Tahitian gardenia ([here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_c06_r1.xhtml_img252)) [Credit 251](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img251a)

SOUTH PACIFIC

**French Polynesia**

Tropical islands with a Parisian touch

I**F YOU HAVEN’T SET FOOT** in French Polynesia, it’s hard to imagine why personalities as diverse as mutineer Fletcher Christian, actor Marlon Brando, painter Paul Gauguin, and singer Jacques Brel fell head-over-heels for these far-away tropical isles. But all it takes is a single stroll along a black-sand strand, a dive into a translucent lagoon, or a single session of sensuous local dance to see why islands like Tahiti and Bora-Bora are considered paradise.

Spread across an area as large as Western Europe, French Polynesia encompasses five large archipelagos—the Society Islands, Tuamotus, Marquesas, Gambier group, and Austral Islands. They range from volcanic “high islands” with mountainous backbones swathed in rain forest to astoundingly flat coral atolls that wrap around some of the world’s largest lagoons. Only 67 of the territory’s 118 islands are inhabited.

Tahiti is one of the largest and by far the most populated (pop. 184,000) of the Society Islands. It went from obscurity to instant world renown in 1789 when Fletcher Christian and his mates decided to hijack the English ship H.M.S. *Bounty* and remain in paradise with their Tahitian girlfriends rather than return to Europe with Captain William Bligh. Gauguin enhanced the island’s reputation with his alluring portraits of local beauties. Papeete, the territorial capital, offers a cosmopolitan mix of Parisian-style sidewalk cafés and Polynesian-style tattoo parlors.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg)![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_L02_r1.jpg) *[Tahiti is] the finest island in the world…where the allurements of dissipation are beyond anything that can be conceived.*
~ CAPTAIN WILLIAM BLIGH

The string of Society Islands west of Tahiti are equally intriguing, a necklace of tropical pearls that includes Moorea, Huahine, Raiatea, Tahaa, and Bora-Bora. Rather than homogeneous landfalls, the islands have distinct personalities. Raiatea is renowned for its ancient Polynesian ruins and religious sites, Tahaa for its vanilla plantations, Huahine for its “savage” wilderness terrain, Bora-Bora for its jagged volcanic peak and shark diving, and Moorea for its picture-postcard beauty.

An hour’s flight north from Tahiti, the Tuamotu Archipelago offers an opposite landscape of palm-shaded coral atolls like Rangiroa, Manihi, and Tikehau where scuba diving, sea kayaking, and shopping for locally harvested black pearls are among the ways to while away a day. Farther north, the Marquesas were the jumping-off point for Polynesian migration to Hawaii.

Among the least visited of any South Pacific landfalls, the supersecluded Austral and Gambier Islands are slices of old Polynesia that remain refreshingly unfettered by modern life and in some cases are completely off the grid.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_253_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_253_r1.jpg)

Kayaking over crystalline waters past resort bungalows on Moorea Island [Credit 253](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img253a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[tahiti-tourisme.com](http://tahiti-tourisme.com)***

**• HOW TO STAY: There are few accommodations in the Marquesas, Austral, and Gambier Islands. The M.S. *Paul Gauguin* offers cruise itineraries through the Society, Tuamotu, and Marquesas Islands *([pgcruises.com](http://pgcruises.com))*. National Geographic Unique Lodges of the World member The Brando is on the Society Islands atoll of Tetiaroa (see [here](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_bm1_r1.xhtml)).**

**• HOW TO GET THERE: All international flights touch down in Papeete, where travelers can transfer to interisland ferries or flights on Air Tahiti *([airtahiti.com](http://airtahiti.com)).* The mixed passenger/freighter vessel *Aranui 5* offers a voyage to 10 islands in the three main groups *([aranui.com](http://aranui.com)).***

**NAT GEO FLASHBACK**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_254_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_254_r1.jpg)

Split coconuts dried to produce copra (left); coconut fronds form a fishing dredge (top right); Marlon Brando during shooting of *Mutiny on the Bounty* on Tahiti in 1962 (bottom right). [Credit 254](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img254a)

The World Discovers Tahiti

*N****ational Geographic*** **writer and photographer Luis Marden visited Tahiti in 1962 to see how the South Pacific island’s first airstrip had changed it. Its sense of exotic seclusion had ended with the arrival of the first passenger aircraft. He wrote ruefully, “Tahiti is no longer remote, a misty isle of legend, a place untouched by the niggling realities of the outside world. Now it is as near as your airport.”**

**But he found some things unchanged. Papeete was still Tahiti’s only town, the Tahitian language was still barely written, and natural bounty like fish and coconuts were still staples of the islanders’ existence. They had 20 words for the coconut alone, and used every part of it in day-to-day life. Above left, Tahitians dry split coconuts in the sun to create the island’s chief export, copra. After sunning, the white meat was cut out of the nut and further dried over a coconut-husk fire. Top right, coconut fronds form a seine to dredge small fish from the water.**

Hidden Journeys

FRENCH POLYNESIA

**Hiva Oa**

An island of forgotten Polynesian ruins

**NOWADAYS, THE REMOTE** Marquesas Islands feel like the proverbial last place on earth. But a thousand years ago, this archipelago was the major hub of Polynesian society.

In addition to dispatching the first long-distance canoes and human inhabitants to Hawaii, the volcanic isles were the launching point for the settlement of the rest of French Polynesia and ultimately places as far afield as Easter Island.

Hiva Oa safeguards much of this ancient heritage, including some of the most important archaeological sites in the South Pacific. Foremost among these is a massive ceremonial complex in the Taaoa Valley. Scattered across the valley floor, some still obscured by jungle, are more than 1,000 *paepaes*—dark volcanic stone platforms once used in religious rites.

Near the island’s eastern extreme looms the largest stone tiki in French Polynesia, part of a Me’ae Iipona temple complex restored in the 1990s and still a place where locals commune with ancient gods. In the nearby Tahauku Valley are the Tehueto petroglyphs, age-old rock art that depicts islanders.

French impressionist Paul Gauguin and legendary Belgian singer Jacques Brel spent their last days in Atuona, the island’s largest town. Both are buried in Calvary Cemetery.

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_255_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_255_r1.jpg)

The jagged eastern slope of Hiva Oa in the Marquesas archipelago [Credit 255](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img255a)

TRAVEL TIPS

**• RESOURCES: *[tahiti-tourisme.com/​islands/​marquesas/​marquesas.asp](http://tahiti-tourisme.com/islands/marquesas/marquesas.asp), [www.marquises.pf/​2index.htm](http://www.marquises.pf/2index.htm)*, *[marquises-hivaoa.org.pf](http://marquises-hivaoa.org.pf)* (French only)**

**• HOW TO STAY: The island’s only overnight digs are in Atuona. There’s but a single hotel: the Hiva Oa Hanakee Pearl Lodge *([pearlodge.com/​en/​home-hanakee](http://pearlodge.com/en/home-hanakee)).***

**• HOW TO GET THERE: Air Tahiti *([airtahiti.com](http://airtahiti.com))* offers flights from Papeete (via Nuku Hiva). It’s also possible to reach Hiva Oa by sea aboard the M.S. *Paul Gauguin* cruise ship *([pgcruises.com](http://pgcruises.com))*, the *Aranui 5* passenger/freighter vessel *([aranui.com](http://aranui.com))*, or an annual Zegrahm Expeditions cruise *([zegrahm.com](http://zegrahm.com))* that calls on Hiva Oa.**

![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_256_r1.jpg) ![](OEBPS/images/Coch_9781426218545_epub3_256_r1.jpg)

[Credit 256](#Coch_9781426218545_epub3_cre_r1.xhtml_img256a)

CLASSIC SHOT

P**hotographer Aaron Huey came to the French Polynesian atoll of Fakarava for a *National Geographic Traveler* story about farming cultured black pearls, which are formed by the giant black-lipped oyster. The remote isle is one of the world’s best places to find them. “I can say in all honesty that I have never again seen a place that so resembles paradise,” Huey says. Of this image, he remembers, “I had to find a very tall ladder so that you could see the dock and be high enough to see through the surface of the water to the coral clubs below.” And it wasn’t just oysters down there: “The waters in this atoll were filled with blacktip reef sharks.”**

~ AARON HUEY

AUSTRALIA AND THE PACIFIC

• **Capella Lodge**, Australia

• **Lizard Island**, Australia

Set upon 2,500 pristine acres of tropical forest and coastline, Lizard Island is the only accommodation in Lizard Island National Park. Remote reaches of the Great Barrier Reef are just offshore, accessible by glass-bottom boat or kayak, snorkel or scuba.

• **Longitude 131°**, Australia

Longitude 131° rests in the curve of a sand dune within the Ulur–u-Kat–a Tjuta National Park—each stunning tent angled for a view of Uluru. Discover the sacred geography of the Australian outback on walks and hikes with local guides, and have dinner atop a dune.

• **Southern Ocean Lodge**, Australia

• **The Brando**, French Polynesia

The idyllic Polynesian atoll of Tetiaroa—home to The Brando—was once the retreat of Tahitian kings and later a private refuge of actor Marlon Brando. Spend your days floating above coral gardens, and strolling beaches where sea turtles nest.

NORTH AMERICA

SOUTH AMERICA

• **Belcampo Lodge**, Belize

Tucked into a secluded bay on Santa Cruz Island, Finch Bay is the base from which to explore the Galápagos. Set out with a guide to meet wild giant tortoises, mingle with sea lions on the beach, or explore the islands on the lodge’s private yacht.

• **Inkaterra Hacienda Concepción**, Peru

• **Inkaterra Hacienda Urubamba**, Peru

• **Inkaterra La Casona**, Peru

• **Inkaterra Machu Picchu Pueblo Hotel**, Peru

Inkaterra Machu Picchu Pueblo Hotel is a haven of Andean charm tucked into the cloud forest just beneath the Inca citadel of Machu Picchu. Settle into your whitewashed casita or discover rare birds and orchids species along forest trails on the property.

• **Mashpi Lodge**, Eucador

• **Reserva do Ibitipoca**, Brazil

• **Tierra Atacama Hotel & Spa**, Chile

Tierra Atacama is a sublime gateway to one of the most surreal places on the planet, where volcanoes and geysers meet vast salt flats and the local culture is vibrant and ancient. Get to know it with lodge naturalists on excursions by foot, mountain bike, or 4x4.

• **Tierra Patagonia Hotel & Spa**, Chile

Tierra Patagonia blends into the banks of Lake Sarmiento, with panoramic views of Torres del Paine National Park. Join a gaucho for a day exploring on horseback, or head out on a glacier walk. Return to a soothing treatment and hydrotherapy at the Uma Spa.

National Geographic Books would like to thank Editorial Director Lisa Thomas for brainstorming the concept for this project, and editor Olivia Garnett for turning it into a book.

Art director Elisa Gibson designed and laid out every page, and photo editor Moira Haney researched every image. Thanks to cartographer Mike McNey for producing the world map, and to National Geographic editor emeritus Barbara Noe Kennedy for consulting on the book’s contents. Authors Larry Bleiberg, Karen Carmichael, Kate Evans, Barbara Noe Kennedy, Michael Luongo, Meghan Miner Murray, and Joe Yogerst drafted the manuscript; Sophie Massie researched the copy; and Mary Norris wrote the captions. Thank you to the National Geographic photographers who weighed in from all over the world to answer questions about the images featured in this book. Thanks also to National Geographic Creative Services for helping secure the archival National Geographic images that appear in these pages.

Production editor Mike O’Connor managed the final stages of editing; many thanks to him, copy editor Jane Sunderland, proofreader Nicole Wayland, and indexer Dianne Hosmer.

National Geographic would also like to thank Rachel Faulise, Jennifer Thornton, Marshall Kiker, Melissa Farris, Susan Blair, Heidi Vincent, Ford Cochran, Susan O’Keefe, Sarah Erdman, Patrick Bagely, and Pinar Taskin for their contributions.
